  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts: HGV driver income £300/week net (£1,300/month equivalent); Sea Cadets volunteer £20/week net (£86.67/month equivalent); Kim employment income £1,100/month net; Client state pension £230/week net (£1,000/month equivalent); Kim state pension £230/week net from age 67 (£1,000/month equivalent, projected); Total monthly income net £4,386.67 (current); Household expenditure £1,700/month; Monthly surplus £1,786.67; Total assets £6,000; Total liabilities £0; Net worth £6,000.
    File:     Fact Find [P1] and [P10] state: Total net monthly income £1,430.00 (shown as sum of Basic Income £1,200.00 and State Pension £230.00); Total monthly expenditure £1,718.00; Monthly disposable income (£288.00) deficit. [P10] lists income items separately: 'Basic Income, Employed, CDH Recruitment' monthly £1,200.00 net; 'State Pension' monthly £230.00 net. [P17] suitability report narrative confirms £300/week HGV driver income and £20/week Sea Cadets income. [P1] expenditure table shows itemised categories totalling £1,718.00 monthly. [P13] confirms cash holdings £6,000.00. No liabilities recorded.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report total monthly income £4,386.67 vs Fact Find total £1,430.00 — difference of £2,956.67 (206% variance)
      - Report HGV driver income £1,300/month (derived from £300/week × 52/12) vs Fact Find 'Basic Income' £1,200.00 monthly — difference £100/month (8.3% variance)
      - Report Sea Cadets income £86.67/month vs Fact Find no separate line-item; not visible in income breakdown
      - Report Kim employment income £1,100/month net stated but Fact Find shows only 'Basic Income' £1,200.00 and 'State Pension' £230.00, no separate Kim income entry
      - Report state pension £1,000/month (derived from £230/week × 52/12) vs Fact Find state pension £230.00 monthly — £230 appears to be stated as monthly, not weekly equivalent
      - Report total household expenditure £1,700/month vs Fact Find calculated total £1,718.00 — difference £18 (1% variance, within tolerance)
      - Report monthly surplus £1,786.67 vs Fact Find monthly disposable income (£288.00) deficit — opposite signs, £2,074.67 variance
    The canonical model extracts five separate income streams: client HGV £300/week net, client Sea Cadets £20/week net, Kim employment £1,100/month net, client state pension £230/week net, Kim state pension £230/week net (projected). The report sums these to £4,386.67/month current income. However, the Fact Find [P1] and [P10] record only two income items in the summary: 'Basic Income' of £1,200/month and 'State Pension' of £230/month, totalling £1,430/month. The Fact Find's 'Basic Income' at £1,200/month does not match either the HGV driver income alone (£1,300/month at £300/week × 52/12) or any single component from the report. The £1,200/month figure from [P10] appears to represent employment income only (possibly CDH Recruitment employer name suggests agency work), but it is £100/month short of the £1,300 the report derives from £300/week. Guard: £300/week should annualise to £1,300/month, not £1,200/month. The report's conversion £300/week = £1,300/month is arithmetically correct (£300 × 52 weeks ÷ 12 months = £1,300). The Fact Find's entry of £1,200/month for the same income stream contradicts this by £100/month. This is a discrepancy of material significance: it affects the calculated surplus and the affordability of retirement. The £20/week Sea Cadets income is stated in the report and quoted in [P17] but does not appear as a separate line in the Fact Find income table [P1]; the Fact Find may consolidate it into 'Basic Income' or omit it, but [P1] shows no second employment category. Kim's employment income (£1,100/month net per [P17]) is not itemised in the Fact Find summary [P1], which lists only 'Basic Income' and 'State Pension'. The Fact Find records two clients but does not clearly split income by owner in the summary total of £1,430. The report attributes £1,100 to Kim (client C2) and £1,386.67 to the primary client (C1: £1,300 HGV + £86.67 Sea Cadets), but the Fact Find summary does not show this split. The state pension entry in [P10] is £230.00 monthly, not weekly; however, [P17] explicitly states '£230 per week' for the client and 'around £230 per week' for Kim. The report correctly interprets this as weekly (£1,000/month equivalent for each when both are drawing), but the Fact Find may record it as monthly. Expenditure: The report states £1,700/month; the Fact Find [P1] itemises categories totalling £1,718.00. Difference £18 (1%), within rounding tolerance. The report's figure of £1,700 is slightly rounded or simplified; the detailed Fact Find supports a marginally higher figure. The surplus calculation is inverted between the two: the report shows surplus (income > expenditure by £1,786.67), while the Fact Find shows deficit (income £1,430 < expenditure £1,718 = deficit of £288). This inversion flows from the income discrepancy. The core issue is that the Fact Find total monthly income of £1,430 does not reconcile with the report's derived total of £4,386.67. The report includes Kim's income and the full annualised state pension for both; the Fact Find summary in [P1] may record only the primary household or may omit one income stream. Assets and liabilities reconcile: both report and Fact Find show £6,000 cash, £0 liabilities.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Client (JS) is in receipt of full State Pension of £230 per week (stated page 3 as 'You are in receipt of your full State Pension of £230 per week'). Client states annual state pension income of £11,973 per annum (page 5). Spouse (Kim) will receive full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age of 67 (page 3). Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67 (page 5). Report confirms Kim is currently 65 and intends to retire at age 66.
    File:     Intelliflo planning Baseline scenario (P1) states STATE PENSION AGE as 66 for one party and shows 'Retire' age 66 in 2026 and 'Retire' age 71 in 2027 for two persons. Intelliflo planning (P1) shows date of birth 07/05/1960 making person 65 years old at time of planning. Intelliflo documents (P1, P4) show state pension age of 66. The suitability report (P5, P9) confirms JS is aged 69, Kim aged 65, JS receives £230 per week state pension, Kim will receive £230 per week at State Pension age. Report (P5) states 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67' and 'You receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum' for JS. Annual value of £230 weekly = £11,960 per annum (£230 x 52), which reconciles to stated £11,973 within rounding.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Kim's State Pension age as 67 (page 3: 'when she reaches State Pension age' and page 5: 'from age 67'). Intelliflo planning Baseline scenario (P1) indicates state pension age of 66 for the same party. This is a 1-year discrepancy in State Pension age.
      - Report states JS will retire 'within the next two years' (age 69, so by age 71). Intelliflo Baseline scenario (P1) shows retirement at age 66 (in 2026) for one party. Birth date 07/05/1960 makes person age 66 in 2026, consistent with retirement intention, but report narrative says 'within the next two years' from current age 69, which would be age 71.
    The requirement checks consistency of state pension entitlement facts and figures across the report and supporting evidence. Two observations: First, weekly to annual reconciliation: £230 per week × 52 = £11,960 per annum. The report states £11,973 per annum. This is a £13 difference (0.1%), well within normal rounding tolerance and does not represent a material inconsistency. Second, State Pension age discrepancy: The report explicitly states Kim 'will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age' and later on page 5 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67.' The Intelliflo planning document (P1) categorised as [F] shows 'STATE PENSION AGE 66' in the baseline scenario. The guard notes that 'State pension age and intended retirement age are routinely conflated in reports.' Intelliflo shows retirement event at age 66 and separately shows state pension age as 66. The report's assertion of Kim's state pension age as 67 differs from the evidence file figure of 66 by one year. This is material to the retirement cashflow modelling and the timing of income availability. The discrepancy cannot be explained by the retirement age / state pension age conflation guard, as the evidence file explicitly labels one field 'STATE PENSION AGE' with value 66. However, the figure-check shows 67 is corroborated by [P5] itself and 66 is the nearest alternative in evidence at 1.5% away. Given that both numbers appear in the evidence pack (66 in planning file P1, 67 in suitability report P5 which is category I), and the guard permits conflation of ages as routine, the presence of the 1-year gap is a legitimate finding. The annual state pension income figure of £11,973 is consistently stated for both clients and reconciles to weekly figures within tolerance. The key discrepancy is the state pension age for Kim: report says 67, evidence file says 66.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The extraction identified five internal inconsistencies: (1) Risk rating questionnaire result of 4 versus agreed/confirmed position of 5, with recommendations aligned to rating 5. (2) Existing Aviva fund 'Aviva pension my future focus consolidation' versus recommended new Aviva funds 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' (50%) and 'CT Universal MAP Balanced' (50%). (3) Standard Life existing charge stated as 0.52% on page 5 versus 0.18% recurring charge on page 11, with the EA5 charge row computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002.00). (4) Scottish Widows fund value £211.32 with estimated net receipt of £170 after encashment, which is arithmetically plausible but implies a tax treatment. (5) Aviva plan simultaneously 0.24% cheaper (like-for-like) and 4.24% more expensive (including adviser fee) than current plans.
    File:     No supporting document was searched; this requirement compares the report against itself. The extraction report itself documents the inconsistencies identified in internalInconsistencies[]. The derived figures confirm arithmetic mismatches: EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.52% implies fund value of £35,805.77, which matches neither arrangement; the same row labelled EA5 shows impact of £268.94 at 0.26%, which implies Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46), confirming the row calculation uses the wrong plan. Aviva Platform Personal Pension platform charge (£231.39 at 0.21%) implies £110,185.71, investment management charge (£253.43 at 0.22%) implies £115,195.45, neither of which equals any single arrangement or the combined total of £116,998.47.
    Discrepancies:
      - Standard Life (EA5) existing charge: 0.52% on page 5 versus 0.18% on page 11 (Pension Switch Charges table)
      - EA5 charge row on page 11: computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002.00), making the comparison unreliable
      - Risk rating: questionnaire result 4 versus agreed/confirmed position 5
      - Existing Aviva fund ('Aviva pension my future focus consolidation') versus recommended new Aviva funds ('HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' and 'CT Universal MAP Balanced')
      - Aviva recommendation cost direction: 0.24% cheaper (like-for-like) versus 4.24% more expensive (including adviser fee)
      - Aviva Platform Personal Pension platform charge implies fund value of £110,185.71, which matches no arrangement
      - Aviva Platform Personal Pension investment management charge implies fund value of £115,195.45, which matches no arrangement
      - Aviva Platform Personal Pension total ongoing charge (£484.81 at 0.44%) implies fund value of £110,184.09, which matches no arrangement
      - Scottish Widows: fund value £211.32 versus estimated net encashment receipt £170 (tax treatment implied but not explicit)
    This requirement assesses internal consistency of the report against itself. Five material inconsistencies are documented in the extraction: (1) Risk rating: the questionnaire indicated 4, the client confirmed 5, and recommendations target rating 5. This is not an error of arithmetic but a documented override; the final position (rating 5) is confirmed on page 4 ('You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'). The investment strategy correctly reflects rating 5. Internal consistency on the final agreed position is maintained, though the questionnaire-vs-agreed discrepancy is explicit. (2) Existing Aviva fund: the report switches from 'Aviva pension my future focus consolidation' (lifestyle/consolidation strategy) to 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' and 'CT Universal MAP Balanced' (third-party multi-asset funds on the Aviva platform). This is a material change in fund selection and strategy within the same provider, documented across pages 5 and 12. It is not arithmetically inconsistent but strategically inconsistent. (3) Standard Life charge: page 5 states 0.52% fund charge; page 11 (Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment) lists 0.18% as the current annual recurring charge. These cannot both be the full recurring charge of the same plan. The derived figures confirm that the EA5 row on page 11 is computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46 at 0.26% = £268.94) rather than Standard Life's own (£3,002.00), indicating the row calculation applied charges to the wrong plan. This is an arithmetic error that affects the cost comparison. (4) Scottish Widows: fund value is £211.32; estimated net receipt is £170. The arithmetic is plausible (25% tax-free = £52.83, remainder subject to income tax at an effective rate around 17% yields approximately £126 net, total ~£179), but the tax treatment is implied rather than explicit. No direct contradiction, but incomplete disclosure. (5) Cost direction: the recommendation is simultaneously 0.24% cheaper (like-for-like) and 4.24% more expensive (including adviser fee). Both statements are internally consistent with their respective premises (one excludes adviser fees, one includes them), but the tension is material to suitability: without adviser fees the switch saves costs; with them, it does not. This is correctly captured in the two-scenario analysis, not an error, but highlights a key asymmetry in the recommendation. Arithmetic tolerance: Standard Life 0.52% vs 0.18% exceeds rounding tolerance (0.01% max). EA5 computation on wrong plan's value is a material arithmetic error. Aviva charges do not sum to match any single arrangement value, suggesting component-level charges may not map to a coherent fund total.

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states client income as: £300 per week net (HGV driver, page 3); £20 per week net (Sea Cadets volunteer, page 3); £1,100 per month net (spouse employment, page 3); £230 per week net (State Pension, page 3); £230 per week net projected (spouse State Pension from age, page 3). Monthly equivalents calculated as: £1,300 (HGV £300 × 52 ÷ 12); £86.67 (Sea Cadets £20 × 52 ÷ 12); £1,100 (employment, stated); £1,000 (State Pension £230 × 52 ÷ 12); £1,000 (spouse State Pension projected). Total monthly income net stated as £4,386.67. Expenditure stated as £1,700 per month net (page 3), both actual and intended. The extraction note for total monthly income confirms sum as £3,486.67 current plus £1,000 projected spouse pension = total stated as £4,386.67.
    File:     Fact Find (Test 1) page 9-10 shows: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00; Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00; Basic Income (Employed, CDH Recruitment) Monthly £1,200.00 net; State Pension Monthly £230.00 net (page 9). Page 6 confirms occupation as HGV Driver, employer CDH Recruitment. Expenditure detail (page 9-10): Rent £600, Council Tax £200, Electricity £120, Water £60, Food & Personal Care £300, Car/Travelling Expenses £150, Building Insurance £25, Entertainment £100 = total £1,718.00 monthly. No mention of spouse (Kim) income in Fact Find; no weekly HGV income of £300 stated; no Sea Cadets volunteer income of £20 per week stated. Spouse information appears only in family section showing spouse date of birth 07/05/1960, age 65, relationship spouse, no financial dependants, living with client.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states HGV driver income £300 per week net (page 3); Fact Find shows employed income as £1,200.00 per month net (page 9), which annualizes to £14,400 gross/net annually. £300 per week = £15,600 annually (52 weeks). These do not reconcile: £15,600 ≠ £1,200 monthly (£14,400 annual).
      - Report states Sea Cadets volunteer income £20 per week; Fact Find contains no record of this income stream.
      - Report states spouse Kim earns £1,100 per month net (page 3); Fact Find does not disclose spouse employment income in income section and shows only marital status and dependent status.
      - Report states State Pension for client £230 per week; Fact Find shows £230.00 per month (page 9). Weekly £230 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,000 monthly; monthly £230 ≠ £1,000. These are incompatible.
      - Report total monthly income £4,386.67 derived from: £1,300 + £86.67 + £1,100 + £1,000 + £1,000 = £4,486.67, not £4,386.67 as stated. Internal arithmetic error in the extraction itself.
      - Report states total monthly income £4,386.67; Fact Find income total is £1,430.00 (page 10). Difference of £2,956.67 or 207% variance. These cannot both be correct.
      - Report states expenditure £1,700 per month; Fact Find shows £1,718.00 calculated total (page 10). Difference £18.00 or 1.1% variance. Within rounding tolerance but not exact match.
    The report and Fact Find diverge materially on income composition, frequency, and totals, with multiple irreconcilable figures. (1) HGV driver income: the report asserts £300 per week net but the Fact Find shows £1,200 per month employment income from CDH Recruitment (HGV Driver role). Converting weekly to monthly: £300 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,300 monthly, not £1,200 monthly. The 8.3% shortfall (£100 per month) is material and cannot be explained by rounding or as-at date difference. (2) Sea Cadets income: stated in report as £20 per week (£86.67 monthly equivalent) with no corroborating entry in the Fact Find income schedule. This income is unsupported by the evidence. (3) Spouse income: the report lists Kim's employment income as £1,100 per month net on page 3, but the Fact Find does not disclose this in the income section (page 9), recording only marital status (spouse) and family details. The £1,100 figure is asserted in the report with no corresponding entry in the supporting Fact Find. (4) State Pension frequency mismatch: the report states £230 per week for the client's State Pension (annualizes to £11,960 ÷ 12 = £996.67 monthly), while the Fact Find states £230.00 per month. These represent different amounts: weekly £230 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,000 monthly, whereas monthly £230 is 77% lower. The discrepancy cannot be reconciled by treating them as the same frequency or basis. (5) Total monthly income: the report derives £4,386.67 (stated as 'total') by summing: £1,300 + £86.67 + £1,100 + £1,000 + £1,000 = £4,486.67, which contradicts its own stated total of £4,386.67 (internal error of £100). The Fact Find total monthly income is £1,430.00 (page 10), a 207% variance from the report's figure. (6) Expenditure: the report states £1,700 monthly; the Fact Find calculated total is £1,718.00 (1.1% variance, within tolerance). On income, the guards do not explain the discrepancies: the weekly-to-monthly conversion rule (52 ÷ 12 not 4.33 × 12) introduces only 0.4% arithmetic tolerance, well below the 8.3% income shortfall observed. The missing basis (gross vs. net) guard is negated here because both the report and Fact Find state net basis where disclosed. The report contradicts itself on total income (£4,386.67 stated vs. £4,486.67 derived from its own components). The report also asserts figures (spouse income, Sea Cadets income) that have no supporting entry in any section of the Fact Find. These are unsupported assertions.

  [G1.11] Facts used in calculations, cashflow modelling, projections and assumptions match the facts stated in the report narrative
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that cashflow modelling was performed (wasCashflowModelled = true) and discloses the following key assumptions: General expenditure inflation 2%, Savings inflation 2%, State pensions inflation 2.5%, Aviva personal pensions inflation 5%, Low growth rate 0.00%, Mid growth rate 2.95%, High growth rate 5.88%, Surplus income percentage saved 0%, Retirement age (client) within 2 years, Retirement age (spouse) age 66, Annual expenditure in retirement approximately £1,700 per month, Critical yield required 4.8% per year to age 75 (mid-growth scenario). The report states income from HGV driver work at £300 per week net, Kim receives £1,100 per month, client receives state pension £11,973 per annum, Kim receives state pension £11,973 per annum from age 67. Monthly liquid assets and accessible pensions totalling £116,997 comprised of: Peoples Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00. Current monthly surplus calculated as £1,786.67 (net income £3,486.67 minus expenditure £1,700).
    File:     The evidence pack contains the suitability report itself [P1, P22], intelliflo planning Baseline scenario [P2, P9, P10, P14], intelliflo planning proposed scenario [P4, P5, P6, P7, P11, P20, P21], and Pension switch report [P3, P12, P13, P15, P16, P18, P19]. The cashflow assumptions in [P1] and [P11] confirm: General expenditure 2%, Savings 2%, State pensions 2.5%, Aviva personal pensions 5%, Surplus income percentage saved 0%. The proposed scenario [P7] shows total assets of £122,997 comprising: Savings £6,000, Peoples pension £6,601, Scottish Widows £211, New Aviva pension £110,185. The Baseline scenario [P14] lists opening values: Savings £6,000, Aviva SP50163762 £3,744, Zurich P10026-719-001/DL £103,439, Standard Life D2301334000 £3,002, Peoples pension 6197175/PP £6,601, Scottish Widows ZU4225575 £211, totalling £116,997. [P22] confirms: Kim receives £1,100 per month, client receives state pension £11,973 per annum, Kim receives state pension £11,973 per annum from age 67, annual expenditure approximately £1,700, cashflow runs to age 100, surplus income is spent not saved. The critical yield table in [P1] shows retirement age 75, mid-growth rate 2.95% (not 2.94%), and critical yield 4.8%.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states mid-growth rate 2.95% in /needsAnalysis/assumptions but [P15] Pension switch report shows mid-growth rate 2.94%
      - Report states retirement age for critical yield analysis as 75 but client is age 69 and expects to retire 'within 2 years' (age 71), not age 75
      - Report's derived current monthly income (£3,486.67) does not reconcile: if HGV driver £300/week net = £1,300/month, Kim £1,100/month = £2,400/month, and state pension £11,973/annum = £998/month, total is £4,698/month gross, or approximately £3,500 net depending on tax treatment, but the stated figure of £3,486.67 does not appear corroborated
      - Report shows page numbers 19, 18, 19, 19 for assumption extracts but evidence [P1] shows these assumptions on a single section headed 'Cashflow Assumptions' with no differentiation by page within that section
      - Figures checked in code: page 19 cited for assumptions but nearest match is page 20 in evidence; page 14 cited for retirement within 2 years but that retirement age is stated as 'within next two years' not tied to specific age; page 66 (spouse retirement age) is corroborated but stated as from 'age 66' not as retirement date in specific future year
    The cashflow modelling was performed and is evidenced by the Baseline and Proposed scenario documents [P2, P4, P7, P14]. The core inflation and spending assumptions are consistently stated across the report and supporting documents. The critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth 2.95% is corroborated by [P1]. However, a material inconsistency arises: the critical yield comparison table uses retirement age 75 (as stated in the footnote of [P1]), but the client is currently age 69 and the report itself states 'You expect to retire within the next two years' [P22], which would be age 71, not 75. The retirement age 75 appears to be the analysis horizon for the pension switch comparison, not the client's actual retirement age. This is an internal tension within the report: the cashflow analysis uses client retirement within 2 years (approximately age 71) but the switching analysis uses age 75 as the comparison point. This is a methodological choice (comparing to a fixed age 75 maturity for the pensions, which is standard FCA practice for pension illustrations per [P1] footnote explaining 'The retirement age used is 75'), but it creates potential confusion between the client's intended retirement age and the illustration maturity point used in the switching analysis. The monthly surplus of £1,786.67 cannot be exactly verified from the passages as the component income figures do not cleanly reconcile when summed and divided by 12, though [P22] confirms the narrative uses £1,700 annual (monthly) expenditure and references income sources that approximate to the figure stated. The proposed scenario [P7] shows total assets £122,997, but the Baseline scenario [P14] shows £116,997 (£6,000 savings + £116,997 pensions). The difference (£6,000) is the liquid savings/surplus account, which is correctly included in the proposed scenario total. Page number citations for assumptions: [P1] presents all assumptions under 'Cashflow Assumptions' and 'Critical Yield' headings; the extraction noted page 19 for several but evidence shows these consolidated in a single document section. The guard on illustration growth rates (prescribed and differing from cashflow assumptions) applies here: the critical yield analysis uses illustrative growth rates (0.00%, 2.95%, 5.88%) which differ from any cashflow assumption and are FCA-prescribed; this is not an inconsistency. The mid-growth rate discrepancy (2.95% vs 2.94%) is minimal and within rounding tolerance for financial modelling. Overall, facts and figures used in the cashflow are consistent between the narrative and the modelling outputs, with the caveat that the switching analysis's use of age 75 as the comparison point differs from the client's stated retirement intention but follows FCA standard practice for pension illustrations.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 6 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7). Report asserts single target date of 2027-10-14 with 2-year term, but evidence shows two different retirement dates: Kim retiring age 66 in 2026 (approximately 1 year from 2025) and John retiring age 71 in 2027 (approximately 2 years from 2025). Report states intendedRetirementAge of 66 only, but evidence shows both clients with different retirement horizons: one at 66 (2026) and one at 71 (2027). Report lists timeHorizon as both 'LongTerm' and 'ShortTerm' but does not clearly assign which objective corresponds to which horizon. 16 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three client objectives in priority order: (1) Ensure continued affordability of lifestyle through lifetime (priority rank 1); (2) Retire within two years with household income of £1,700 net per month (priority rank 2); (3) Consolidate pensions into a streamlined structure for clarity and retirement preparation (priority rank 3). All three are marked as client-stated (isClientStated: true).
    File:     The fact find [P3], [P7] records: calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00; total net monthly income of £1,430.00; disposable income of -£288.00 per month (expenditure exceeds income). The fact find [P12] records retirement planning details: 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month' and consolidation priority: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has.' File notes [P5] record the client is 'in receipt of a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week, continue to work as an HGV driver earning around £300 per week net, and receive an additional £20 per week from your role with the Sea Cadets.' The intelliflo planning timeline [P8] shows retirement ages: Kim (spouse) age 66 in 2026, John (client) age 71 in 2027, confirming 'two years' is approximate (from September 2025 to 2027). The suitability report [P2] restates all three objectives: 'You want to ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime. You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month. You would like a clearer understanding of your pension arrangements and to ensure they are appropriately structured for your retirement. You also prefer a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers.'
    All three objectives stated in the suitability report are corroborated by evidence. Objective 1 (afford lifestyle through lifetime) is supported by: the fact find expenditure calculation showing £1,718 monthly household spend [P7], the client's statement in the fact find that they expect no changes to expenditure in the foreseeable future [P7], and the file notes recording capacity for loss discussion [P5]. Objective 2 (retire in 2 years with £1,700 net per month household income) is explicitly evidenced: the fact find retirement planning section [P12] records 'he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month' verbatim; the intelliflo timeline [P8] confirms retirement ages aligning with this (Kim at 66 in 2026, John at 71 in 2027); and the target income of £1,700 matches household expenditure in current-day terms. Objective 3 (consolidate pensions into streamlined structure) is recorded in the fact find [P12] as 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has' and reinforced in file notes discussing consolidation rationale. The priority order (lifestyle affordability first, then retirement income target, then consolidation for clarity) is implicit in the fact find's placement of retirement planning as the primary advice area and the recurrent emphasis on consolidation. The wording in the report is a paraphrase of the fact find language but captures the substance accurately. No material inconsistencies exist between what the report asserts and what the evidence records. The current cash flow deficit (£288 per month shortfall [P7]) is addressed by the retirement planning scenario, which models the position post-retirement when employment income ceases and pension drawdown begins. The planning timeline and projected asset positions [P17] show the strategy is designed to support the stated retirement income target from age 71 onwards. Each objective is client-stated and supported by contemporaneous evidence from the fact find and file notes.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) Target date of 2027-10-14 with termYears of 2 years; (2) TimeHorizon values of both 'LongTerm' and 'ShortTerm'; (3) Intended retirement age of 66 (for one party); (4) Life expectancy basis: ONS life expectancy for 69-year-old male is age 86, and for 65-year-old female is age 88; (5) Funds expected to last throughout lifetime; (6) No cashflow shortfall issues; (7) State pension income will cover bills in retirement.
    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Timeline in intelliflo planning [P10] states retirement (Kim) at age 66 in 2026, and retirement (John) at age 71 in 2027; (2) State pension age for both parties is 66 [P10]; (3) Minimum pension age is 55 [P10]; (4) Current ages are 69 (male, born 07/06/1956) and 65 (female, born 07/05/1960) [P10]; (5) Pension switch report [P1] shows retirement age 75 for Aviva plan illustration; (6) Risk profile report [P11] states investment period of 2 years with investment of £106,000; (7) Zurich plan selected retirement age is 65, planned retirement age is 75 [P15]; (8) Standard Life plan illustrations requested to age 65 but member already 69, normal retirement date used [P20]; (9) Life expectancy of 86 for 69-year-old male and 88 for 65-year-old female match report [P24].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts single target date of 2027-10-14 with 2-year term, but evidence shows two different retirement dates: Kim retiring age 66 in 2026 (approximately 1 year from 2025) and John retiring age 71 in 2027 (approximately 2 years from 2025)
      - Report states intendedRetirementAge of 66 only, but evidence shows both clients with different retirement horizons: one at 66 (2026) and one at 71 (2027)
      - Report lists timeHorizon as both 'LongTerm' and 'ShortTerm' but does not clearly assign which objective corresponds to which horizon
      - Pension switch report [P1] uses retirement age 75 for illustrations, contradicting the 66/71 ages stated in planning documents
      - Zurich plan [P15] shows selected retirement age 65 but planned retirement age 75, creating ambiguity about which retirement date applies
      - Risk profile investment period of 2 years [P11] aligns with one objective but the report does not explicitly link this to the specific client or goal
    The comparison can be performed because sufficient evidence exists across multiple documents. However, material inconsistencies emerge: (1) The report's extraction captured a single target date (2027-10-14) and 2-year term, but the underlying case involves a couple with differentiated retirement objectives. The intelliflo planning document [P10] clearly shows two retirement events: Kim retiring at 66 in 2026 and John retiring at 71 in 2027. This is not a simple mismatch but reflects the fact that the case involves multiple clients with different time horizons. (2) The report's stated intendedRetirementAge of 66 matches only one client's retirement date. (3) The assignment of 'LongTerm' and 'ShortTerm' to objectives is unclear in the extraction; a 2-year horizon to John's retirement (2027) is short-term, but the report also references life expectancy to age 86/88, implying a long-term draw-down phase. (4) The pension switch report [P1] uses age 75 as the retirement age for projections, which differs from both the planning document retirement dates and the 66/71 ages. This reflects the contractual default on the plans rather than the clients' intended retirement dates. The guard acknowledges this: 'Selected retirement age on a plan is a contractual default, not necessarily the client's intended date.' (5) The 2-year investment period in the risk profile [P11] and the 2-year termYears in the report's objectives align; however, the report does not clearly state which client this serves or how it reconciles with the longer accumulation phase to John's retirement in 2027 (approximately 2 years from mid-2025 to mid-2027). (6) Life expectancy figures corroborate the report's statements. (7) The cashflow and state pension coverage assertions are supported by the planning documents [P10, P24].

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies two access types: (1) Emergency access of £6,000 held in cash savings to cover 'Unexpected emergencies', with the statement 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies, giving you reassurance as you progress with your retirement planning' [P10]; and (2) Planned access via Pension Flexi Access Drawdown (FAD) with a note that 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' [P10]. The report confirms the client is 'comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' [P10]. The report also states 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends' [P24], indicating that pension funds are preserved for ad-hoc access rather than immediate drawdown.
    File:     The Fact Find [P19] confirms cash savings of £6,000 held in a savings account as of 22/09/2025. The intelliflo planning documents [P1, P2, P5, P9] model liquid assets (cash) of £6,000 as the opening position in 2025, classified separately from accessible and non-accessible pensions. The planning projections show liquid assets declining modestly over time (from £6,000 in 2025 to £5,956 in 2026, then gradually to £4,773 by age 100/96 in 2056) [P2, P9], demonstrating that emergency reserves are maintained throughout the projection. The report describes five pension arrangements available for FAD: People's Pension (offering 'Full and partial UFPLS'), Aviva (offering 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS'), Zurich (offering 'Full and partial UFPLS'), Standard Life (offering 'FAD'), and Scottish Widows (offering 'Full and partial UFPLS') [P23]. The recommendation consolidates these into a single Aviva Platform Personal Pension with a transfer value of £110,185 [P21], which supports FAD access. The cashflow analysis [P24] confirms no shortfall is projected throughout the lifetime, indicating sufficient income coverage without requiring immediate pension access.
    The report's assertion of emergency and planned access requirements is fully corroborated by the evidence. The £6,000 emergency fund is documented consistently across the Fact Find, the baseline scenario, and the proposed scenario, all showing the same opening value. The client's confirmation of sufficiency is supported by the planning projections, which demonstrate that liquid assets remain available (though declining) throughout the plan horizon, and that employment income plus state pension income is projected to cover expenditure without shortfall, meaning pension access for emergencies is not forced. The 10-15 working day access window stated for pension drawdown is a standard operating characteristic of FAD and is not contradicted by any document. The recommendation to consolidate into an Aviva Platform Personal Pension is appropriate because Aviva explicitly offers FAD [P23], meeting the stated planned access requirement. The statement 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement' is supported by the cashflow analysis [P24], which shows total expenses covered by combined income sources. No access need is left unaddressed: emergency access is provided via the £6,000 cash reserve; planned access is provided via FAD in the recommended Aviva plan; and ad-hoc access is supported by pension growth over time as described. The access timeline (10-15 working days for pension access) is reasonable and disclosed. All access requirements are client-specific (drawn from confirmed client statements) and are linked to specific funding mechanisms.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three prioritized objectives: (1) 'You want to ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime'; (2) 'You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month'; (3) 'You would like a clearer understanding of your pension arrangements and to ensure they are appropriately structured for your retirement. You also prefer a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers.' The report states a retirement target date of 2027-10-14 (2 years from the report date), with a projected retirement age of 71 for one party and state pension age of 66 for the other. The target date is corroborated by the planning timeline which shows 'Retire (Kim)' in 2026 at age 70/66 and 'Retire (John)' in 2027 at age 71/67. The report provides cashflow projections to age 75 (P8) as the retirement age used for calculations. The planning scenario extends projections to 2060 (ages 104/100), implying a lifetime horizon. The objectives link to recommendations: consolidation into a single Aviva platform, balanced risk rating 5 funds for growth to retirement, and ongoing adviser service (1% per annum) for review.
    File:     The planning baseline scenario (P15, P16) identifies opening cash savings of £6,000 and five existing pensions totalling £116,997. The proposed scenario (P11, P12) shows the same household with events marked as: 'Retire (Kim)' 2026 age 70/66, 'Retire (John)' 2027 age 71/67, 'Illness (John)' 2056 age 100, 'Illness (Kim)' 2060 age 100, and death events at the same ages. Cashflow tables (P9) show total regular income in 2026 of £40,703 (including employment income of £16,640 plus spouse £13,200), dropping to £24,181 in 2027 after one retirement, then stabilizing. Surplus/deficit columns show positive cash flow sustained throughout, with liquid assets and accessible pensions remaining stable around £122,900–£122,997 through the planning horizon (P9, P5). The planning does not project a specific household income target of £1,700 net per month; rather, it models total expenditure and shows surpluses or deficits against income. Regular expenditure in first year is stated as £24,001 (P16), implying required income of at least this amount plus tax/NI.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states a retirement income target of '£1,700 net per month' (P4), but no supporting detail in the planning files quantifies or validates this specific figure. The cashflow assumes total expenditure of £24,001 in the first year (P16), which annualizes to £2,000/month, higher than the stated £1,700 net target and does not match the itemized breakdown provided.
      - The report uses retirement age 75 as the planning horizon for critical yield calculations (P8), but the planning scenarios extend to age 100/104 (P5, P9, P11, P12), creating a 25-year mismatch in the stated time horizon used for investment justification.
      - Priority ranking is extracted as [1, 2, 3] (three distinct priorities) and a target date of '2027-10-14', but no targetAmount field is present in the extracted model. The £1,700 net monthly income target is stated narratively in the report (P4) but is not extracted as a discrete targetAmount, creating ambiguity about whether this is a constrained objective or a descriptive statement.
      - The report states the client 'prefer[s] a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans' (P4), and the recommendation consolidates four plans into a new Aviva solution (switching People's, Scottish Widows, Standard Life, and Aviva's own consolidation fund). However, the Zurich arrangement (£103,439, the largest holding) is retained and not switched, meaning the recommendation does not fully achieve the stated consolidation objective—it reduces from 5 to 2 plans (new Aviva + retained Zurich).
    The suitability report asserts three client objectives with explicit priority ranking. Objective 1 (maintain lifestyle through lifetime) is supported by the planning projections, which show cash flow remaining in surplus throughout the 60-year horizon with no required drawdowns exceeding available assets (P5, P9). Objective 2 (retire in 2 years with £1,700 net per month household income) is stated but not quantitatively validated in the evidence. The planning baseline shows total expenditure of £24,001 in the first planning year (2025, before either retirement), which does not align to a discrete £1,700/month net target. The cashflow model does not isolate the net household income available after retirement; instead, it models total money in and total money out, showing overall surplus. The stated target of '£1,700 net per month' (£20,400 per annum) is lower than the £24,001 annual expenditure modelled, but no document confirms the target has been stress-tested or validated against the proposed plan. Objective 3 (consolidation and streamlining of five separate plans) is partially addressed: the recommendation switches four smaller arrangements into a single Aviva platform but retains Zurich (£103,439, 88.5% of total pension value). The stated preference for 'streamlined' management is thus not fully met by the recommendation, which still leaves two separate providers. The report does link recommendations to objectives (P3, P4, P6: funds chosen for 'retirement objectives', consolidation into Aviva platform), but the linkage to objective 3 is incomplete because the largest holding remains outside the recommended consolidation. The retirement age discrepancy (age 75 used for yield calculations vs. 100/104 in planning projections) is a methodological choice, not an inconsistency: age 75 is the assumed drawdown trigger for critical yield purposes, while the planning extends to natural lifespan. This is explained in the report footnote (P8: 'The retirement age used is 75'). The three priority ranks [1, 2, 3] are extracted but no targetAmount appears. The narrative confirms priorities as ordered but does not explicitly state relative importance weights or trade-offs. The vulnerability overlay: no support needs, communication requirements, or reasonable adjustments are flagged in the evidence (category B fact find, P21, contains no vulnerability markers), so the vulnerability overlay does not materially affect this check.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts five recommendations linked to objectives: recommendations 1 and 2 are linked to OBJ3 only; recommendations 3, 4, and 5 are linked to both OBJ2 and OBJ3. The report states a selected retirement age of 69 for the recommended Aviva Pension Portfolio. The report identifies a need for £1,700 net per month income in retirement. The report states planned withdrawals include taking up to 25% of the pension fund as a tax-free lump sum at outset and regular or ad-hoc income withdrawals thereafter, taxed at marginal rate. Regular contributions are stated as £1,193.33 per annum (client) and £897.13 per annum (employer). The report projects cashflow to age 100 and states 'based on the current assumptions, no shortfall is projected throughout your lifetime' and 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement.'
    File:     [P1] states client needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement. [P10] (report itself) confirms client expects annual expenditure in retirement of approximately £1,700 and includes investments from Aviva transfer (£3,744), Zurich transfer (£103,439), and Standard Life (£3,002). [P10] shows state pension income of £11,973 per annum and Kim will receive £11,973 per annum from age 67. Employment income stated as £16,640 in baseline scenario [P8]. [P6] and [P7] confirm normal minimum pension age currently 55, rising to 57 from 6 April 2028, and that client can take up to 25% tax-free, with remainder taxed at marginal rate. [P24] (Fact Find) shows total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 and total net monthly income of £1,430, resulting in monthly disposable income of -£288. The Fact Find records 'No' to whether client expects any changes in expenditure in foreseeable future. No specific time horizons, foreseeable life changes, or ad-hoc access requirements beyond the standard pension access rules are documented in the evidence. The objectives OBJ2 and OBJ3 themselves are not defined in any retrieved passage.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states retirement age of 69; evidence shows normal minimum pension age is currently 55 (rising to 57 from April 2028), but the report's selected retirement age of 69 is not contradicted by evidence—it is client's chosen age, which is permissible. However, no evidence in the file records the client explicitly choosing age 69 as their retirement age.
      - Report links recommendations to OBJ2 and OBJ3, but neither objective is defined or described in any retrieved passage; the objectives themselves are absent from the evidence.
      - Report states need for £1,700 net per month income in retirement; [P24] Fact Find shows current household expenditure of £1,718 per month with current net income of £1,430 per month, producing a monthly shortfall of £288. This creates tension between stated retirement income need and current affordability, though the report's cashflow projection [P10] states no shortfall is projected. The discrepancy is not between stated need (£1,700) and income (which is described as coming from state pension and drawdown in retirement), but between current expenditure exceeding current income and the assumption that retirement income will be adequate.
      - Report states regular contributions of £1,193.33 (client) and £897.13 (employer) annually; [P5] (Terms and Conditions) and other evidence confirm these amounts are structured and deductible, but no evidence confirms these contributions are sustainable or that the client has capacity to maintain them given current monthly deficit of -£288 shown in [P24].
    The requirement asks whether stated client goals, priorities, time horizons, access requirements and foreseeable life changes are consistent with and supported by evidence. Comparison can be performed on what is evidenced, but material components are absent. The report links recommendations to OBJ2 and OBJ3, but these objectives are never defined in any document retrieved. The canonical model carries the labels but not the content; a passage categorized [I] (the report itself) cannot be cited to support what the report asserts, so the absence of OBJ2 and OBJ3 definitions anywhere in the file means these links rest on no evidence at all. The report states retirement age 69; this is not contradicted by evidence (access rules permit it), but no document records the client choosing this age or the reasoning behind it. The report identifies a need for £1,700 net per month in retirement and projects no shortfall to age 100; this is consistent with [P10] which incorporates the same figure. However, [P24] shows the client's current household expenditure of £1,718 per month against net income of £1,430, producing a monthly deficit of £288 before any account is taken of the pension strategy. The report does not address this current deficit or how the recommended contributions (£1,193.33 client + £897.13 employer = £2,090.46 annually or £174.21 per month) bridge a current shortfall of £288 per month, nor does it explain how the client funds current spending given the documented £288 monthly deficit. The access mechanism (retirement age 69, flexible drawdown, 25% tax-free, remainder taxable) is consistent with standard pension rules and the report's description. However, without definition of OBJ2 and OBJ3, the recommendation cannot be demonstrated to address stated objectives; the link is by object ID only, not by narrative or specific purpose. The guard warns that a link by boilerplate is not evidence of linkage—require a specific objective to be named. Here the objectives are named (OBJ2, OBJ3) but never defined; the name alone is boilerplate in the absence of content. The report does not explain why the Aviva Pension Portfolio specifically is appropriate for the objectives it cites, beyond generic pension benefits. Time horizon is stated as 'LongTerm' and 'ShortTerm' in the canonical model, but no evidence defines what these mean for the client or how they relate to the stated retirement age of 69 (which is approximately 45 years away from the fact-find date, if the client is currently age 24, or a different horizon if age differs). No foreseeable life changes beyond standard pension access rules are documented; [P24] records the client's response as 'No' to expectation of expenditure changes, which is consistent with the static assumptions in the cashflow, but does not evidence that foreseeable life changes have been considered or discussed. Recommendations 1 and 2 are linked only to OBJ3; recommendations 3, 4, and 5 to both OBJ2 and OBJ3. Without definition of these objectives, the specificity of the linking cannot be assessed.

  [G2.7] Goals, assumptions or planning requirements stated in the report that are not supported by the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three client objectives: (1) 'Ensure continued affordability of lifestyle through lifetime' (stated, intended); (2) 'Retire within two years with household income of £1,700 net per month' (stated, intended); (3) 'Consolidate pensions into a streamlined structure for clarity and retirement preparation' (stated, intended). The report also states nine planning assumptions including retirement within 2 years, spouse retirement at age 66, annual expenditure of £1,700 per month, critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75, and various inflation rates (general expenditure 2%, savings 2%, state pensions 2.5%, Aviva pensions 5%). All three objectives are marked as 'isClientStated: true' with provenance from the Suitability Report Test 1.pdf, pages 3 and 14.
    File:     The Fact Find (P4, P5) dated 19/09/2025 records under 'Retirement Planning' the client's stated need: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' The Fact Find also shows (P8) 'Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure' of £1,718.00 and 'Total Net Monthly Income' of £1,430.00, yielding 'Total Monthly Disposable Income' of £-288.00. File notes (P1) record capacity for loss discussion: client 'confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' with income from State Pension ~£230/week, HGV driver earnings ~£300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week, and spouse Kim contributing £1,100/month and also receiving State Pension. The intelliflo planning scenario (P20) shows 'Total FUM Advised on and IAF OGS Fees: AUM: £116,998.49. IAF: 3% £3,509.95. OAF: 1% pa.' File notes (P1) confirm the client 'wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' and 'are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating' [note: there is internal confusion in P1 between risk 5 and risk 6, but final position is risk 5]. Fact Find (P9) shows existing personal pension value of £116,555.45 as at 23/07/2026. No explicit documentation of the critical yield calculation of 4.8% per year to age 75, the inflation assumptions (2% general expenditure, 2% savings, 2.5% state pensions, 5% Aviva pensions), or the 'ensure continued affordability of lifestyle through lifetime' objective is present in the retrieved evidence.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the client needs household income of £1,700 net per month in retirement. The Fact Find records current total monthly expenditure as £1,718.00 with net monthly income of £1,430.00, showing a current shortfall of £288/month, not a surplus. The report does not explain how a target of £1,700/month (exceeding current net income of £1,430/month) will be achieved or funded in retirement.
      - The report assumes '0% of any in-year surplus will be saved within the plan - all surplus income is spent' (page 19, Cashflow Assumptions) but current cash flow shows a monthly deficit of £288, not a surplus, creating internal contradiction in the planning model.
      - The report asserts the objective to 'Ensure continued affordability of lifestyle through lifetime' but provides no evidence of how this links to the stated retirement income goal of £1,700/month or how it will be delivered by the recommended strategy given current income/expenditure imbalance.
      - The file notes (P1) record risk rating 5 as agreed position, but also state 'you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5– Balanced investor, you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating' which is internally inconsistent—if 5 is the agreed position, it cannot simultaneously be higher than the agreed rating.
      - The assumptions around inflation (general expenditure 2%, savings 2%, state pensions 2.5%, Aviva pensions 5%) and the critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 are stated in the report but are not evidenced in any retrieved document; no illustration, cashflow schedule, or assumption confirmation appears in the file.
    The three stated objectives are corroborated at substance level by the Fact Find: consolidation is clearly evidenced (P5, P4), retirement within 2 years is stated (P5), and household income target of £1,700 net/month is recorded (P5). The file notes (P1) confirm the retirement timeframe and income target through discussion records. However, there is a material tension between the stated affordability objective and the actual financial position. The Fact Find shows current net income of £1,430/month against expenditure of £1,718/month—a deficit of £288/month—yet the retirement goal is £1,700/month, which exceeds current net income. The report does not reconcile this gap or explain how the affordability objective will be met. The guardpost permits that 'the adviser inferred and the client then confirmed at a meeting is stated, not unsupported—read the file notes before calling it unevidenced,' and the file notes do confirm client discussion of the retirement and consolidation goals. However, the first objective ('Ensure continued affordability of lifestyle through lifetime') appears inferred but lacks explicit client authorship in the evidence; it is not quoted in client's own terms anywhere in the Fact Find or file notes. The planning assumptions (inflation rates, critical yield, surplus income percentage) are all stated in the report but none appear in the retrieved evidence. The report's own extract metadata notes these assumptions as 'Stated' with 'Assumed' modality but they do not appear to be extracted from any evidence document in the pack. The critical yield of 4.8% appears in the extraction but the supporting calculation or cashflow modelling that would evidence it is not retrieved. The spouse's retirement age of 66 is recorded in the Fact Find reference in P1 ('Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66') so that is supported. The risk rating (5) is supported by file notes (P1) and internal consistency between the recommendation and the stated ATR is present. The vulnerability overlay: there is no evidence of vulnerability factors in the file that would materially affect this check—no mention of health, capacity, communication needs, or reasonable adjustments that would alter the suitability of the stated objectives or assumptions.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G3.1, G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.8, G3.10, G3.11). The report assigns a 'Medium' capacity for loss rating, but the risk profile report [P20] records 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Capacity for loss for the goal', suggesting no formal capacity for loss assessment or rating was documented at the time of the risk profiling process. The capacity for loss is asserted in the suitability report as a narrative conclusion but no corresponding quantified rating or loss tolerance threshold (e.g., acceptable loss in £ or %) is documented in the questionnaire, risk profile or supporting files. The report describes capacity for loss using lifestyle impact language ('would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living') but this is presented as a client confirmation statement rather than as an objective assessment of actual capacity based on financial modelling. 9 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G3.1] Risk rating and summary
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) Assessed risk rating: 4 ('Cautious Balanced'); (2) Agreed risk rating: 5 ('Balanced'); (3) Scale name: 'Risk Rating Scale'; (4) The client completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire that indicated rating 4, but the report states 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' and 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor' (P4). The report documents that the client is 'aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk' and confirms understanding that 'your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value' (P4).
    File:     The evidence corroborates both the assessed and agreed ratings on the same scale: (1) The Risk profile report [P1] shows the questionnaire result as 'Risk rating 4 Cautious Balanced' from responses completed 18/09/2025. (2) The Risk profile report [P1] and SIPP research report [P2, P6, P9] all confirm the agreed risk level as 'Risk rating 5 Balanced'. (3) The scale used throughout is consistently labelled 'Risk Rating Scale' on a 1-7 scale, with level 4 and level 5 both defined within that scale. (4) The descriptor for level 4 states 'As a Cautious Balanced investor you tend to see yourself as quite a cautious person and are inclined to view risk negatively rather than as a source of opportunity' [P1]. (5) The descriptor for level 5 states 'As a Balanced investor you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk' [P2, P6, P9]. (6) The Suitability Report [P4] explicitly documents the override: 'You completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire. We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire. You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5.'
    The comparison is complete and valid. Both the assessed code (4) and the agreed code (5) are evidenced on the same scale ('Risk Rating Scale', a 1-7 scale). The assessed rating of 4 is corroborated by the questionnaire responses in [P1] and [P17], which show responses indicating a cautious approach ('Cautious', 'More than £4,000', 'Caution', 'More cautious', 'A slight risk of capital loss'). The agreed rating of 5 is corroborated by [P1, P2, P6, P9], which document the final agreed risk level with its corresponding 'Balanced' descriptor. The override from 4 to 5 is explicitly justified in [P4]: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite', and client acknowledgement is documented: 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor, you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk with your pension.' The client's capacity for loss is also addressed in [P4]: 'You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living. You are in receipt of a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week, continue to work as an HGV driver earning around £300 per week net, and receive an additional £20 per week from your role with the Sea Cadets.' The recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced, both Risk Rating 5) in [P10] align with the agreed risk rating of 5. The scale is consistent throughout (1-7 scale termed 'Risk Rating Scale'), so no cross-scale comparison error arises. The override represents a legitimate upward adjustment from the initial questionnaire result, justified by the adviser's view and supported by explicit client agreement and understanding of the implications.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts a capacity for loss rating of 'Medium' and bases this on: (1) client's income stability — State Pension £230/week, HGV driver income £300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week, plus spouse's £1,100/month employment income and State Pension eligibility; (2) household surplus — combined income comfortably meets £1,700/month expenditure; (3) liquid assets — £6,000 in savings; (4) no outstanding liabilities; (5) client's confirmation that 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living'; (6) client's limited investment experience but understanding that returns are not guaranteed.
    File:     The case file contains: (1) Risk profile report [P1] and [P20] showing 'Capacity for loss for the goal' with a note 'No additional notes were recorded', indicating no separate capacity for loss assessment was documented in the risk questionnaire section; (2) Capacity for Loss questionnaire responses [P2] in a table format with no recorded notes; (3) Suitability report [P11] restating the same income, expenditure and asset figures verbatim; (4) Financial planning documents [P3], [P4], [P6], [P10], [P12] showing baseline and proposed scenario cashflows, confirming £6,000 liquid assets, household income and expenditure alignment, and projected surplus throughout retirement horizon to age 100/96; (5) No separate written capacity for loss assessment document; (6) No quantified loss tolerance scenario or modelling of loss impact on lifestyle.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report assigns a 'Medium' capacity for loss rating, but the risk profile report [P20] records 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Capacity for loss for the goal', suggesting no formal capacity for loss assessment or rating was documented at the time of the risk profiling process.
      - The capacity for loss is asserted in the suitability report as a narrative conclusion but no corresponding quantified rating or loss tolerance threshold (e.g., acceptable loss in £ or %) is documented in the questionnaire, risk profile or supporting files.
      - The report describes capacity for loss using lifestyle impact language ('would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living') but this is presented as a client confirmation statement rather than as an objective assessment of actual capacity based on financial modelling.
      - No large-loss scenario or stress test specific to capacity for loss appears in the evidence; the stress tests in [P15] model market crashes and inflation but frame them as tests of whether pension funds would be needed for living expenses, not as direct assessments of loss capacity.
    The capacity for loss rating of 'Medium' is supported by the financial facts: the client has stable multi-source income (State Pension, employment, volunteer income), a spouse with similar income security, household expenditure that is comfortable within combined income, £6,000 in readily accessible savings, no liabilities, and projected positive cashflow throughout the planning horizon to age 100/96 as shown in [P3], [P4], [P6] and [P10]. These financial supports are all corroborated verbatim in the evidence.
    
    However, the capacity for loss assessment as presented in the report does not meet the standard for a separately evidenced CFL evaluation. The risk profile report [P20] explicitly records 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Capacity for loss for the goal', and the questionnaire responses [P2] likewise record no notes. The capacity for loss appears to have been assessed informally or not documented as a discrete exercise. The report's statement that 'You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' is presented as a client view rather than as a structured adviser assessment. COBS 9.2.1R requires that capacity for loss be specifically assessed; whilst the financial facts support a medium rating, the absence of documented CFL-specific assessment methodology, loss tolerance quantification, or scenario modelling means the CFL rating is asserted as a restatement of the financial position rather than as an independently justified assessment. The guard notes that 'A CFL rating with no supporting financial reasoning is not evidenced merely because the words appear'—here the opposite applies: financial reasoning exists but is not explicitly labelled as or framed as a capacity for loss assessment. The stress tests in [P15] confirm the portfolio would withstand a -30% loss and continued 6% inflation without requiring funds for living expenses, which provides indirect support for a medium (not low) capacity; but this stress test is framed as a cashflow test, not as an explicit CFL validation. The client's confirmation statement relies on his subjective perception ('you confirmed') rather than on a documented adviser assessment of actual loss capacity. The report does not quantify loss tolerance (e.g., acceptable £ loss, or % drawdown).
    
    Citation of the income figures confirms the report's factual assertions are accurate and corroborated. The assessment framework—stable income, manageable expenditure, liquid assets, no liabilities—is sound and supported. But the separate identification and documentation of capacity for loss as distinct from attitude to risk is not evident in the supporting evidence files. COBS 9.2.1R requires both ATR and CFL to be assessed; the report conflates them or presents CFL as a consequence of financial position rather than as a structured, documented evaluation.
    
    The check asks whether CFL is 'consistent with and supported by the evidence'. It is consistent (the financial position supports medium capacity), but the independent documentary evidence of the CFL assessment itself—as a separate, evidenced exercise—is absent. This is a material gap: the report asserts a CFL rating but the file shows no contemporaneous CFL assessment or questionnaire response analysis that would ordinarily support such a rating.

Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month, you hold savings of around £6,000, and have no outstanding liabilities.   [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts two statements about tolerance for loss: (1) 'You are aware that investment values can rise as well as fall, and although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed' [P18]; (2) 'You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value' [P18]. The report further states 'You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' and details the client's income sources (State Pension £230/week, HGV driver income £300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week, spouse contribution £1,100/month) [P18]. The report does not quantify a specific tolerance for loss amount or percentage. The agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced), and the report notes stress tests including a -30% immediate market crash scenario with high inflation [P23], which the client's plan could withstand without requiring pension drawdown.
    File:     The Risk Profile Report [P19] records capacity for loss questionnaire responses but states 'No additional notes were recorded' under the Capacity for Loss Questionnaire section [P24]. The report confirms an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) [P14, P19]. The SIPP research report [P1] describes risk level 5 as suitable for a 'Balanced investor' who 'do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person' and 'understand that you may need to take some risk to meet your investment goals' [P5]. The planning scenario [P10] projects cash flow and asset allocation to age 100+ with pensions accessible and liquid assets available. The pension switch report [P7, P12] models three growth scenarios (0%, 2.98%, 5.88%) with projected maturity values at age 75, all of which are positive across scenarios. No explicit quantified tolerance for loss (e.g., maximum acceptable loss, loss capacity as percentage of portfolio) is documented in any evidence.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts tolerance for loss through two generic statements about volatility awareness and downside risk acknowledgement, but provides no quantified measure of how much loss the client can tolerate (e.g., maximum drawdown, percentage of capital at risk, specific loss scenario). The evidence includes stress tests (e.g., -30% market crash) but does not establish these as the client's stated tolerance threshold or capacity limit.
      - The report states 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' as a capacity for loss assessment, but this is a conclusion about sufficiency of income, not a measurement of loss tolerance or capacity. No evidence links this income sufficiency to a specific acceptable loss amount or horizon.
      - The Risk Profile Report records that a Capacity for Loss Questionnaire was completed [P24] but states 'No additional notes were recorded', which creates ambiguity: either no follow-up discussion occurred, or notes of that discussion were not documented. The questionnaire responses themselves are not quoted or summarized in the passages provided.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Quantified tolerance for loss (maximum acceptable loss in absolute or percentage terms), Capacity for loss questionnaire responses (the actual answers given, not just acknowledgement that it was completed), Documented discussion or notes on capacity for loss findings from the questionnaire, Need-level risk assessment — the report does not extract any objective need-level risk figure, only inferred tolerance from income sufficiency
    The comparison cannot be fully performed because the critical input — a quantified tolerance for loss — is absent from both the report's extracted canonical model and the supporting evidence. The requirement asks whether tolerance for loss and need-level risk are 'consistent with and supported by evidence'. The report makes two factual assertions about the client's awareness of volatility and downside risk, both of which are generic statements repeated verbatim across similar advice. These are supported as client acknowledgements by [P18], which quotes them from the suitability report itself — internal consistency, not external support. The report infers capacity for loss from the client's income position (State Pension, employment income, spouse contribution) and stress-tests the plan against a -30% loss scenario [P23]. This approach is reasonable and supported by the financial position documented, but it is not a quantified tolerance for loss assertion that can be compared to evidence. The Risk Profile Report [P19, P24] records that a Capacity for Loss Questionnaire was administered on 18/09/2025, but the responses are not quoted in any passage provided, only the process acknowledged. The guard states: 'Many firms do not quantify tolerance for loss separately; absence is a documentation gap, not a contradiction.' Here, tolerance for loss is not quantified in either the report or the evidence. Need-level risk is not extracted as a separate figure by the canonical model. The suitability report documents the agreed risk level (5) and justifies it by reference to the client's wish for 'best growth potential over a 10-year period' [P18] and income sufficiency [P18], but does not separate or define what risk is 'needed' to meet objectives from what risk the client can 'tolerate' or 'afford to take'. Under COBS 9.2.1R and 9A.2.1R, the firm must assess capacity for loss; the evidence shows a questionnaire was administered but its results are not presented in the passages retrieved. The stress tests (0% low, 2.95% mid, 5.88% high growth; -30% immediate loss) demonstrate scenario modelling, but these are provider-generated projections, not measures of the client's own quantified capacity or tolerance. The comparison is therefore incomplete: the report's tolerance for loss and need-level risk assertions cannot be substantively compared to quantified evidence because neither side provides quantification.
