  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts an investment term (investmentTermYears) of 10 years. The report also lists objectives with time horizons of both 'LongTerm' and 'ShortTerm'.
    File:     [P3] states 'Investment Period: 2 years' in the risk level justification table for the agreed risk level 5 (Balanced). [P5] shows a planning timeline from 2025 (current) through 2060, with retirement events: client JS retires at age 66 in 2026 (1 year), and client KS retires at age 71 in 2027 (2 years). [P1] references 'desired time horizon' as a factor in discussion but does not specify what that horizon is. [P4], [P6], [P7], [P8] provide cashflow projections extending to 2060 (35 years from 2025). [P9], [P10] confirm agreed risk level 5 (Balanced) without specifying a time horizon. [P13] states investments 'should be viewed as medium to long term (typically more than five years)'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts investment term of 10 years; [P3] explicitly states 'Investment Period: 2 years' for the risk level projection.
      - Report lists objectives with both 'LongTerm' and 'ShortTerm' horizons; [P5] shows retirement occurring in 2026 (1 year for JS) and 2027 (2 years for KS), which is neither 10 years nor aligned with a single long-term horizon.
      - Report's 10-year term cannot be reconciled with the 2-year investment period used in the risk projection [P3] or the 1–2 year retirement timelines in [P5].
      - [P4], [P6], [P7], [P8] project cashflows extending to age 100 (2060, 35+ years), suggesting planning horizon differs materially from the stated 10-year investment term.
    The report asserts an investment term of 10 years, but the risk profile document [P3] explicitly states 'Investment Period: 2 years' in the table used to justify the agreed risk level 5 (Balanced). This is a direct contradiction. The report also asserts mixed time horizons ('LongTerm' and 'ShortTerm'), but the planning evidence [P5] shows the clients are approaching retirement imminently: JS retires in 2026 (age 66, current age 69 per [P5]) — actually already retired or retiring immediately — and KS retires in 2027 (age 71, current age 65). This creates a critical inconsistency: a 10-year investment term is incompatible with retirement in 1–2 years. The guard permits a longer horizon where money is invested through retirement, but the report must say so. The planning scenarios [P6]–[P8] do project decades ahead, but the risk justification [P3] uses a 2-year period for the projections that establish suitability of risk level 5. A 10-year term would support a higher allocation to growth assets than a 2-year term; the report does not explain why a Balanced (level 5) profile is appropriate for a 10-year horizon when the risk projection was modelled on a 2-year one, nor why the multiple time horizons are reconciled. The objectives in the canonical model include both ShortTerm and LongTerm, but the evidence does not clarify which horizon applies to which component of the recommendation, or how the 10-year assertion relates to the imminent retirement dates.

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Target risk rating: 5. Holdings: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk rating 5, 0.18% OCF) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk rating 5, 0.29% OCF). Strategy type: Model Portfolio. Total allocation: 100%.
    File:     [P8] Agreed risk level 5 Balanced investor. [P11] Fund recommendation table shows HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 50% Risk Rating 5 Annual Charge 0.18%, CT Universal MAP Balanced C 50% Risk Rating 5 Annual Charge 0.29%, Total 100%. [P10] 'The client's agreed risk level is 5. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' [P16] 'The client's agreed risk level is 5. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' [P2] CT Universal MAP Balanced fund classified in category 4 (medium fluctuations historically). [P3] HSBC fund rated 4 on Risk and Reward Indicator. [P12] HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio: 'The Fund's risk profile is rated as 3 where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk.'
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts both recommended funds carry risk rating 5; [P3] states HSBC fund 'is rated 4' on Risk and Reward Indicator; [P12] HSBC factsheet states 'The Fund's risk profile is rated as 3'. Risk ratings conflict across documents and do not align with report's assertion of 5 for HSBC.
      - Report asserts HSBC fund 0.18% OCF; [P12] fund factsheet does not state an OCF for the fund itself. OCF sourcing unclear.
      - Report asserts CT Universal MAP fund risk rating 5; [P2] KIID classifies this fund in category 4, not 5. Risk rating mismatch between report assertion and fund category.
      - Report states both funds individually and combined reflect risk rating 5; [P12] HSBC factsheet states its own risk profile as 3; a 50/50 blend of a risk-3 fund and a risk-4 or risk-5 fund does not mechanically yield risk 5 without stated combined methodology.
    The report asserts a target risk rating of 5 for the recommended strategy and both constituent funds. Evidence from fund factsheets and KIIDs reveals material inconsistencies. [P12] is the HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio factsheet, which explicitly states 'The Fund's risk profile is rated as 3' — not 5. [P3] and [P2] are KIIDs for the funds, which classify them on different scales: the HSBC fund is rated 4 on the Risk and Reward Indicator (a 1-7 scale where higher = higher risk), and the CT fund is classified in category 4 (medium fluctuations). These are fund-level risk classifications, not necessarily on the client's 1-5 agreed risk scale used in the recommendation. However, the report explicitly claims each fund has 'riskRating: 5' on the firm's client scale. The guard notes that fund-level ratings from a provider are not necessarily on the firm's client risk scale — but the report itself is asserting them on its own scale, and the factsheet for HSBC contradicts this by stating the fund's own risk profile as 3. The SIPP research report [P10] and [P16] confirm the agreed client risk level is 5 and the portfolio risk mapping is 5, but do not provide the individual fund risk ratings on the firm's scale or a stated methodology for how a 50/50 blend of a fund rated 3 and a fund in a different category on a different scale yield a combined 5. The OCF figures are corroborated: 0.18% for HSBC matches [P11], and 0.29% for CT matches [P11]. Allocation percentages sum to 100% and match [P11]. The fundamental tension is that the report claims both funds individually and combined target risk 5, but the HSBC factsheet itself states its risk profile as 3. This is a data inconsistency between the report's assertion and the fund's own published risk profile. It is material because the check requires the strategy risk rating to equal the agreed rating on a stated scale, and a fund asserted as risk 5 cannot simultaneously be published as risk 3. The KIID classifications for both funds on their respective scales (4 for HSBC on 1-7 indicator; category 4 for CT) do not directly contradict the firm's internal 1-5 scale, but the HSBC factsheet's explicit statement of 'risk profile rated as 3' on a 1-5 scale (where 5 is higher risk) directly contradicts the report's assertion. No combined risk rating methodology is stated or evidenced to explain how these individually-rated funds produce a portfolio target of 5.

  [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that the questionnaire indicated risk rating 4, but the client agreed to rating 5. The override is justified on two grounds: (1) ClientPreference: 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor, you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk with your pension.' (2) GrowthObjective: 'You have confirmed that you are not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period and feel a balanced approach to this investment would achieve this goal.' Client acknowledgement is recorded: 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5.' The report states 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.'
    File:     The Risk profile report (P2, P6, P20) confirms that the natural risk level was determined by the Attitude to Risk Questionnaire completed on 18/09/2025, scored as rating 4 (Cautious Balanced). It states: 'The natural risk level, which is ascertained from the psychometric attitude to risk questionnaire score, is used as a starting point to discuss the actual level of risk you are willing and able to take to meet your specific investment objective.' The report then confirms an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) was reached following discussion. The questionnaire responses (P14) show the client's answers to all questions, consistent with a Cautious Balanced profile. The SIPP research report (P16, P20) confirms 'The agreed risk level considered your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal' with agreed risk level 5. The Suitability Report passage (P4) restates the override verbatim. However, the Risk profile report (P1, P3, P14) contains no narrative notes recording the discussion that led to the override: both 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' sections state 'No additional notes were recorded'. No supporting documentation records the adviser's reasoning, client discussion, or capacity for loss assessment that would justify the upward override from 4 to 5.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts client preference ('You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5') but the Risk profile report records 'No additional notes were recorded' under both 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire', creating an inconsistency between the assertion of a recorded discussion and the absence of any such notes in the risk profile documentation.
      - The suitability report cites a growth objective ('you wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period and feel a balanced approach to this investment would achieve this goal') but the Risk profile report shows no investment term recorded; the risk profile template contains empty fields for investment target notes, investor experience notes, and capacity for loss notes, suggesting the discussion, if it occurred, was not documented.
      - The report's justification relies on client awareness and preference statements, but the capacity for loss questionnaire responses or assessment are not shown in the Risk profile report, which states 'No additional notes were recorded' in that section.
    The comparison can be made. The report and file agree that: (1) questionnaire score was 4; (2) agreed rating became 5; (3) override direction was upward; (4) final agreed rating is 5. The trigger is satisfied: an override from 4 to 5 exists. The problem is not that the override was not justified in the report itself—the suitability report (P4) does provide textual justification and client acknowledgement. The problem is that this justification is not corroborated by the case file evidence. The Risk profile report (P1, P3, P14, P20), which is the standard vehicle for documenting the discussion that leads to an agreed risk rating, explicitly records 'No additional notes were recorded' in three key sections: (1) 'Agreed risk level' discussion notes; (2) 'Investor Experience' notes; (3) 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' notes. This creates a material gap: the suitability report asserts that the client confirmed specific preferences and growth objectives and that the adviser reached a considered view ('We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite'), but the Risk profile report—the document that should record that discussion—contains no such notes. The guard permits this comparison where assessed and agreed ratings match, which they do (assessed 4, agreed 5, override direction upward, final agreed 5). No evidence in category D, G or H records the discussion that produced the override or the reasoning that supported it beyond the client preference statement already in the report itself. The suitability report is internally consistent (it states the override and justification) but that internal statement is not supported by file evidence of the discussion or the adviser's reasoning. This is not a case of a missing override; it is a case of an override asserted in the report with no corroborating discussion notes or adviser reasoning documented in the case file.

  [G3.8] Conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels across the file
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts an Attitude to Risk rating of 4 from the questionnaire (assessedCode: '4') which was overridden upward to an agreed rating of 5 (agreedCode: '5') on the 'Risk Rating Scale' with scaleMin 1 and scaleMax 9. The label is 'Balanced'. The justification states 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire' and 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor'. The client acknowledged 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5.' The description elaborates: 'you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk with your pension' and 'As a Balanced investor, you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk.'
    File:     P1 [D] Risk profile report states the natural risk level questionnaire result as '4 Cautious Balanced' (truncated passage, but clearly indicated as the questionnaire outcome). P1 also describes risk 4: 'As a Cautious Balanced investor you tend to see yourself as quite a cautious person and are inclined to view risk negatively rather than as a source of opportunity.' P1 later states 'By using these projections and discussions, we came to an agreed risk level for your specific investment objective. A portfolio will be built to meet your agreed risk level.' P2 [G] SIPP research report confirms 'Agreed risk level' with 'Following an interactive discussion, and considering your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal, we agreed a risk level.' P2 shows the agreed risk level as '5 Balanced' and states: 'As a Balanced investor you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk.' P6 [G] confirms 'Agreed risk level 5' with identical descriptor. P7 [G] states 'The client's agreed risk level is 5. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' P9 [G] states 'The client's agreed risk level is 5'. P12 [D] Risk profile report confirms 'Agreed risk level' with risk level '5 Balanced' and the same Balanced descriptor. P15 [D] notes 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Capacity for loss for the goal'. P1 questionnaire appendix shows the client's responses: 'I would describe my investment approach as: Cautious', 'I associate the phrase investment risk with the idea of: Caution', 'Compared to other people, I would describe myself as: More cautious', 'In seeking a return on my investment, in the short-term I'm willing to accept: A slight risk of capital loss'. P17 and P18 [H] KIIDs describe a fund rated at risk category 4 on a 1-7 scale with descriptor 'medium level of volatility' and asset allocation 'Equities: 45-70%, Bonds: 20-55%, Other: 0-25%'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts questionnaire result of 4; file confirms this questionnaire outcome as 4 (Cautious Balanced) — this is consistent, not a discrepancy, as the report correctly states the assessment before the override.
      - Report asserts agreed rating of 5 (Balanced); file confirms agreed rating of 5 (Balanced) across multiple passages [P2, P6, P7, P9, P12] — this is consistent.
      - Report descriptor for agreed rating 5 states 'you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk' — this descriptor appears verbatim in [P2, P6, P12] confirming consistency.
      - Report uses risk scale name 'Risk Rating Scale' with scaleMin 1 and scaleMax 9 — [P1] and [P2] reference the same scale system and mapping process without naming it explicitly, but the scale (1-9) is consistent across the profile documents.
      - Report describes questionnaire result 4 as 'Cautious Balanced' in [P1] — the report does not use this label in the canonical model; instead, it extracts '4' with label 'Balanced', which is the agreed label, not the questionnaire label. The questionnaire result should carry descriptor 'Cautious Balanced' per [P1], not 'Balanced'.
      - Questionnaire responses in [P15] show client self-describes as 'Cautious', associates risk with 'Caution', and describes self as 'More cautious' — these responses support a rating lower than the agreed 5 and are consistent with the questionnaire result of 4 or Cautious Balanced.
      - Report states override direction as 'Increased' from 4 to 5 with justification including 'ClientPreference' and 'GrowthObjective' — [P1] and [P2] confirm the interactive discussion process and client confirmation, supporting the override. [P15] shows no additional notes recorded under 'Capacity for loss for the goal' or 'Agreed risk level' sections, meaning the override rationale is recorded in the narrative but not in a dedicated capacity-for-loss assessment field.
      - Report provides justification: 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor' and 'You have confirmed that you are not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period' — [P2] and [P6] confirm agreed risk level 5 was reached following discussion considering 'natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return', but [P12] shows investment period of 2 years, not 10 years, creating an internal inconsistency in the report's own timeline assertions.
      - Report states client acknowledgement 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5' — this appears in the report's own narrative [Page 4, Attitude to Risk section] but is not independently corroborated by a separate signed acknowledgement or client signature in the file evidence provided.
    The report's assertion of an upward override from questionnaire result 4 to agreed rating 5 is supported by consistent evidence across the file. The questionnaire result of 4 is corroborated by [P1], which shows the natural risk level as '4 Cautious Balanced'. The agreed rating of 5 is corroborated by [P2, P6, P7, P9, P12], all consistently stating agreed risk level 5 with the Balanced descriptor. The risk scale (1-9) is used consistently across the report and evidence. The override is explained as reflecting client preference and growth objectives, with justification present in [P1, P2, P6, P12] through the narrative that states 'Following an interactive discussion...we agreed a risk level' and confirming the client's request to increase risk. However, one internal inconsistency within the report's own assertions surfaces: the report justifies the override citing a '10-year period' investment horizon, but [P12] Risk profile report shows the investment period as '2 years'. This suggests either the justification references a different goal or timeline than the one modelled, or the assertion is inaccurate. Additionally, [P15] shows 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Capacity for loss for the goal', which means the capacity-for-loss assessment, while referenced in the override justification, is not explicitly detailed in the questionnaire notes section of the evidence file. The questionnaire responses in [P15] ('Cautious', 'Caution', 'More cautious', 'Slight risk of capital loss') support a lower natural risk tolerance than the agreed 5, which is consistent with a questionnaire result of 4 and confirms the override was necessary to reach the higher agreed level. No conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels are present across the evidence for the agreed rating of 5; all documents use the same descriptor ('Balanced', 'do not see yourself as particularly cautious') and the same scale. The evidence does not contain a separate risk assessment on an incompatible scale that would create a conflict. The report's label 'Balanced' matches the file's label for risk level 5 across [P2, P6, P12]. The override is documented and explained, satisfying the transparency requirement. The 2-year vs. 10-year discrepancy is a finding within the report's own assertions and is not resolved by the file evidence, but it does not constitute a conflicting risk score or label across different documents — it is an internal consistency issue affecting the justification's credibility rather than a direct contradiction on the risk rating itself.

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) Questionnaire indicated risk rating 4, but client agreed to rating 5 after discussion; (2) Capacity for loss is Medium, supported by stable income (State Pension £230/week, HGV driver £300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week, spouse £1,100/month), household expenditure of £1,700/month, savings of £6,000, no liabilities, and client confirmation that underperformance would not impact day-to-day living; (3) Tolerance for loss includes awareness that values rise and fall, understanding returns are not guaranteed, and acknowledgement that higher risk brings larger potential drops; (4) Investment term is 10 years (stated in override justification); (5) Recommended strategy is Risk 5 Balanced portfolio: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, OCF 0.18%, Risk Rating 5) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, OCF 0.29%, Risk Rating 5); (6) Both recommended funds are Risk Rating 5, matching the agreed profile; (7) Strategy rationale includes risk alignment, diversification, active/passive blend, simplicity, competitive charges, strong peer performance, and CIP oversight.
    File:     The evidence corroborates: (1) Risk profile report [P1], [P8] confirms agreed risk level 5 as Balanced investor, stating 'As a Balanced investor you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk' [P8]; (2) Risk profile report [P1] documents the natural risk level as 4 from questionnaire, and agreed level as 5 following discussion of projection and interactive dialogue [P1]; (3) Capacity for loss is supported by multiple passages: income stability [P17], household cashflow comfort [P17], liquid savings [P17], no liabilities [P17], and confirmed non-detriment statement [P17]; (4) Investment term of 2 years is stated in risk profile projections [P8], contradicting the 10-year term stated in the override justification [P17]; (5) Recommended funds are confirmed as HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (50%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund (50%) [P5], [P14], [P18]; (6) SIPP research report [P5] confirms both funds are Risk 5; (7) File notes [P10] show alternative options were considered and rejected: Aviva only has Risk 4 mixed asset (does not meet ATR), Zurich requires blend of 7 and 2 (not recommended due to performance and asset type concerns), Standard Life funds more expensive; (8) Tolerance for loss statements are present in report [P17] matching the canonical assertions; (9) KIID for CT Universal MAP shows category 4 (medium fluctuations) [P22], though risk rating in strategy is stated as 5.
    Discrepancies:
      - Investment term stated as 10 years in override justification (page 4: 'best growth potential over a 10-year period') but risk profile projections show 2-year investment period [P8]
      - CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund KIID category is 4 (medium fluctuations) [P22], but strategy states Risk Rating 5 for this fund [P5], [P18]
    The report's core assertions on ATR, CFL, tolerance and strategy are well-supported by the evidence. The override from rating 4 to 5 is explicitly justified: client preference stated in suitability report [P17], documented in reconciliation as increase toward growth objective, with client acknowledgement 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5' [P17]. The capacity for loss assessment is comprehensive and corroborated across income, savings, expenditure and client confirmation in multiple passages [P17]. Both recommended funds carry Risk Rating 5 in the strategy [P18], [P5], matching the agreed profile. The SIPP research report [P5], [P14] and suitability report [P18] align on fund names, allocations and individual risk ratings. File notes [P10] show adviser considered alternatives against the agreed profile and rejected them on grounds of mismatch or underperformance. The discrepancy on investment term (10 years vs 2 years) is material: the override justification cites a 10-year horizon as supporting the upward risk adjustment, but the risk profile report's projection base is 2 years [P8]. This is a statement about a factor (time horizon) used to justify the rating increase, and the two documents contradict. The KIID categorisation of CT Universal MAP as category 4 versus its Risk Rating 5 in the strategy is a potential tension, though KIID category and internal risk rating may use different methodologies (KIID uses historical price volatility; internal rating scales may use prospective asset allocation). The guards permit this: the boilerplate risk warnings are standard, uncontroversial statements on this client are not gaps but alignment, and the absence of evidence for standard-warning content is not a concern. However, the 10-year vs 2-year discrepancy is a material contradiction regarding the time horizon asserted to support the override and requires clarification as to which applies to the recommendation.

The fund's KIID states it is 'rated 4' in one section [P3] referring to a different Fund. The HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio [P8] shows performance and volatility data for a Risk level 5 fund. The agreed risk profile [P4] confirms agreed risk level 5 for a 'Balanced' investor. The risk profile document [P4] and research report [P10] both confirm agreed risk level 5. The SIPP research report [P9] shows agreed risk level 5 and states 'As a Balanced investor you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person.' File notes [P11] state 'Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds, a blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used to create the below 5' in the context of Zurich's Managed AP (Risk Level 7) and other holdings.     Discrepancies:

      - Report gives no indication whether the People's Pension retained arrangement's fund holdings are suitable given the agreed risk profile of 5.
    The report asserts the People's Pension (EA1) is retained with advice 'Retain this scheme as the workplace pension arrangement with ongoing contributions.' The fund held is 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' with a stated risk rating of 9. The agreed client risk rating is 5 (Balanced investor profile). The evidence in the KIIDs [P1] and product documents shows the Global Investments fund on the KIID scale as Risk and Reward Indicator category 6 (on a 1–7 scale), not 9. Even accepting the provider's own rating of 9 on an unspecified scale (if that were the actual plan rating), no explanation is offered for how a risk 9 fund remains suitable when the client's agreed profile is risk 5. The report provides a rationale for retention (workplace pension with ongoing contributions linked to objective OBJ3), which explains why the arrangement is kept, but does not address whether the funds inside it remain aligned with the agreed risk appetite. The file notes [P11] show active consideration of alternative platforms and fund selections based on risk level alignment for the switch recommendations, but no corresponding assessment for the retained arrangement. The discrepancy between the report's stated risk rating (9) and the KIID evidence (6) compounds the gap: the true risk position of the retained fund is unclear, and either value represents a significant divergence from the agreed rating of 5. No mapping between the provider's scale and the client's agreed 5-point scale is provided. The retention rationale does not cure the absence of an alignment assertion or evidence. This is a material gap in suitability documentation.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). Report describes client as having 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' suitable for Balanced approach (P15); questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' when asked to self-describe investment experience and understanding (P10). Report narrative implies moderate product knowledge and understanding; questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares (P1). Report narrative implies moderate product knowledge and understanding; questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' for Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts (P1). 7 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the client's stated knowledge level is 'Moderate'. The report states: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing' (P15). The narrative describes the client as having 'some experience' and 'a degree of understanding of financial matters'.
    File:     The Risk Profile questionnaire (P1, P10) records the client's assessed responses to direct questions about investment knowledge and experience. On the direct question 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?' the response recorded is 'No understanding / knowledge' (P10). On understanding of stocks and shares, the response is 'No understanding / knowledge' (P1). On understanding of Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts, the response is 'No understanding / knowledge' (P1). On understanding of pension planning and income in retirement, the response is 'No understanding / knowledge' (P1). The questionnaire also records: 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years?' — response 'No' (P1). 'Have you invested in Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts within the last 5 years?' — response 'No' (P1). 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' — response 'No' (P1). The only positive response on investment experience is 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years?' — response 'Yes, by myself' (P1).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report describes client as having 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' suitable for Balanced approach (P15); questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' when asked to self-describe investment experience and understanding (P10)
      - Report narrative implies moderate product knowledge and understanding; questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares (P1)
      - Report narrative implies moderate product knowledge and understanding; questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' for Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts (P1)
      - Report narrative implies moderate product knowledge and understanding; questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' for pension planning and income in retirement (P1)
      - Report states client has 'some experience' of investments; questionnaire records no experience with stocks and shares in last 5 years (P1), no experience with Unit Trusts in last 5 years (P1), no experience with pension products in last 5 years (P1)
      - Report extraction assigns 'Moderate' knowledge level; questionnaire responses support only 'Limited' or 'No' knowledge in relation to complex products
    The questionnaire in category [D] is the contemporaneous risk assessment conducted on 18/09/2025 at the point of advice. It contains direct, scored questions about the client's investment knowledge and experience, not self-deprecating asides but structured responses to defined prompts: 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?' answered 'No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work?' answered 'No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts work?' answered 'No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works?' answered 'No understanding / knowledge'. The client's only relevant investment experience recorded is Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts and Cash ISAs, invested in 'by myself' — deposit accounts and cash ISAs are not complex products. The suitability report (P15, category [I]) then narrates: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing' — using template language that presupposes the client meets those criteria. However, the questionnaire explicitly records that the client has 'No understanding / knowledge' of how stocks and shares work, Unit Trusts work, or pension planning works. The guards state: 'The questionnaire's own wording is the evidence. "No understanding / knowledge" recorded against a direct question is an assessed answer, not a self-deprecating aside, and a report describing the same client as comfortable contradicts it outright.' The report's extraction to 'Moderate' knowledge contradicts the questionnaire record. The questionnaire evidence on file supports 'Limited' or 'No' knowledge of complex products, not 'Moderate'. The client's existing holdings (five pension arrangements) show no evidence of prior active investment decision-making; these are workplace schemes into which the client was enrolled. The file contains no record of any questions asked by the client during the meeting, no evidence of comprehension of the proposed funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced), and no adviser note explaining how the gap between the questionnaire and the report narrative was closed. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability drivers recorded in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment, but the mismatch between the questionnaire record and the report narrative is itself a control gap.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states the client has previously taken out cash savings products (confirmed on page 4, Knowledge & Experience section), has NOT taken out stocks & shares investments by himself, and HAS held group pensions for some time.
    File:     The risk profile questionnaire [P1] shows: (1) Client answered 'Yes, by myself' to 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years?' - corroborating cash savings experience. (2) Client answered 'No' to 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years?' - corroborating no direct stocks & shares experience. (3) Client answered 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' - contradicting the report's claim of holding group pensions 'for some time'. (4) Client indicated 'No understanding / knowledge' to 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?' The fact find [P5, P15] lists five existing pension arrangements (People's Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00), totalling £116,998.47, confirming pension holdings exist in the file. However, the questionnaire response 'No' to pension investment in the last 5 years, combined with 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning, contradicts the narrative of long-held group pensions being material prior experience.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts client 'have held your group pensions for some time' (page 4) but risk questionnaire [P1] shows response 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?'
      - Report asserts group pension experience but risk questionnaire [P1] shows client indicated 'No understanding / knowledge' regarding 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works...'
      - Existing arrangements file includes five pension plans but questionnaire response does not record these as active self-directed investment decisions in the last 5 years
    The report's assertion that the client 'have held your group pensions for some time' requires careful comparison against the questionnaire evidence. The distinction between passive membership in a group pension (where contributions and investments are made by/through the employer) and active self-directed pension investment is material. The guard states 'Holding a workplace pension is passive experience; a report distinguishing it from self-directed investing is being accurate, not inconsistent.' However, the questionnaire does not appear to distinguish passive vs active. The client's response 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' is unqualified. If the existing pensions (particularly People's Pension and Scottish Widows, both with low values and marked as 'paid up' or no longer receiving contributions) are group/workplace pensions, the questionnaire 'No' response may reflect that these are passive holdings and the client did not make active investment decisions. Yet the same questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning. The report then recommends a complex product (Risk Rating 5 balanced funds on Aviva platform) to a client the questionnaire records as having 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and no active pension investment experience in the last 5 years. The file does show five pension arrangements exist, confirming the client has pension holdings, but the evidence of knowledge or active experience to support recommending a new self-directed personal pension is absent or contradicted. The report does not explain how passive group pension membership qualifies the client for self-directed pension investment complexity. This is a material consistency gap: either the group pensions are passive holdings (supporting the questionnaire 'No'), in which case the client's knowledge level and experience do not justify the recommendation; or they are active holdings, in which case the questionnaire response and recorded lack of understanding require explanation.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report describes the recommended Aviva Personal Pension Platform with Flexi Access Drawdown as 'Complex' with complexity drivers including: pension platform with 6,900+ available funds; Flexi Access Drawdown with flexible withdrawal options; tax-efficient withdrawal planning required; pension freedoms and multiple retirement options; death benefit options and inheritance tax considerations; multi-asset fund composition (50/50 blend of two funds); and ongoing adviser monitoring and rebalancing. The report recommends investment in HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (50%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced (50%), both rated Risk Rating 5. The client's financial position includes employment income of £300 per week net as an HGV driver, plus £20 per week Sea Cadets volunteer income. The report states the client agreed to increase Risk rating to 5 from an initial questionnaire result of 4. The report details multiple retirement options available: lump sum, flexi-access drawdown with flexible withdrawals, annuity purchase, or combinations thereof. Death benefits are described as available free of tax before age 75, and taxable at beneficiary's marginal rate after age 75.
    File:     The file evidence confirms the Aviva Pension Portfolio platform offers extensive flexibility: 6,900+ available funds through the platform (Aviva Key Features), flexi-access drawdown with up to 25% tax-free lump sum and flexible withdrawals thereafter, multiple retirement pathway options including drawdown, annuity, or cash lump sum (P1, P20). Fund factsheets (P8, P9) show the recommended funds are mainstream UCITS funds with ongoing charges of 0.29% (CT Universal MAP Balanced) and comparable ongoing charges. HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio is described as seeking 'long term growth consistent with a balanced volatility level' with equity exposure typically 30-70% (P8). CT Universal MAP Balanced similarly aims for long-term growth with balanced volatility (P8). Terms and conditions (P7, P11) describe adviser charges, ongoing investment management, and discretionary investment model options. The Key Features document (P20) states the client 'should retain the services of a financial adviser' and describes withdrawal options. The guard provided states 'A mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not complex; do not escalate on product volume or value alone.' The research report (P16, P19) shows investment selection, risk ratings, and performance data for the recommended funds.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report labels the product as 'Complex' with 9 drivers; file evidence confirms it is a mainstream Aviva Platform offering standard flexi-access drawdown available to all clients, with the two recommended funds being mainstream UCITS multi-asset funds (not bespoke or exotic investments).
      - Report emphasizes '6,900+ available funds' as a complexity driver; file confirms this is inherent to the Aviva Platform but is a feature of the platform itself, not a characteristic making the recommended solution inherently complex for this client.
      - Report describes 'tax-efficient withdrawal planning required' and 'multiple retirement options' as complexity drivers; file confirms these are standard pension freedoms available to all flexi-access drawdown clients since April 2015, not features specific to an unusually complex product.
      - Report recommends multi-asset funds (50/50 HSBC Global Strategy Balanced / CT Universal MAP Balanced); file shows these are both mainstream IA Mixed Investment 40-85% Shares sector funds with standard ongoing charges and no exotic features.
      - Report states 'ongoing adviser monitoring and rebalancing' as complexity driver; file confirms this is standard practice for advised clients on any platform, not evidence of product complexity.
    The requirement is to assess whether the recommended product's complexity is consistent with the client's demonstrated knowledge and experience. The report asserts the product is 'Complex', listing nine drivers. However, the evidence shows the Aviva Platform offering flexi-access drawdown with two mainstream multi-asset funds is not an inherently complex product. The guard explicitly states: 'A mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not complex; do not escalate on product volume or value alone.' The complexity drivers cited in the report conflate features of a flexible pension platform (available to all clients) and standard pension freedoms (statutory since 2015) with product complexity. The recommended funds are both standard UCITS funds in the IA Mixed Investment 40-85% Shares sector, not bespoke or exotic instruments. Flexi-access drawdown itself, while offering flexibility, is a standard retirement income option available to all personal pension holders. The file evidence does not support the characterization of this recommendation as 'Complex' in the technical sense. The distinction is material: a mainstream platform offering standard fund choices is not complex merely because it offers many funds or because the client must receive ongoing advice. The report's rationale for complexity appears to conflate breadth of choice and flexibility with complexity of product features or risk characteristics. No evidence in the file demonstrates that this particular product—a mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds—warrants a 'Complex' label or special justification beyond standard suitability correspondence. The report does not evidence that the client's knowledge and experience were assessed as sufficient for a genuinely complex product, nor does it need to, because the recommended product is not complex. The absence of a formal knowledge/experience assessment grading in the file (noted in the extraction metadata) means that dimension cannot be corroborated; however, the mislabeling of the product itself is a material discrepancy between the report's assertion and the file's evidence of what the product is.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report states the client's knowledge level as 'Moderate' and recommends products of 'Complex' nature. The report asserts: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.' It further states the client 'understand[s] that you may need to take some risk to meet your investment goals' and recommends a multi-asset fund strategy with two funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced), both rated Risk Rating 5. The report claims 'you want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple' and that the funds provide 'a professionally managed, diversified investment solution.' The report does not record any client questions about product features or comprehension checks.
    File:     The Risk Profile Report (category D) records the client's responses to the Knowledge & Experience questionnaire completed 18/09/2025. The client answered 'No understanding / knowledge' to: (1) understanding how stocks and shares work and associated risks; (2) understanding Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts and associated risks; (3) understanding pension planning and income in retirement and associated risks. The client answered 'Yes, by myself' only to investing in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts and Cash ISAs within the last 5 years. The client answered 'No' to all other investment experience questions including Unit Trusts, stocks and shares, Enterprise Investment Schemes, Venture Capital Trusts, and pension planning/income products. The Risk Profile Report notes 'No additional notes were recorded' under Investor Experience section. The questionnaire shows the client's natural risk level was initially 4 (Cautious Balanced), not 5, per the Attitude to Risk questionnaire completed 18/09/2025. The client's capacity for loss is noted as limited: the report states the client 'has no experience in stocks and shares' and experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements.' The Fact Find (category B) does not record any statement of the client's investment knowledge level or experience assessment in narrative form.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states knowledge level as 'Moderate'; questionnaire evidence records 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, EIS/VCT, and pension planning/income in retirement, with experience limited to cash savings only.
      - Report recommends 'Complex' products (multi-asset funds with active/passive blend, derivatives exposure, discretionary rebalancing); client questionnaire shows no understanding of stocks and shares or pension products.
      - Report states client 'want[s] to keep the investment strategy fairly simple'; questionnaire and fact find contain no corroborating evidence of this stated preference.
      - Report asserts client 'understand[s] that you may need to take some risk to meet your investment goals'; questionnaire records no understanding of investment risk, and capacity for loss response notes limited experience limited to workplace pension.
      - Report reproduces Risk Profile descriptor 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach'; questionnaire directly contradicts this precondition by recording no understanding of stocks and shares, EIS/VCT, or pension planning.
      - Natural risk level in questionnaire is 4 (Cautious Balanced), not the agreed/recommended 5 (Balanced); report does not adequately evidence the client's grounds for moving from cautious to balanced, only that the client 'confirmed' the upgrade.
    The comparison can be performed because the questionnaire evidence is present and directly addresses knowledge, understanding and experience. The critical mismatch is between the report's characterisation of the client as 'Moderate' knowledge and its recommendation of 'Complex' products, versus the questionnaire's explicit recording of 'No understanding / knowledge' in three core investment domains (stocks/shares, EIS/VCT, pension planning/income) and investment experience limited to cash savings only. The report reproduces a Risk Profile descriptor that carries a precondition ('if you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters') — this precondition is directly contradicted by the questionnaire answers. The guard states: 'A risk-profile description carries preconditions about the client's experience. Where the report reproduces one whose preconditions the file contradicts, the description is being used as evidence of knowledge it does not establish.' The report's reliance on this descriptor to justify the Balanced approach is therefore unsupported. The client's stated preference to 'keep the investment strategy fairly simple' (cited by the report on page 5) is not evidenced in the questionnaire or fact find. The initial questionnaire result was risk rating 4 (Cautious Balanced); the client 'confirmed' an upgrade to 5 but the questionnaire recording does not explain the client's reasoning or demonstrate comprehension of the higher-risk approach. The report asserts the client understands pension freedoms and drawdown options, but the questionnaire explicitly records 'No understanding / knowledge' of 'pension planning and income in retirement works.' The recommended funds are described in the report as providing 'exposure to a range of asset classes and market sectors' and include derivatives exposure; the factsheets show active management and global asset allocation — this is materially more complex than the client's demonstrated knowledge base supports. No category C evidence (such as client notes recording questions, explanations, or comprehension checks) is present to corroborate understanding of complex product features. The client's upgrade from risk rating 4 to 5 is recorded as agreed but not as understood or reasoned through the client's own knowledge gain.

  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states: 'you feel that you are comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' [P1, page 4, Knowledge & Experience section]. The report recommends a complex Customised Investment Portfolio (CIP) comprising multiple funds across different asset classes managed with active oversight and governance, described as offering 'enhanced oversight, risk management, and adaptability' [P7]. The report also states the client 'confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5– Balanced investor' and understands that 'your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value' [P2, ATR Wording section].
    File:     The Fact Find [P21, category B] shows no recorded investor experience beyond workplace pension arrangements. The Risk Profile Report [P14, category D] records 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Investor Experience' and 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' [P16]. File notes [P2] state: 'although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed.' The file records that the questionnaire indicated risk rating 4, but the client agreed to rating 5 [P2]. There is no documented evidence of client questions, explanations, or comprehension testing regarding the complexity of the CIP structure (multiple funds, active committee oversight, diversification across asset classes, regions and sectors, active rebalancing). The recommendation involves switching from existing single-platform arrangements into a multi-asset, multi-fund strategy with discretionary rebalancing by an investment committee—a material increase in complexity from the client's existing experience with workplace pension lifestyle funds.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts client is 'comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding' but file shows experience 'limited to workplace pension arrangements' with no additional investor experience notes recorded
      - Report recommends complex Customised Investment Portfolio with active committee oversight, diversification across multiple asset classes, regions and sectors, and continuous rebalancing—no evidence in file of client questions or expressed comprehension of these features
      - Questionnaire indicated risk rating 4; client agreed to rating 5; report narrative refers to both positions creating internal inconsistency about the agreed baseline against which to assess appropriateness of product complexity
      - File records no comprehension testing, client questions, or explanation documentation for the CIP approach despite its material complexity and departure from client's existing single-fund, lifestyle-based holdings
    The requirement asks whether the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product based on their questions and comprehension. The comparison method is PresenceOnly with a guard: an absence of recorded questions is not proof the client did not understand—this should be reported as an evidence gap weighted by product complexity.
    
    The suitability report asserts the client has reasonable understanding and is comfortable with investments. However, the file evidence shows the client's actual experience is limited to workplace pension arrangements. The Risk Profile Report records no additional investor experience notes and no capacity for loss discussion notes—both fields show 'No additional notes were recorded' [P14, P16].
    
    The critical issue is product complexity. The CIP is described as having 'a carefully curated selection of funds across different asset classes, regions, and sectors' with 'Active Oversight and Governance' via an investment committee that 'actively monitored and reviewed' the portfolio to ensure it 'remains aligned with market conditions' [P4, P7]. This is materially more complex than the client's existing arrangements, which comprised mostly single-platform or single-fund holdings (Aviva consolidation fund, Standard Life fund, etc.) [extracted from case file arrangements table]. The file notes show the adviser considered why investment pathways were not appropriate, but this reasoning focused on tailoring and flexibility, not on client comprehension or experience [P7].
    
    Crucially, there is no recorded evidence in the file of client questions about the CIP structure, no documented explanation by the adviser of how the investment committee works, no discussion of the discretionary rebalancing mechanism, and no comprehension testing. File notes [P2] record that the client understood 'returns are not guaranteed' and accepted the risk rating increase, but this addresses risk appetite, not comprehension of the complex investment mechanism being recommended.
    
    The guard states that absence of recorded questions is not proof of non-understanding. However, where the product is complex and the stated knowledge level is 'limited to workplace pensions,' the burden shifts: the file should contain positive evidence of comprehension (client explanation, adviser notes of discussion, confirmation of understanding of specific features). The absence of such evidence, combined with the material increase in product complexity beyond the client's demonstrated experience, creates an evidence gap. The report asserts reasonable understanding but provides no citation to file evidence of questions or comprehension testing to support this assertion for this specific complex product.

        "although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed"

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three product experience claims: (1) 'You confirmed that you have previously taken out cash savings products' [P1]; (2) 'but have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' [P1]; (3) 'You have however held your group pensions for some time' [P1]. The canonical model extracts five existing arrangements: People's Pension (OccupationalDC, Global Investments up to 85% shares), Scottish Widows (PersonalPension, SW Pension Portfolio Four and Three CS1), Aviva (PersonalPension, Aviva pension my future focus consolidation), Zurich (PersonalPension, Managed AP 100%), and Standard Life (PersonalPension, SL at retirement universal PP 10 year pension fund).
    File:     The evidence holds five arrangement policy documents. People's Pension [P12] is a workplace master trust offering multiple fund options including Global Investments (60%, 85%, 100% shares), Pre-Retirement, Cash, Ethical, Shariah, and Annuity funds; the client's holding is stated as Global Investments (up to 85% shares) [P12, P21]. Scottish Widows [P13] holds SW Pension Portfolio Three and Four [P13]. Aviva [P2] holds Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2 [P2], described as having a 'Lifestage Investment Approach' (auto-switching) [P2]. Zurich [P3, P8] holds Managed AP (accumulation) [P8]. Standard Life [P5, P14] holds SL at Retirement Universal (PP 10 Year) Pension fund [P14]. The report states the client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself'; however, the existing holdings include multiple equity-exposure funds: People's Pension in Global Investments (up to 85% shares), Scottish Widows in Pension Portfolios, Aviva in a lifestage consolidation fund, and Zurich in Managed AP. These are all stocks and shares investments held by the client, contradicting the statement that the client has not taken out stocks and shares investments.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' but existing arrangements include People's Pension Global Investments (up to 85% shares), Scottish Widows SW Pension Portfolio Three and Four, Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation, Zurich Managed AP, and Standard Life SL at Retirement Universal — all equity-exposure products that constitute stocks and shares investments.
      - Report states client 'held group pensions for some time' and extracts People's Pension as OccupationalDC (occupational pension), but People's Pension [P12, P13] is a workplace master trust section 101 scheme, not a traditional occupational pension scheme. The distinction matters: a master trust is a multi-employer arrangement, not a group pension in the classical sense. The client's tenure in it is not confirmed in the file.
      - Report does not elaborate on the complexity or characteristics of the existing funds held (e.g., that Aviva's holding is in a lifestage/auto-switching consolidation fund, that People's Pension Global Investments offers up to 85% equity exposure, that Standard Life is in a 10-year target date fund). The suitability narrative does not explain whether the client actively selected these funds or inherited them through defaults.
    The guardnotes state: 'A workplace pension the client was auto-enrolled into evidences ownership, not investment experience; do not count it as either without something more' and 'Holding a product is not the same as understanding it. Holdings corroborate experience; they are not proof of knowledge.' The People's Pension is a workplace scheme suggesting auto-enrolment; without evidence that the client actively selected Global Investments (up to 85% shares) over other available funds, the holding evidences exposure but not necessarily active investment experience. However, the report's explicit claim that the client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' is contradicted by the fact of holding five arrangements containing equity-exposure funds. The report may intend to mean the client has not independently selected and opened a stocks and shares ISA or brokerage account (as opposed to pension schemes), but the wording is unqualified and the holdings in the file are pension-based stocks and shares investments. The Scottish Widows arrangement offers a choice of funds [P13] including equity and mixed portfolios; the Aviva holding is in an auto-switching lifestage fund [P2], suggesting passive management rather than active selection. The Standard Life holding is in a targeted retirement fund [P14]. The Zurich holding is 100% in Managed AP [P8]. Taken together, the client's holdings show exposure to stocks and shares through pension arrangements, but little evidence of active selection or independent investment decision-making — which aligns with the report's framing if interpreted charitably. However, the explicit statement 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment' is factually inaccurate as written: the client has taken out (i.e., holds) stocks and shares investments; the question is whether they did so independently or by passive enrolment/default. The report's knowledge narrative asserts limited experience, which the holdings (mostly auto-switched or default funds) could support; but the wording chosen contradicts the documented existence of equity holdings.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension is 'Complex' and lists seven complexity drivers: (1) Pension platform with 6,900+ available funds; (2) Flexi Access Drawdown with flexible withdrawal options; (3) Tax-efficient withdrawal planning required; (4) Pension freedoms and multiple retirement options; (5) Death benefit options and inheritance tax considerations; (6) Multi-asset fund composition (50/50 blend of two funds); (7) Ongoing adviser monitoring and rebalancing. The report provides five rationale statements: (a) client's current workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions, (b) Scottish Widows small pot encashment under small pots rules with £170 net estimate, (c) existing lifestyling funds drift outside agreed risk range, (d) Zurich lacks risk level 5 funds and multi-asset diversification, (e) lifestyling drift repeated. No explicit assertion that the client's knowledge, experience or understanding matches this complexity level is made in the extracted rationale statements.
    File:     File evidence on client knowledge and experience: [P8] states client 'experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and the client 'understand[s] that returns are not guaranteed'. [P22] documents risk profiling: natural risk level from questionnaire was 4; agreed risk level became 5 after discussion. [P14] notes 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Investor Experience' section. [P8] records client is 'a risk rating 5' (Balanced investor descriptor follows: 'you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk. If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach'). File shows client's existing holdings span five pension arrangements but notes (from extraction summary) indicate existing Aviva holding is in 'Aviva pension my future focus consolidation' (a lifestyle/consolidation fund), Scottish Widows and People's Pension in small pots, and others in basic funds. No evidence that client previously managed a complex platform, made active investment decisions across multiple funds, or demonstrated understanding of tax-efficient withdrawal planning, inheritance tax, or pension freedoms mechanics. File notes [P10] record advisor reasoning for recommending Aviva (alternatives rejected) but do not record client articulation of understanding of the product's complexity.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report describes complexity as 'pension platform with 6,900+ available funds' and 'tax-efficient withdrawal planning required'; file records client's experience as 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' with 'no additional notes' on investor experience despite this being the primary complexity driver.
      - Report's complexity drivers include 'Death benefit options and inheritance tax considerations' and 'Pension freedoms and multiple retirement options'; file does not record any discussion, questions or demonstrated client understanding of these features.
      - Report states multi-asset fund composition is a complexity driver (50/50 blend of HSBC Global Strategy Balanced and CT Universal MAP Balanced); file records existing Aviva holding is in 'Aviva pension my future focus consolidation' (a single lifestyle fund), indicating no prior experience with multi-asset blends or active rebalancing.
      - Report lists 'Ongoing adviser monitoring and rebalancing' as complexity driver; file notes do not record client acknowledgement or understanding of ongoing monitoring obligations or rebalancing mechanics.
      - Risk profiling shows natural questionnaire result was 4, upgraded to 5 by agreement [P8], [P22]; report rationale does not address why a client at natural risk level 4 with limited experience is suitable for a complex product at risk level 5, or whether complexity explanation occurred at that discussion point.
    The comparison can be performed because both the report's assertion of complexity and the file's record of client knowledge and experience are present. The discrepancies are material. Report [I] asserts the Aviva Platform recommendation is complex and lists seven drivers, five of which are substantive (tax planning, pension freedoms, death benefits, inheritance tax, multi-asset construction, ongoing rebalancing). These drivers require client understanding of features significantly more sophisticated than workplace pension administration. File evidence [P8] and [P22] document the client's knowledge as 'limited to workplace pension arrangements' with 'no additional notes were recorded' [P14] on investor experience, which is the primary baseline for assessing suitability for complexity. The file does not record: (1) any discussion of tax-efficient withdrawal mechanics; (2) any explanation of pension freedoms or multiple retirement options; (3) any client question or acknowledgement of death benefit or inheritance tax considerations; (4) any prior experience with multi-asset fund selection or rebalancing; (5) any client articulation of understanding the platform's scope or capabilities. The report's rationale focuses on why consolidation is appropriate (employer contributions, lifestyling drift, risk mismatch at Zurich) but does not address whether the client's knowledge level supports the product complexity itself. The file notes [P10] show the adviser's reasoning for selecting Aviva over alternatives (Zurich lacks multi-asset funds, Standard Life charges higher) but this is adviser analysis, not corroboration that the client understands what they are being recommended to. Risk level upgrade from 4 to 5 [P8] reflects client willingness to accept more risk, not necessarily comprehension of product architecture. The distinction matters: a client may agree to higher risk while remaining unable to manage or comprehend a complex platform. Severity is High because the report describes a complex product with substantial operational and tax-planning components, the client's knowledge is explicitly recorded as limited, and no evidence corroborates that the client's understanding has been brought to match the complexity level. This is not merely absence of documentation of a discussion; it is absence of any evidence the complexity was explained or understood, coupled with explicit limitation of experience in the file.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). Report asserts affordability based on monthly surplus; Fact Find [P1] shows monthly disposable income of -£288.00 (deficit), not surplus. Report states household expenditure approximately £1,700 per month; Fact Find [P1] shows calculated expenditure of £1,718.00 (£18 difference, but in context of reported deficit). Report asserts income comfortably meets expenditure; Fact Find shows expenditure exceeds income by £288 per month without additional adjustments. 28 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Initial advice fee of 3% of amount invested = £3,305.55 with current transfer values. Ongoing annual review charge of 1% per annum based on current values & expected contributions, minus initial fee = circa £1,101.85 for first year. Basis of affordability: combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; client holds savings of around £6,000; no outstanding liabilities; any potential underperformance would not have detrimental impact on day-to-day living.
    File:     Fact Find [P1] shows: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00; Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00; Total Monthly Disposable Income £-288.00 (deficit). Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure £1,718.00. Savings of £6,000 documented [P1]. intelliflo planning scenarios [P2], [P5], [P8], [P9], [P11] show income sources: CDH Recruitment £15,600 p/a (£300 weekly), Sea Cadets £1,040 p/a (£20 weekly) until 2027, State Pension £11,973 p/a from age 66. Proposed scenario [P5] shows liquid assets rising from £6,000 (savings) + £0 (surplus account) in 2025 to £159,934 by 2045. One-year projection at [P2] shows annual in-year surplus of £21,064–£21,195 from 2045–2055, then deficits emerging (£2,930 in 2056). Suitability report [P7], [P22] confirms fees: initial £3,305.55, ongoing 1% per annum £1,101.85 first year.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts affordability based on monthly surplus; Fact Find [P1] shows monthly disposable income of -£288.00 (deficit), not surplus.
      - Report states household expenditure approximately £1,700 per month; Fact Find [P1] shows calculated expenditure of £1,718.00 (£18 difference, but in context of reported deficit).
      - Report asserts income comfortably meets expenditure; Fact Find shows expenditure exceeds income by £288 per month without additional adjustments.
    The report claims affordability is supported by income comfortably meeting expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month with £6,000 savings. The Fact Find [P1] corroborates the £6,000 savings figure and shows monthly expenditure of £1,718 (£18 more than the report's approximate £1,700). However, the Fact Find also shows Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00 against Total Monthly Expenditure of £1,718.00, yielding a Total Monthly Disposable Income of -£288.00, a deficit rather than a surplus. The report's assertion that income 'comfortably meets' expenditure directly contradicts the Fact Find's calculated monthly deficit. The initial fee of £3,305.55 is a one-off transfer cost and per the guard does not test against monthly income. The ongoing fee of £1,101.85 per annum (approximately £92 per month) would further worsen the monthly deficit, which already stands at £288. The intelliflo planning scenarios [P2], [P5] project forward from the current position and show that the recommendation results in positive in-year surplus figures (£21,064–£21,195 annually from 2045 onwards), but these projections rely on pension crystallisation and drawdown strategies not yet active. The critical issue is that the report's assertion of affordability based on current monthly income and expenditure is contradicted by the Fact Find, which documents a current monthly cash deficit. The absence of any discussion of how this current monthly deficit is being managed (or will be managed if new commitments are added) represents unsupported affordability reasoning. A guard explains that fees taken from the fund reduce investment, not client income, so the ongoing fee should not be tested against the monthly deficit directly. However, the initial £3,305.55 is paid 'directly by the pension schemes on transfer' per the report [P7], reducing transfer value, and the report offers no evidence that the client can afford this from within the transfer proceeds rather than additional cash outlay. No evidence shows income will increase or expenditure decrease to create the surplus on which affordability rests.
