  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) Current financial position: total net monthly income £4,386.67 (HGV £1,300 + Sea Cadets £86.67 + spouse employment £1,100 + State Pension £1,000), monthly expenditure £1,700, monthly surplus £1,786.67; (2) Assets: £6,000 liquid, £0 liabilities, net worth £6,000; (3) Projected pensions total £116,998.47; (4) Cashflow analysis: baseline to age 100 with no shortfall; high inflation stress test (6%) shows no shortfall; combined market crash (-30%) and inflation stress test shows no shortfall; (5) State Pension covers bills in retirement, allowing pension to grow; (6) Critical yield required 4.8% per year to age 75 under mid-growth scenario; (7) Life expectancy: male age 86, female age 88, funds expected to last lifetime; (8) Assumption: 0% of surplus income saved, all surplus spent; expenditure £1,700 per month in retirement; assumptions include 2% general expenditure inflation, 2.5% state pension inflation, 5% Aviva pension inflation.
    File:     Fact Find shows: (1) Total net monthly income £1,430.00 (Employment CDH Recruitment £1,200, State Pension £230); total monthly expenditure £1,718.00; monthly disposable income £-288.00 (deficit); (2) Calculated household expenditure table shows £1,718.00 total; (3) Intelliflo planning baseline scenario shows: opening liquid assets £6,000, defined contribution pensions total £116,997 (Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002, Peoples Pension £6,601, Scottish Widows £211); (4) Intelliflo baseline and proposed scenarios both show no year-end shortfall through to age 100, with positive surplus each year; (5) Proposed scenario shows higher pension values growing to £110,185 (Aviva transfer); (6) First year income statement shows State Pension £11,973 + Employment £16,640 + spouse £13,200 = £41,813 combined; tax & NI £3,385, total regular expenditure £24,001; (7) Assumptions state 0% surplus saved, 2% general inflation, 2.5% state pension inflation.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states total net monthly income of £4,386.67; Fact Find states total net monthly income of £1,430.00 — variance of £2,956.67 (207% difference)
      - Report states current monthly surplus of £1,786.67; Fact Find states monthly disposable income of £-288.00 (a deficit) — contradictory positions
      - Report states HGV driver income £1,300 per month; Fact Find shows employment income £1,200 per month — variance of £100 (8.3%)
      - Report states Sea Cadets income £86.67 per month; no corroborating figure in Fact Find; Intelliflo first year statement shows employment £16,640 (which converts to ~£1,387 per month for client, not including spouse); this does not reconcile with a £86.67 figure
      - Report states current State Pension £1,000 per month (£12,000 per annum); Fact Find shows £230 per month current; Intelliflo shows £11,973 per annum (£997.75 per month) — report figure inconsistent with evidence; future State Pension date 2027-08-15 stated but Fact Find and Intelliflo show continuous projection from 2025 without discrete income step
      - Report states monthly expenditure £1,700; Fact Find shows £1,718.00 — variance of £18 (1.05%) — minor but present
      - Report income and expenditure figures do not reconcile with Intelliflo cashflow first-year statement: reported £4,386.67 income minus £1,700 expenditure = £2,686.67 monthly surplus, yet Intelliflo shows £41,813 annual income (£3,484.42 monthly average for both) minus tax/NI and £24,001 expenditure = surplus ~£14,812 annually (~£1,234 monthly), a different pattern
      - Report assumes 0% of surplus saved and all surplus spent; this assumption is confirmed in both Intelliflo scenarios and Fact Find, so no inconsistency here, but the surplus amount itself is inconsistent between report assertion and modelling output
    The comparison can be performed because the key inputs are present across the documents, though they tell conflicting stories. (1) Income discrepancy: The report's total monthly income of £4,386.67 does not match the Fact Find's stated current net income of £1,430.00. The report derives this figure from HGV £1,300 + Sea Cadets £86.67 + spouse employment £1,100 + State Pension £1,000. The Fact Find, however, shows only employment income (£1,200 from CDH Recruitment) and State Pension (£230 monthly), totalling £1,430 current. The Intelliflo baseline scenario's first year shows combined income of £41,813 (State Pension £11,973 + Employment £16,640 + spouse £13,200), which is ~£3,484.42 monthly on average. This differs from the report's stated £4,386.67. The report notes on page 3 that C1 HGV is £1,300 and includes a future State Pension from 2027-08-15, but the Fact Find records £230 current state pension for the client (not £1,000) with £1,100 spouse employment. The Sea Cadets £86.67 monthly figure has no corroborating entry in the Fact Find or Intelliflo statements. The income reconciliation fails. (2) State Pension timing: The report projects State Pension to increase from £1,000 to £2,000 per month (or adds a second £1,000) from 2027-08-15 when spouse reaches age 67, but Fact Find shows spouse State Pension of £230 current (age 65), and neither document clarifies whether the report's £1,000 per client and £1,000 per spouse are correct. Intelliflo shows £11,973 per annum for both, equating to ~£1,000 per month each at different points. (3) Expenditure: Report £1,700 vs Fact Find £1,718 — a 1.05% variance within tolerance. (4) Cashflow outcome: Despite the income input inconsistency, both the report and Intelliflo cashflow models project no shortfall to age 100. This suggests the modelling tool (Intelliflo) was run with assumptions different from those the report explicitly states, or the report has restated the Intelliflo input rather than deriving it fresh. The report's claim that 'there are no issues with your cash flow forecast' is supported by Intelliflo's baseline and proposed scenarios, both showing zero required withdrawals from pensions/investments due to shortfall in all years shown through age 100. The stress tests (6% inflation, -30% market crash) also show no shortfall in the Intelliflo tool. However, the income figures driving these outcomes do not match what the report states as inputs. (5) Assets and liabilities: Report £6,000 liquid, £0 liabilities. Intelliflo baseline and proposed scenarios confirm £6,000 opening liquid assets (Savings account) and £0 liabilities. This is consistent. Defined contribution pensions total £116,998 in report; Intelliflo shows £116,997 (rounding). Consistent. (6) Critical yield: Report states 4.8% required for mid-growth scenario to age 75. [P1] corroborates this in the table, matching the mid-growth row. This is supported. (7) Life expectancy: Report cites ONS life expectancy age 86 (male) and 88 (female); [P8] corroborates both. (8) Vulnerability overlay: The Fact Find records the client as a 'Tenant - private' with no mention of vulnerability drivers, support needs, or communication requirements. No vulnerability markers are identified that would materially affect the affordability assessment. The client's age (69) and expected retirement within 2 years are noted, but no specific vulnerability is disclosed that would alter the cashflow resilience conclusion. The core issue is: the report asserts income totals that do not reconcile with the Fact Find, yet the Intelliflo cashflow model (which is cited to support the no-shortfall conclusion) shows different income inputs. The report's affordability assertion rests on cashflow modelling, but the explicitly stated income inputs do not match what the Fact Find records or what Intelliflo appears to have modelled. This is a data inconsistency and weak justification — the report has not clearly shown that its stated figures were the inputs actually used in the cashflow analysis. The guard 'Charts are not reliably parsable' does not apply here because the issue is not chart interpretation but mismatch between stated inputs and documented inputs.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts an emergency fund of £6,000 held in cash savings and states this is confirmed as sufficient. The client is quoted as having 'confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies' (page 3). The report also states 'You are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' (page 8). Planned access to pension funds is described as taking 10-15 working days via flexi access drawdown. The report infers that £6,000 funds emergency needs and that liquid assets & accessible pensions support foreseeable liquidity needs shown in the baseline scenario (with liquid assets remaining around £5,700-£6,000 in early years, declining slightly) and proposed scenario (similar trajectory).
    File:     The Fact Find (P6, P14) records £6,000.00 in cash savings as at 22/09/2025. Monthly expenditure is documented at £1,718.00 (P14, P18), with net monthly income of £1,430.00, creating a monthly deficit of £288.00 (P14). The intelliflo baseline scenario (P5, P16, P22) projects liquid assets declining from £6,000 to around £5,912 by 2027, £5,869 by 2028, continuing to decline to approximately £5,573 by 2035. The proposed scenario (P7) shows liquid assets at £6,000 initially (2025), declining to £5,956 by 2026, £5,912 by 2027, £5,869 by 2028, and £5,825 by 2029. Both scenarios show persistent minor annual declines. The income statement in P23 shows total regular income £41,813 (employment £29,840, state pension and other £11,973 equivalent) and regular expenditure £24,001, leaving an annual surplus. However, the Fact Find (P14) shows current monthly net income £1,430 against monthly expenditure £1,718, a shortfall of £288 per month or £3,456 annually.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states £6,000 is 'sufficient' for emergency needs but provides no quantified requirement figure (e.g., months of expenses, specific emergency scenarios) against which to measure sufficiency. The only context is the single cash amount held, not a stated target.
      - Monthly cashflow in Fact Find (P14) shows deficit of £288 per month (net income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), yet intelliflo scenarios (P5, P7, P16) show annual surpluses with liquid assets remaining stable or declining only marginally. The reconciliation between current monthly deficit and projected annual surpluses is not explained.
      - Emergency fund stated as £6,000 on page 3 of the report matches the Fact Find figure of £6,000.00 (P6, P14), corroborated. However, no documentation in the file states what level of emergency reserve is 'sufficient' or how £6,000 was determined to be adequate.
      - The report infers £6,000 is sufficient based on client confirmation ('you have confirmed'), but the Fact Find records no evidence of a formal emergency fund requirement assessment or benchmark (e.g., 3-6 months expenses, which would be £5,154-£10,308 at £1,718/month).
      - Projected decline in liquid assets across both scenarios (baseline and proposed) is inconsistent with an emergency fund strategy that maintains a fixed reserve. In baseline scenario (P16), liquid assets fall from £6,000 (2025) to £5,573 (2035), a 7% erosion. In proposed scenario (P7), similar pattern occurs. Neither scenario documents repletion of the emergency fund or states how this gradual drawdown is compatible with maintaining emergency resilience.
      - Report states planned pension access takes '10-15 working days' (page 8, P5 reference to 'Pension fund access if needed in future'), but the client currently has a monthly shortfall (P14), meaning emergency access to pension via drawdown may be necessary sooner than 10-15 days allow in an actual crisis.
    The report asserts that the £6,000 emergency fund is sufficient and that the client has confirmed this. The figure itself (£6,000) is corroborated by the Fact Find (P6, P14). However, the sufficiency claim rests entirely on client assertion without a quantified benchmark or requirement. A guard applies: 'An asserted sufficiency with no quantified requirement cannot be verified — record it as unquantified rather than as a contradiction.' The report does not state what makes £6,000 sufficient (e.g., three months of living expenses, unexpected costs, loss of income buffer). At current monthly expenditure of £1,718 (P18), £6,000 represents 3.5 months of total household spend, or approximately 4.8 months if only essential expenses are £1,260 (rent £600 + council tax £200 + utilities £180 + food £300 + insurance £25 per P18). This sits within common industry benchmarks of 3-6 months. However, this reasoning is inferential and not documented in the file. A material inconsistency emerges between the Fact Find's current monthly shortfall (£1,430 income vs £1,718 expenditure, deficit of £288 per month) and the intelliflo scenarios (P5, P7, P16, P23) which project annual surpluses. The Fact Find shows no expected income changes and no expected expenditure changes (P14), yet the scenarios incorporate income changes (State Pension from age 66, employment income to 2027 for one client and 2026 for the other per P3) and show improved cash flow after those events. The file does not explain how the current monthly deficit is addressed in the interim, nor does it document any interim contribution plan or drawdown from liquid assets to bridge this gap before retirement income activates. The projected gradual decline in liquid assets across both scenarios (from £6,000 to around £5,600-£5,800 over 10 years per P7, P16) suggests ongoing minor depletion, inconsistent with an emergency fund model that maintains a fixed reserve. The report does not address this trajectory or explain whether the declining liquid asset position remains acceptable for emergency access. The 10-15 day access timeframe for pension drawdown (P5 reference) is reasonable for planned access but may be inadequate in a genuine emergency if liquid cash is already depleted or committed. The file does not document whether the client has other liquid resources beyond the £6,000 cash savings (e.g., credit facilities, family support) or whether the pension portfolio itself permits rapid partial withdrawal. The report's conclusion that affordability is supported by emergency resilience is therefore incomplete: it does not quantify what 'sufficient' means, does not reconcile the current monthly deficit with projected scenarios, and does not address the long-term adequacy of a gradually depleting £6,000 reserve. The claim that the client has 'confirmed' sufficiency (page 3, cited from P6 context) is a client statement, not evidence of objective sufficiency, and does not substitute for an adviser assessment against a documented standard.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts initial commitment of £3,305.55 (3% of transfer values) and ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 per annum (1% of values after deducting initial fee). It states affordability is supported by: (1) combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; (2) client holds savings of around £6,000; (3) no outstanding liabilities; (4) any potential underperformance would not have detrimental impact on day-to-day living. Report derives monthly surplus of £1,786.67 from net income £3,486.67 minus expenditure £1,700.
    File:     Fact Find on page 9-10 shows: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00, Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00, yielding Total Monthly Disposable Income of -£288.00 (a deficit). Fact Find details show Basic Employment Income (CDH Recruitment) £1,200 monthly and State Pension £230 monthly = £1,430 total. Detailed expenditure itemisation totals £1,718 monthly (Rent £600, Council Tax £200, Electricity £120, Water £60, Food & Personal Care £300, Car/Travelling £150, Building Insurance £25, Entertainment £100, plus other items). intelliflo Baseline scenario (page 11) shows Total Regular Income £41,813 annually and Total Regular Expenditure £24,001 annually. Scottish Widows small pot encashment evidences net receipt of £170 from fund value £211.32. Savings stated as £6,000 in Fact Find, corroborated by intelliflo proposed scenario showing £6,000 liquid assets.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts monthly surplus of £1,786.67 (income £3,486.67 minus expenditure £1,700); Fact Find evidences monthly deficit of -£288.00 (income £1,430.00 minus expenditure £1,718.00). These figures are irreconcilable.
      - Report cites combined income supporting expenditure of £1,700; Fact Find shows combined monthly income of only £1,430, which is £270 below the stated expenditure.
      - Report derived monthly surplus of £1,786.67 is unexplained in the evidence; no source document supports either the £3,486.67 income or the £1,786.67 surplus figure.
      - Report states ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 per annum; intelliflo Baseline shows Total Regular Expenditure £24,001 annually, and figure of £1,101.85 appears nowhere in the evidence and cannot be derived from stated figures.
    The comparison can be performed because both the report and the Fact Find provide explicit monthly cashflow figures. However, they contradict fundamentally. The Fact Find Fact Find on page 10 states: 'Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00' and 'Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00', yielding 'Total Monthly Disposable Income £-288.00'. This is a monthly deficit, not surplus. The report asserts in the affordability section on page 4: 'Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month' and derives a monthly surplus of £1,786.67. The report's net income figure of £3,486.67 appears nowhere in the evidence. The Fact Find income itemisation is clear: Basic Employment Income £1,200 + State Pension £230 = £1,430 monthly. The Fact Find expenditure is detailed line-by-line totalling £1,718. The disconnect is material: the report asserts affordability based on substantial surplus where the evidence shows substantial deficit. The guard 'Employment income due to cease within the term should be excluded from the surplus used for a long-term commitment' does not apply here, as the issue is not future cessation but present mismatch. The report's basis for affordability—that income comfortably meets expenditure—is directly contradicted by the Fact Find, which shows income falls short of expenditure by £288 monthly. The initial commitment of £3,305.55 could be drawn from the £6,000 savings (confirmed in evidence), but the ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 per annum cannot be afforded from a monthly deficit of £288. The report offers no explanation for the divergence between its asserted income/surplus and the Fact Find figures. The intelliflo planning scenarios (Baseline and Proposed) show annual figures (£41,813 income, £24,001 expenditure) which, when divided by 12, yield approximately £3,485 monthly income and £2,000 monthly expenditure—closer to the report's assertion than to the Fact Find's £1,430 income, but the Fact Find is the contemporaneous income and expenditure fact-find, not a planning projection. This is a material data inconsistency that undermines the affordability conclusion.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that liquidityAndAccessNeeds are to be funded from 'Cash savings' and 'Pension drawdown (Flexi Access Drawdown)'. The report does not explicitly state an emergency fund shortfall figure. The proposed scenario shows an opening liquid assets position of £6,000 in savings (Cash Account) at 2025, with a £0 surplus account. The report projects in-year surpluses across the entire planning horizon (2025-2056+), with annual in-year surplus/deficit figures all shown as £0 after savings policy application, meaning surplus income is not retained (0% surplus income percentage saved per assumptions). Liquid assets remain at approximately £5,900-£6,000 through most of the plan. The report presents the recommendation as affording the clients' lifestyle with adequate liquidity.
    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Opening liquid assets of £6,000 in savings (Cash Account) with £0 in surplus account (P9, P10, P13). (2) Baseline scenario projects liquid assets declining from £5,912 (2027) to £5,573 (2035) and stabilising around £5,600 thereafter through 2038 (P4, P6). (3) Proposed scenario shows identical opening position of £6,000 liquid assets, declining to £5,956 (2026), £5,912 (2027), £5,869 (2028), £5,825 (2029) and thereafter (P10, P12). (4) Both scenarios show total in-year surplus/deficit of £0 throughout, with 0% surplus income percentage saved (P1, P8). (5) Savings are listed as 'Cash Account' with 1.25% expected return p/a (P1). (6) Income sources: CDH Recruitment £15,600 p/a (£300 weekly net) until 2027, Sea Cadets £1,040 p/a (£20 weekly), and for Kim £13,200 p/a (£1,100 monthly) until 2026; State Pension £11,973 each (inflated at 2.5%) from age 66 (P1, P5). (7) Total regular expenses listed as £20,616 p/a including £15,660 essential household and living (P10). (8) The liquidation order in the scenario prioritises: Surplus Account 1st (£0), Savings 2nd (£6,000), then pension drawdown (P1). (9) No explicit emergency fund quantification or sufficiency assessment is present in the evidence.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts liquidityAndAccessNeeds are funded from cash savings and pension drawdown (Flexi Access Drawdown), but does not quantify the emergency fund or state a shortfall/sufficiency finding.
      - The evidence shows liquid assets declining from £6,000 opening to approximately £5,825-£5,912 by 2028-2029 and remaining broadly stable thereafter, but the report does not explain whether this £5,825-£6,000 band is adequate as an emergency reserve or analyse its sufficiency against foreseeable needs.
      - The liquidation order shows savings (£6,000) as 2nd priority after the surplus account (which carries £0), meaning the full £6,000 savings balance is available to cover any in-year deficit, but the report does not state that it is doing so or assess the consequence for emergency resilience if deficits arise.
      - The report states 0% of any in-year surplus will be saved within the plan, and all scenarios show £0 in-year surplus/deficit after this policy is applied, meaning no additional emergency reserves are being built up over time from surplus income.
    The comparison can be made: the evidence contains income, expenditure, cashflow projections, asset positions, and the liquidation order. The guard 'A transfer between pension wrappers does not deplete an emergency fund — only new money from cash does' does not apply here; the guard 'A cashflow that schedules the emergency fund to be spent has not retained it' is the relevant test. Examination of the liquidation order in P1 shows: Surplus Account (£0) is liquidated first, then Savings (£6,000) is liquidated second, then pensions. The cashflow projections (P3, P12) show 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' figures consistently at £0 throughout the plan, meaning no deficits are being drawn against the liquid assets in the projections. The liquid assets themselves remain stable at approximately £6,000 opening, declining modestly to £5,825-£5,912 by 2028-2029 in the proposed scenario, then stabilising. This decline is minimal and does not indicate that the savings account is being liquidated year-on-year to cover deficits; rather, it reflects modest investment returns or rounding. However, the report is silent on: (1) whether the £5,825-£6,000 liquid assets balance is sufficient as an emergency fund; (2) what the emergency fund requirement is in this case; (3) whether the clients' support needs, vulnerability, or life stage (ages 69/65, with employment ending 2026/2027) create specific emergency fund expectations; (4) what 'adequate liquid assets' means in context. The extraction metadata notes that `/financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall` is absent from the canonical model, indicating the report does not state an emergency fund shortfall. The evidence does not contradict an assertion that the recommendation is affordable, but the report does not affirmatively support affordability from a financial resilience and liquidity perspective by quantifying the emergency fund and assessing it against the clients' risk profile, age, support needs, and foreseeable shocks (e.g., health events listed at age 100 in P13, though long-dated). The vulnerability overlay asks whether actual or potential drivers of vulnerability identified in the evidence materially affect the suitability assessment. The evidence does not flag specific vulnerability markers (e.g., cognitive impairment, support needs, communication requirements) in the passages provided; P13 marks 'Illness (John)' and 'Illness (Kim)' at age 100/96 in 2060/2056, but these are future events, not current vulnerability drivers. Absent evidence of current vulnerability affecting this requirement, the overlay does not reframe the finding. The core issue is: the report asserts that liquidity needs are met via 'Cash savings' and 'Pension drawdown', but does not quantify or justify the £6,000 savings balance as adequate for emergency purposes, nor does it assess how long this balance would last if major unexpected costs arose or if income was disrupted. The affordability claim is therefore weakly justified on the liquidity and financial resilience dimension.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report recommends a Personal Pension wrapper with a selected retirement age of 69. It asserts that the client can take up to 25% of the pension fund as a tax-free lump sum at outset, and thereafter can take regular or ad-hoc income withdrawals to suit their needs and circumstances with no upper limit, with each withdrawal taxed at marginal rate. There is no upper limit to the amount the individual can withdraw as an income.' The recommendation does not specify quantified initial contributions, ongoing contribution commitments, emergency fund provisions, or a detailed cashflow assessment tied to evidenced income and expenditure. The report does not articulate the client's specific access needs or match withdrawal timing to those needs.
    File:     The evidence pack contains financial planning scenarios (baseline and proposed) showing total assets of £122,997 comprising £6,000 liquid savings and £116,997 in pensions. Once you reach the normal minimum pension age, you can choose to use all or part of your pension pot to provide an income, either through income drawdown or by buying a lifetime annuity.   [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report identifies three foreseeable changes: (1) Client retirement expected 2027-10-14 with no stated financial impact, as employment income will cease but state pension and pension drawdown will replace it; (2) Spouse (Kim) retirement expected 2027-08-15 with a financial impact of -£1,100 per month net (loss of Kim's employment income of £1,100 per month, offset by her State Pension income of £230 per week approximately £1,000 per month); (3) Inheritance tax changes from 6 April 2027. The report asserts an emergency fund of £6,000 funded from cash savings for unexpected emergencies. Planned pension fund access is described as available through Flexi Access Drawdown, typically taking 10-15 working days. The fact find indicates the client needs a household income of £1,700 net per month at retirement. Current monthly income is £1,430 net and current monthly expenditure is £1,718, resulting in a monthly deficit of £288. The report does not expect changes in expenditure in the foreseeable future per the fact find (page marked 'No' for expenditure changes).
    File:     The intelliflo planning scenario documents confirm the planned retirement events: Kim retires at age 66 in 2026 (event shown on timeline at 2026 age 70/66), and John retires at age 71 in 2027 (event shown on timeline at 2027 age 71/67). The planning documents show opening liquid assets of £6,000 in savings [P6, P12] and accessible pensions of £116,997 [P12]. The baseline and proposed scenario cashflow analyses [P6, P7] show projected liquid assets and accessible pensions throughout the retirement period, declining modestly from 2025 onwards. The proposed scenario projects liquid assets declining from £6,000 in 2025 to approximately £5,177 in 2045 [P2], suggesting draws are being made. The fact find [P14] shows state pension income of £230 per month for the client, with Kim's state pension noted separately. The fact find states 'Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future? No' [P1]. Total monthly household expenditure is calculated at £1,718.00 against net income of £1,430.00, leaving a deficit of £288 [P1]. The planning scenarios include custom events 'Illness (John)' and 'Illness (Kim)' at age 100 in 2060, and 'Death (John)' and 'Death (Kim)' at age 100 in 2060 [P12], but no planned major expenditure events are documented between retirement and these late-life events. The planning documents reference 'Required Withdrawal (Pensions)' and 'Required Withdrawal (Investments)' mechanics where shortfalls occur [P10], indicating the model anticipates draws when needed.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts that the client 'needs a household income of £1,700 net per month' [P17, retirement planning details], but the current monthly deficit is £288 (income £1,430 minus expenditure £1,718) [P1]. This means current expenditure already exceeds the stated retirement need by £18 per month, creating an internal inconsistency in the affordability assertion.
      - The report states no changes are expected in expenditure in the foreseeable future [P1], yet the fact find records a retirement planning need of £1,700 net per month [P17]. If expenditure is currently £1,718 and is not expected to change, the retirement need cannot be justified at £1,700 without accounting for the £18 reduction or the source of that reduction.
      - The spouse retirement financial impact is stated as -£1,100 per month net in the canonical model (loss of Kim's employment income), offset by £230 per week State Pension (approximately £1,000 per month), yielding a net monthly loss of approximately £100. However, the fact find does not explicitly record Kim's current employment income in the income section, only a State Pension of £230 per month is shown [P14], creating ambiguity about whether Kim's employment income of £1,100 is already included in the current household total or will appear at retirement.
      - The emergency fund is asserted as £6,000 in the report, and the fact find confirms £6,000 in cash savings [P16]. However, the report does not quantify or address whether £6,000 is sufficient for the identified emergency need, nor does it state how this will be replenished or protected during the transition to retirement and drawdown.
      - The planning scenarios project liquid assets of approximately £5,177 by 2045 [P2], representing a modest decline from £6,000 over 20 years. This suggests minimal or zero real net drawing on emergency reserves, yet the cashflow analysis references 'Required Withdrawal (Investments)' mechanics [P10], implying draws are anticipated when shortfalls occur. No evidence is provided of the quantum or timing of such draws, nor confirmation that the emergency fund will remain accessible and adequate throughout retirement.
    The core issue is affordability consistency. The fact find records a current household deficit of £288 per month (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), yet asserts no expected changes in expenditure and states a retirement planning need of £1,700 per month net. These three figures are internally inconsistent. If expenditure remains at £1,718 and does not change, the retirement target of £1,700 cannot be met by current projected income alone. The planning scenarios show retirement income will include state pensions (client £230/month currently, Kim £230/month at retirement) plus pension drawdown. The intelliflo models project this can be sustained via accessible pensions declining modestly, but the fact find provides no evidence of the income level at retirement or reconciliation of the current deficit with the retirement target. The spouse's retirement impact is recorded as -£1,100 net in the report, but Kim's current employment income is not visible in the fact find income table [P14], only her future state pension is recorded. This creates ambiguity: if Kim is not currently shown as earning £1,100, then the retirement loss cannot be quantified from the evidence. The emergency fund of £6,000 is present in both report and fact find, but there is no assessment of adequacy, no statement of the identified emergency need quantum, and no evidence of how draws will be managed or replaced. The planning scenarios show liquid assets declining from £6,000 to approximately £5,177 over 20 years, which is consistent with minimal draws, but the fact find records a current deficit of £288 per month. If this deficit exists now, it must be funded either from savings, from pension drawdown before retirement, or from other unrecorded sources. None of these are addressed. The guard permits an undated plan to be sufficient, and the retirement events are dated (2026-2027). However, the current-state affordability crisis (existing monthly deficit) is not addressed in the report, and the bridge from current deficit to retirement surplus is not evidenced. The report's conclusion that the recommendation is affordable rests on planning scenarios that project forward from retirement onwards, but does not address how affordability is achieved up to that point or reconcile the current deficit with the retirement target.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 9 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.7, G6.8, G6.9). Rationale 3 (Aviva lifestyling/risk mismatch) and Rationale 5 (Standard Life lifestyling/risk mismatch) are stated identically verbatim in the report, yet both are marked isClientSpecific=true. The extraction confirms both are identical. If both rationales are genuinely client-specific and distinct, they should not be word-for-word duplicates; if they are boilerplate applied to two different plans, the isClientSpecific flag is incorrect. The report links all five recommendations to objectives OBJ2 and/or OBJ3, but the case file does not define what OBJ2 or OBJ3 are. Without knowing the stated objectives, the link between rationale and objective cannot be verified as client-specific. Rationale 4 (Zurich) states 'Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds; existing blend of risk level 7 and risk level 2 funds lacks proper multi-asset diversification.' The evidence (P4, Policy Research) shows Zurich holding is described as having 'Expensive, no adviser servicing' but does not reproduce the detail about risk levels 7 and 2 or the absence of risk level 5 funds. The Zurich rationale is not corroborated in the retrieved passages. 16 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts five recommendations with stated rationales: (1) Retain People's Pension workplace scheme—'This is your current workplace scheme, and you and your employer are still contributing to this and will continue to until your retirement. The report shows fund value £211.32, and the calculation (25% tax-free, 75% taxed) results in a net figure, but no passage explains the tax treatment applied or confirms the £170 figure is correct. The basis of the tax calculation is unsupported.
      - The report names five discrete recommendations but does not explicitly state for each one: (a) whether the adviser is recommending the action (beyond the recommendation type 'retain', 'switch', 'withdraw'); (b) whether the adviser will arrange/implement it; (c) the intended outcome. Recommendation wording is minimal.
      - The Policy Research summary (P4, P8) lists 'Alternative Strategies' as 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand. Wants to simplify things.' This is a summary of the client's problem, not a comparison of alternative products, providers or funds. No evidence shows that specific alternatives to the recommended Aviva solution (e.g., other platforms, other funds, retention of existing arrangements) were considered and rejected with reasons.
    Comparison can be performed: the report asserts five client-specific rationales linked to stated objectives, and the file contains enough evidence to assess whether those rationales are supported, specific, and distinct. However, material gaps and inconsistencies emerge: (1) Two rationales (for Aviva and Standard Life) are identical verbatim, yet both are marked client-specific. This is a red flag. The guard for boilerplate language applies: identical rationale bullets repeated across recommendations should be counted once and isClientSpecific marked false. The extraction's own metadata flags this as an internal inconsistency. Both rationales cite the same lifestyling/risk mismatch problem, suggesting they are templated rather than tailored to each plan's distinct circumstances. (2) The objectives OBJ2 and OBJ3 are not defined in the evidence. Without that definition, the claim that each rationale is 'linked to specified goals' cannot be verified. The report asserts linkage but provides no evidence of what the goals are. (3) The Zurich rationale introduces specific detail (risk levels 7 and 2, absence of risk 5, lack of diversification) that does not appear in the retrieved passages. The Policy Research summary (P4, P8) states only 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'. The specific detail is unsupported by evidence. (4) The Scottish Widows rationale quotes £170 net, but the derivation from £211.32 gross is not explained in the evidence, and the tax assumption is unstated. (5) The Policy Research (P4, P8) lists 'Alternative Strategies' generically ('Current set up isn't working...') but does not show structured comparison of alternative products, providers or funds. The evidence does not demonstrate that alternatives were actively considered and rejected with reasons—only that the client found the existing situation complex and wanted simplification. A recommendation to switch from Aviva's lifestyling fund to HSBC and CT Universal MAP Balanced funds (per P7) is presented without comparison of other multi-asset options or justification for those two funds over others. (6) The report does not clearly state for each recommendation whether the adviser will implement it, when, and what the intended outcome is (beyond the action type). (7) The risk profile evidence (P12) shows the client moved from risk level 4 (questionnaire) to risk level 5 (agreed), but no meeting notes explain the discussion or how the lifestyling mismatch in Aviva and Standard Life was identified as problematic during that process. The rationale assumes the risk assessment process detected a mismatch, but the evidence is silent. The extraction notes record: 'No additional notes were recorded' for the agreed risk level discussion (P11, P12). (8) Vulnerability overlay: The extraction does not identify any actual or potential vulnerability characteristics that would affect the assessment of suitability rationale. The file shows the client found multiple pensions confusing (policy research summary), but no evidence indicates disability, language barrier, cognitive impairment, or other support needs that would materially change whether a written rationale is adequate.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts Aviva Pension Portfolio as the recommended solution with multiple rationales: (1) 'It is the most suitable solution given your circumstances and retirement objectives' (page 9, linked to OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3); (2) tax efficiency and pension freedoms / Flexi Access Drawdown capability (page 9); (3) provider strengths including financial rating 'A (Stable) by S&P, with £407bn AUM (31 Dec 2024)'; choice of 6,900 funds; Aviva Investors expertise; competitive charges; online tools; governance (page 10). For investment strategy, the report recommends 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C and 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced C (page 12), both Risk Rating 5. Rationales include: reflection of risk profile (OBJ1, OBJ3); diversification across asset classes; blend of active/passive; alignment with client preference for simplicity (OBJ3); competitive charges; peer performance; CIP membership with quarterly investment committee review (page 12). The report states it 'considered investment pathway solutions offered by the recommended provider' but rejected them because they are generic and do not reflect individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements, and risk profile (page 12 implied from P2 evidence).
    File:     Evidence from the research documents shows: (P1, P5, P9) Aviva Platform was identified in a SIPP research report as one of 4 platforms matching filters applied, with Aviva Platform Personal Pension - Choice product shown as the recommended SIPP platform. The research notes 'No DNA applied' and 'No filters were applied', indicating a non-filtered, whole-of-market panel review. (P5) Research profile describes Aviva formed in 2000 from CGU/Norwich Union merger, serving 18 million customers as at 31 Dec 2020 — but provides no S&P rating or £407bn AUM figure contemporary to the report date (23 Oct 2025). (P10, P12) Fund performance data is available: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C shows 1-year return 9.92%, 5-year 40.06%, 10-year 122.95%; CT Universal MAP Balanced C shows 1-year 11.17%, 5-year 44.73% (no 10-year disclosed). (P10) MiFID II cost disclosure shows HSBC ongoing costs 0.18%, CT ongoing 0.29%, combined total 0.37% including transaction costs. (P14) Pension Switching Report confirms recommendation to 'consolidate your existing pensions into a new solution' switching from Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life to Aviva Platform Pension Portfolio - Choice, with retirement age 75 used for comparison. (P8) Aviva Platform product literature confirms it is a SIPP providing flexible, tax-efficient retirement, with two options (Core and Choice), cash account, and consolidated investment management. (P2, P13) Policy Research notes existing Aviva arrangement (EA3) was in 'Aviva pension my future focus consolidation' (lifestyle strategy); the recommendation switches to different funds (HSBC and CT multi-asset) on Aviva Platform. (P13) Policy Research summary states for Aviva: 'Recommendation: Switch. Why/Why isnt this scheme suitable for the client? Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'. (P2) The report narrative explains investment pathways were rejected in favour of the CIP, noting 'You require a more tailored solution than any of the four generic solutions' and 'It does not reflect your individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile as accurately as the recommended investment strategy.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The report cites S&P rating 'A (Stable)' and '£407bn AUM as at 31 December 2024' for Aviva as justification for provider selection. The research document (P5) provides only 2020-dated AUM (18 million customers, not AUM figure), and no S&P rating or 2024 AUM data appears in the evidence pack. The cited financial strength metric cannot be corroborated from the case file.
      - The report recommends switching FROM an existing Aviva arrangement (EA3, 'Aviva pension my future focus consolidation') TO the same provider (Aviva) but on a different platform and in different funds. Policy Research (P13) notes the existing Aviva arrangement was rejected as 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing', yet the report does not explicitly explain why switching to Aviva Platform Choice resolves the 'expensive' and 'no adviser servicing' criticisms — the charge comparison on page 11 shows the switch costs 4.24% more INCLUDING adviser fees, undermining the cost rationale.
      - The report states investment pathways were considered and rejected (P2 evidence supports this narrative). However, the report does not name or quantify the alternative investment pathways from Aviva to show why the CIP was chosen over them. The rejection narrative is generic ('tailored solution', 'more accurate reflection') rather than comparative.
      - Provider selection is asserted as 'most suitable' but the report does not evidence a comparison with other SIPP providers beyond Aviva. Research document (P1, P9) shows 4 platforms were identified (Aviva Platform, Elevate/Aberdeen, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime Platform) with filters applied showing 'No filters were applied' and 'No DNA applied'. The research outcome is that only Aviva Platform matched; however, the report does not explain what criteria or objectives drove the filter design or why the other three were unsuitable — the research shows shortlist of 1 from 4 but provides no comparative justification.
    The report provides multiple rationales linking the recommendation to client objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) and states that suitability has been assessed against circumstances and retirement objectives. The Aviva Platform provider choice is supported by documented research (SIPP research report in P1, P5, P9) that identified Aviva as the recommended platform from a panel of 4 SIPP solutions. The fund selection (HSBC and CT multi-asset funds) is supported by performance data (P10, P12) and risk-profiling (Risk Rating 5 matching the client's agreed attitude to risk). The investment strategy rationale is detailed, client-specific, and linked to objectives.
    
    However, material gaps exist in the consistency and completeness of the justification:
    
    (1) Financial strength claim: The S&P rating 'A (Stable)' and '£407bn AUM (31 Dec 2024)' cited on page 10 are not corroborated by any document in the evidence pack. The research data (P5) is 5 years old (31 Dec 2020). This is an unsupported assertion on a material claim used to justify provider suitability.
    
    (2) Provider selection rationale incomplete: The report states Aviva is 'most suitable' but does not explain why the other three SIPP platforms identified in the research (Elevate, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) were rejected. The research shortlist shows 1 from 4 matched; the report does not connect this to client objectives or circumstances. A whole-of-market search was performed (P5 'Whole of Market has been used'), but the filter design and outcome are not explained in the suitability narrative. This leaves provider selection appearing arbitrary rather than justified against alternatives.
    
    (3) Internal switching cost inconsistency: The existing Aviva arrangement (EA3) was lifestyle-oriented and rejected as 'expensive' and lacking adviser servicing (P13). The report recommends switching to Aviva Platform Choice on the same provider. The charge comparison (page 11, referenced in instructions) shows the recommendation is 4.24% more expensive INCLUDING adviser fees than the current combined plans. The report does not reconcile how moving to Aviva resolves the 'expensive' criticism when the numerical comparison contradicts it. This is a material contradiction between the justification (tax efficiency, competitive charges, value) and the stated charge impact.
    
    (4) Investment pathway rejection rationale generic: The report rejected investment pathways from Aviva in favour of the CIP. The evidence (P2) supports that pathways were considered and the CIP was selected for reasons of tailoring and alignment. However, the report does not quantify or compare the pathways' cost, features, or performance against the recommended CIP strategy, making the rejection narrative explanatory rather than comparative.
    
    (5) Fund selection and existing arrangement relationship: The report does not explicitly state that switching from the existing Aviva 'my future focus consolidation' (lifestyle) fund to the recommended HSBC/CT multi-asset funds is a material change in strategy. This transition is documented in the policy research (P3, P13) but is not addressed in the suitability report narrative, leaving it unclear whether the change reflects objectives or is incidental to the consolidation.
    
    The guard on provider marketing claims applies: the report cites Aviva's strengths (financial rating, choice, expertise, charges) as absolute claims rather than comparative ones. These are asserted without evidence they are superior to competitors (Elevate, Aegon, Wealthtime). The guard on panel membership applies: the report appears to rely on documented panel/research processes (P1, P5, P9) which is legitimate, but the report itself does not state it is relying on a documented panel process — the research exists but the suitability report does not cite or explain reliance on it.
    
    The comparison WAS performed (research documents exist, provider and fund data are present, objectives are stated), so comparisonPerformed is TRUE. The issues are gaps in explanation and evidence, not absence of comparison.

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The canonical model asserts that all 5 recommendations carry client-specific rationale (isClientSpecific: [true, true, true, true, true]). The suitability report [P13] states: 'This report is designed to explain how and why my recommendations can help meet these needs and objectives'. The report identifies the client's objective as needing to consolidate pensions to simplify understanding before retirement in 2 years, with a target household income of £1,700 net monthly and ATR 5. Five recommendations are made: (1) Switch Aviva pension to new advised Aviva pension (£3,744.36); (2-5) Switch or retain other arrangements (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Zurich, Standard Life).
    File:     [P5] and [P14] document the client's stated objective: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' [P14] shows a policy research matrix confirming the objective but with sparse justification entries. For Aviva it notes 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'. For Zurich: 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'. For People's Pension: 'Active workplace pension'. For Scottish Widows: incomplete entry. Standard Life entry also incomplete. [P13] provides the headline recommendation but does not elaborate with client-specific rationale linking each recommendation to the consolidation objective or explaining why each product/provider/wrapper choice meets the stated need to simplify.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts all 5 recommendations are client-specific, but [P14] policy research shows only brief, generic notes ('Lifestyle, expensive', 'Active workplace pension') rather than detailed, personalised rationales linking each recommendation to the client's stated consolidation and simplification objective.
      - The suitability report [P13] does not provide explicit alternative consideration narratives; [P14] lists alternatives under 'Alternative Strategies' as 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand. Wants to simplify things', but does not explain why the specific recommended arrangements (e.g., new Aviva platform over existing Aviva, or retention of People's Pension) were chosen over identified alternatives.
      - No evidence in the file explains why switching Aviva to a new advised Aviva platform specifically meets the consolidation objective better than other consolidation vehicles, despite the extraction metadata noting the existing fund ('Aviva pension my future focus consolidation') is replaced with entirely different funds ('HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' and 'CT Universal MAP Balanced').
      - The file does not contain explicit rationale explaining the recommended outcome for each arrangement (e.g., why People's Pension is retained vs. consolidated, why Scottish Widows is recommended for encashment under small pots rules rather than consolidation).
    The comparison can be performed because both the canonical model (reporting all 5 rationales as client-specific) and the supporting evidence (client objectives, policy research matrix, recommendation summaries) are present. However, a material discrepancy exists. The report claims isClientSpecific: true for all 5 recommendations, implying each is tied to the client's individual circumstances and stated objective of consolidation and simplification. The evidence [P14] shows only abbreviated, generic justifications ('Lifestyle, expensive', 'Active workplace pension') without elaboration linking each recommendation to how it achieves the consolidation or simplification goal. [P13] confirms the overall objective but does not itemise client-specific reasoning for each discrete recommendation. The extraction metadata also notes an internal inconsistency: the existing Aviva fund (lifestyle/consolidation-oriented) is being replaced with entirely different multi-asset funds, yet the file provides no explanation of why this specific fund switch meets the client's stated objective. Additionally, [P14] lists alternatives ('Current set up isn't working') but does not explain why the recommended arrangement was chosen over those alternatives. This is a weak justification pattern: the rationale exists in headline form but lacks the personalised detail connecting each recommendation to the client's circumstances. The client's vulnerability context (not evident from the provided file extracts) has not been assessed against this requirement, so no overlay impact is apparent here. The standard regulatory wording guard (past performance, tax caveats) does not apply to this finding because the issue is absence of substantive personalisation, not presence of boilerplate disclaimers.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report presents four alternatives considered for existing pension arrangements: (1) Leave pot to grow until retirement (do nothing) — presented without rejection reasons; (2) Leave existing pension scheme untouched — rejected because 'funds do not meet your risk profile' and plan is '0.06% more expensive than recommended solution'; (3) Switch investment funds within existing scheme — rejected because 'There are no Multi-Asset funds and only a limited range of other options'; (4) Leave Zurich scheme in place with internal fund switch — rejected because 'no multi-asset funds available within your risk range' and 'main priority is to consolidate your pensions, gain better control, and be well-positioned for retirement with all available drawdown options'. For the employer scheme (Option 4 in research), rejected because employer contributions would be lost, funds do not meet risk profile, and plan is more expensive with limited fund range. The report recommends consolidating existing pensions into a new Aviva Platform solution. Client-specific rationale is provided: lower costs, better fund range, meets risk rating 5, consolidation objective, and full drawdown flexibility.
    File:     Evidence from P6 (suitability report itself) lists five existing arrangements with different features: People's Pension (7 funds, no adviser servicing, workplace scheme); Scottish Widows (29 funds, no adviser servicing); Aviva (80+ funds); Zurich (60+ funds); Standard Life (300 funds, allows servicing adviser). P22 (file notes) records that Scottish Widows was 'discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility'; Aviva's only mixed asset fund is 'risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR'; Zurich 'do not have risk level 5 funds' and blending would reduce diversification — 'main priority for the client is to consolidate, get a better handle on his pensions and be ready for retirement'; Standard Life funds are 'more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva'. P1, P2, P3, P4, P5, P10, P14, P19 present pension switching reports comparing existing pensions against new Aviva solution with critical yield analysis. P5 shows overall effect of switching: at mid growth (2.95%), projected value decreases by £12,000 (9.4%) from £127,000 to £115,000, with critical yield of 4.8%. P3 shows Standard Life comparison with £2,000 decrease at mid growth. P10, P19 show overall position if some pensions retained. P16 (suitability report) discusses stakeholder pension alternative, stating the recommended plan is 'more suitable for your circumstances because the solution is more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension' with better fund range and access. P18 (suitability report) provides detailed rejection of Scottish Widows alternative, explaining lack of multi-asset funds at risk rating 5, absence of adviser management, and limited withdrawal flexibility. Final advice table in P18 shows: People's Pension retain; Scottish Widows encash; Aviva switch; Zurich (implied switch); Standard Life (implied switch).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report cites 'funds do not meet your risk profile' for employer scheme (Option 4) and existing Aviva scheme, but P22 shows only Standard Life comparison was completed on fund performance grounds ('more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva'); employer scheme fund risk rating not documented in evidence
      - Report states alternative 1 ('Leave pot to grow until retirement') has no rejection reasons stated, but this applies only to Scottish Widows small pot per P18 context; the alternative structure in canonical model does not clearly distinguish which 'pot' is being referenced
      - Canonical model lists 'Leave existing pension scheme untouched' as separate from employer scheme (Option 4), but evidence in P6 and P18 treats employer scheme as one of five specific arrangements, not a generic alternative
      - Report asserts plan is '0.06% more expensive than recommended solution' for employer scheme, but evidence does not disclose the specific charge figure for this arrangement; P6 shows 'Other Considerations' for People's Pension as 'Current workplace scheme' but no charge is stated
      - Report states Zurich alternative rejected due to 'no multi-asset funds within your risk range', but P1, P3, P5, P10 pension switching reports show Zurich was analysed as one of the arrangements being consolidated into new solution, making it evaluated but not presented as a standalone 'leave in place with internal fund switch' alternative in the way the report frames it
      - Pension switching evidence (P1-P5, P10, P14, P19) shows critical yields of 0.0% to 7.8% across different growth scenarios for different arrangements, but report does not disclose or discuss the critical yield required (4.8% per P3 mid-growth scenario noted in extraction metadata) as a limiting factor on recommendation suitability
      - Evidence in P5 shows overall effect of switching is a £12,000 decrease (9.4%) at mid growth, yet report describes this as 'lower costs' without disclosing the net detriment after charges; like-for-like comparison shows 0.24% savings but including adviser fees the plan is 4.24% more expensive per extraction notes
    The report presents alternatives with client-specific rejection reasons that are substantially corroborated by the evidence, though with material gaps and a critical contradiction. Four alternatives are named and each carries stated reasons. The Scottish Widows alternative (P18) is comprehensively justified: lack of multi-asset fund at risk rating 5, absence of adviser management, and limited withdrawal flexibility are all supported by the file notes and suitability report content. The employer scheme alternative is rejected with multiple reasons: loss of employer contributions (supported by P6 showing 'current workplace scheme'), fund range issues, and cost comparison (0.06% stated but not verified in evidence). The Zurich and Standard Life alternatives appear in the pension switching reports as arrangements being evaluated for consolidation rather than as explicit 'leave in place' alternatives, but P22 records that Zurich was rejected because blending to achieve risk 5 would reduce diversification and the main priority was consolidation — this reasoning is client-specific and linked to documented objectives. The Aviva internal fund switch alternative is explicitly rejected in P22 as the only mixed-asset fund available is risk-rated 4, not matching agreed risk 5. However, a material discrepancy exists: the pension switching analysis (P1, P3, P5) demonstrates that the recommended consolidation produces a net detriment of £12,000 (9.4% decrease) at mid-growth rates, with a critical yield requirement of 4.8% that must be exceeded for the recommendation to break even. The report describes this as 'lower costs' and 'more cost effective' without disclosing the critical yield requirement or the maturity value reduction. P5 explicitly shows 'Effect of switching: £12,000 or 9.4% decrease' at mid growth, contradicting the assertion of cost advantage. The extraction notes confirm this tension: 'Like for Like: 0.24% less expensive' but 'Including Adviser Fee: 4.24% more expensive'. The report does not explain how this net detriment is consistent with suitability given the consolidation objective and withdrawal flexibility benefits. Additionally, the canonical model shows the first alternative ('Leave pot to grow until retirement') has no rejection reasons, but P18 shows this applies only to Scottish Widows small pot (£211.32) where the rationale is that 'You could potentially take the full amount within your personal allowance, meaning no tax would be due' — this is contextual and not a true 'no rejection reason' situation but rather a conditional alternative not pursued. The report does not clearly state whether the recommendation to consolidate the three larger pensions (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, totalling £110,185.60) and encash the small Scottish Widows pot is a single recommendation or separate recommendations for each arrangement, though P18 implies distinct advice for each scheme.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies five existing arrangements and assigns each a recommended action: People's Pension (Retain), Scottish Widows (Encash), Aviva (Switch), Zurich (Switch), and Standard Life (Switch). Each action is accompanied by rationales. For Retain: workplace pension with ongoing contributions, linked to OBJ3. For Encash: withdraw full amount under small pots rules to savings account, linked to OBJ3. For the three Switch recommendations, multiple rationales are provided covering lower costs, lifestyling drift, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, multi-asset fund selection, and improved retirement options. All rationales are marked isClientSpecific=true and linked to OBJ3.
    File:     The evidence pack contains policy documents for each arrangement (Aviva [P7], Zurich [P9], Standard Life [P4]/[P1], People's Pension [P13], Scottish Widows [P2]). These documents confirm fund values, plan types, and available features. P21 (Suitability Report) states recommendations: People's Pension as current workplace scheme, Scottish Widows marked 'Retain and Encash', and switches recommended for others. P23 (Suitability Report) provides detailed reasoning for Standard Life switch focusing on lower costs, lifestyling drift, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging, and ongoing monitoring. P12 (Policy Research) notes the client's objective to consolidate pensions for simplification due to complexity and poor understanding. P20 and P22 (Client Agreement) describe ongoing review and advisory services but do not detail specific arrangement outcomes.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts Scottish Widows action as 'Encash' but P21 table lists it as 'Retain and Encash', creating ambiguity about the primary recommended outcome.
      - Report provides rationale for Aviva Switch citing 'lifestyling drift' and 'multi-asset fund selection' as reasons, but P7 shows Aviva's existing fund is 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2' with auto-switching (lifestage approach), and the recommended funds are 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' (50%) and 'CT Universal MAP Balanced' (50%), representing a complete fund change rather than a retention issue.
      - Report rationale for switches cites 'lower costs' but extraction notes show Standard Life existing charge stated as 0.52% on page 5 yet 0.18% on page 11 (Pension Switch Charges table), and the Aviva recommendation is 4.24% more expensive including adviser fee (page 11), undermining the lower-cost rationale.
      - Report provides identical generic rationales across multiple switch recommendations (e.g., 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' appear verbatim for both Aviva and Zurich switches), appearing boilerplate rather than tailored to each arrangement's specific circumstances.
      - Report does not explain whether the adviser will arrange or implement each switch, only recommending the action.
      - Report does not state timing for implementation of the switches.
    All five existing arrangements receive a stated recommended action per the requirement. However, material concerns arise in the quality and consistency of the rationale. The Scottish Widows guidance is ambiguous: P21 lists 'Retain and Encash' but the extraction records the action as 'Encash' alone. The rationale provided ('Withdraw full amount under small pots rules to savings account') aligns with encashment, not retention. The Aviva switch is justified by 'lifestyling drift', yet the existing arrangement (P7) is already in a lifestage/auto-switching fund; the switch would move away from Aviva's own consolidation fund to third-party multi-asset funds, a material change not adequately explained by drift. More critically, the rationale for 'lower costs' is contradicted by the figures: the report itself states (P23) 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (which contradicts 'lower costs'), and P11 shows the switch is 4.24% more expensive when adviser fees are included. The extraction's internal inconsistencies confirm charge amounts are computed on wrong plan values (EA5 impact computed on Zurich value of £103,438.46, not Standard Life's £3,002.00). The rationales for the three switch recommendations (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) share identical language across multiple topics ('Greater Investment Choice', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' appear verbatim), suggesting boilerplate rather than client-specific justification. The client's stated objective (P12) is to simplify and consolidate due to complexity, yet the report does not clearly link each arrangement outcome to this primary need. The report marks all rationales isClientSpecific=true, but the verbatim repetition across different arrangements contradicts this claim. No alternatives are presented for any arrangement except implicit consideration of retention (mentioned only for People's Pension). The report does not state whether the adviser will arrange the switches or when implementation will occur, leaving ambiguity on material execution details.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states five recommendations: (1) Retain People's Pension workplace scheme — Recommended, Client responsibility, outcome 'Continue to benefit from employer contributions while maintaining current investment performance'; (2) Encash Scottish Widows pension plan — Recommended, Adviser will implement, outcome 'Simplify pension arrangements while minimizing tax impact'; (3) Switch Aviva pension (SP50163762) to new Aviva Platform Personal Pension — Recommended, Adviser will implement, outcome 'Consolidate pension arrangements, escape lifestyling funds that move outside agreed risk range, and gain greater investment flexibility'; (4) Switch Zurich pension (P10026-719-001/DL) to new Aviva Platform Personal Pension — Recommended, Adviser will implement, outcome 'Consolidate largest pension holding, gain appropriate risk-level funds, and improve retirement flexibility'; (5) Switch Standard Life pension (D2301334000) to new Aviva Platform Personal Pension — Recommended, Adviser will implement, outcome 'Consolidate pension arrangements, escape lifestyling funds, and ensure investment remains aligned with agreed risk profile'. Implementation timescale stated as '10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes'. Adviser charges stated: initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55), ongoing advisory fee of 1% per annum (circa £1,101.85 for first year). Nomination of beneficiaries form recommended post-transfer. Client comfort with timescale referenced. All five recommendations explicitly flagged as 'Recommended' in adviceStatus field.
    File:     The Aviva Platform Personal Pension terms and conditions (P1-P8) contain generic provisions on charge deduction, adviser fees, implementation timescales, and income drawdown options. The Risk profile report (P9, P11, P15) records that 'No additional notes were recorded' for investment target discussion, agreed risk level discussion, investor experience, and capacity for loss questionnaire. P12 describes the agreed risk level as 'Balanced' (Risk level 5) with investment in CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%). P15 shows projections for the agreed risk level with an estimated annual total charge of 1.5%. No document in the file evidences the adviser's statement of whether each specific change is recommended, who will implement it, or the intended outcome for any individual recommendation. The file contains no advisability statements, no recommendation letters, no change instructions, and no adviser confirmation of implementation responsibility for the five specific pension changes.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts five discrete recommendations each with adviceStatus 'Recommended', implementation responsibility, and intended outcome; file contains no independent corroboration of these five specific recommendations, their advisability, implementation responsibility, or intended outcomes. The terms and conditions are generic product documentation and do not address the suitability or implementation of any specific recommendation.
      - Report names specific intended outcomes for each recommendation (e.g. 'escape lifestyling funds that move outside agreed risk range' for Aviva switch, 'improve retirement flexibility' for Zurich switch); file contains no matching intended outcomes for any specific recommendation.
      - Report states adviser will implement three switches and one encashment; file contains no instruction, agreement, or undertaking from adviser to implement these specific transactions.
      - Report states initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) and ongoing advisory fee of 1% per annum; file contains only generic Aviva platform charge terms with no adviser fee agreement or client acceptance of these specific fees documented.
    The requirement tests whether the suitability report states, for each change suggested, whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome. The report satisfies the structural requirement: each of the five recommendations explicitly states (a) adviceStatus of 'Recommended', (b) implementation responsibility (Client for Retain, Adviser for all others), and (c) a specific intended outcome naming client objectives (consolidation, risk alignment, flexibility, tax efficiency). The implementation field contains the next steps including timescale (10-15 working days), processing considerations (out-of-market risk disclosure, cancellation rights), and adviser fees. The report is internally consistent: all five actions are marked as Recommended; the adviser accepts implementation responsibility for all changes except the Retain action, which requires no adviser action; and each outcome is stated in terms linked to client objectives stated elsewhere in the report (consolidation, risk management, flexibility). The requirement does not mandate that the file hold independent evidence corroborating each recommendation; it asks whether the report contains the statement. The suitability report itself contains the statement. The file's silence on these recommendations — it holds no adviser letter, no instruction, no change authorization — is not a contradiction of what the report says; it is an absence of corroboration. The report's internal consistency between the five recommendations, their status, implementation responsibility, and intended outcomes is complete. No guard applies: these are not 'You may wish to consider' suggestions (they are unambiguously recommended); the report does state who will implement each (the adviceStatus and implementation fields are explicit); and the intended outcomes are clearly named for each. The canonical model extraction captured the report's statements accurately. No discrepancy exists between what the report asserts and what it asserts elsewhere. The question is whether the report contains the required statement: it does.
