  [G6.7] Recommendations that present multiple possible outcomes without stating which is recommended and why
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report makes five recommendations, each with an action and rationale. (1) Retain People's Pension workplace scheme – rationale: employer contributions continue and would be missed if transferred. (2) Encash Scottish Widows pension plan – rationale: small pot rules allow withdrawal with minimal tax impact, estimated net receipt £170. (3) Switch Aviva pension to new Aviva Platform Personal Pension – rationale: current lifestyling funds automatically reduce risk and move portfolio outside agreed risk range; not recommended. (4) Switch Zurich pension to new Aviva Platform Personal Pension – rationale: Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds; existing blend of risk 7 and risk 2 lacks proper multi-asset diversification. (5) Switch Standard Life pension to new Aviva Platform Personal Pension – rationale: lifestyling drift moves portfolio outside agreed risk range. Each recommendation states an action ('Retain', 'Encash', 'Switch') and marks adviceStatus as 'Recommended'. No alternatives analysis or reasons for rejection are presented in the extracted recommendations structure. The report asserts all recommendations are suitable but does not explicitly state which option is being recommended where multiple paths might exist, nor does it systematically present and reject alternatives.
    File:     File notes (P1, P2) record consideration of alternatives for each arrangement. For People's Pension: 'alternative option' noted but 'No alternative as Defaqto risk rates all of them as a 9' (P1). For Scottish Widows: 'alternative option – 30% liquid fund and 70% of Portfolio three' is mentioned (P1). For Aviva: 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR.. Discounted switch' (P2). For Zurich: 'Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds, a blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used to create the below 5. They have no multi asset funds in his risk range either...Wouldn't recommend investing in singular asset types. The blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally. For these reasons we would not recommend an internal fund switch' (P2). For Standard Life: 'Discounted as all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva' (P2). File notes also record ATR discussion (P3): 'You completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire. We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' This shows the recommended risk level (5) was agreed after questionnaire indicated 4. Charges comparison appears on page 11 of the suitability report: 'Like for Like: ...our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' and 'Including Adviser Fee: ...our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' (internal report finding from extraction).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report extracts show no alternatives presented structure; file notes record alternatives considered but report does not systematically state reasons for rejection in the recommendation text itself.
      - Report does not state which outcome is recommended where costs show a contradiction: 0.24% cheaper 'like for like' but 4.24% more expensive 'including adviser fee.' The report does not resolve which scenario applies or why the adviser fee comparison is being included/excluded.
      - Report states Aviva current fund is 'Aviva pension my future focus consolidation' but recommends switching to 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' (50%) and 'CT Universal MAP Balanced' (50%) – entirely different funds, yet report rationale focuses on lifestyling drift without explaining why the new funds are preferable to other lifestyling solutions or alternative consolidation options.
      - File notes state risk rating agreed at 5 but P3 also contains text 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6' which contradicts the agreed level of 5 shown elsewhere in the same passage.
      - Report rationale for Zurich switch cites 'blend of risk 7 and risk 2' as problematic due to lack of multi-asset diversification, yet the new Aviva recommendation (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced + CT Universal MAP Balanced) is presented as suitable without explaining how two separate funds meet diversification needs better than Zurich's managed approach.
      - Standard Life rationale in report states only 'lifestyling drift' (identical to Aviva and current Standard Life funds), but file notes give explicit cost comparison: 'all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva' – cost justification is in notes but not in report rationale.
    The report presents five discrete recommendations, each marked 'Recommended', so it does state which action is being recommended for each arrangement. However, the requirement asks whether multiple possible outcomes are left unresolved without stating which is recommended and why. The report does not present unresolved multiple outcomes; it presents one outcome per arrangement. The finding here is not a failure to choose between options, but a failure to explain the choice adequately. File notes record that alternatives were researched and rejected (Aviva internal switch discounted because risk 4 fund does not meet ATR; Zurich internal switch discounted because no risk 5 multi-asset available; Standard Life switch discounted because funds are more expensive), but these detailed reasons do not appear in the main report recommendations themselves. The cost contradiction is material: the report asserts the Aviva plan is simultaneously 0.24% cheaper and 4.24% more expensive depending on whether adviser fees are included. The report does not state which comparison applies to this client or why the adviser fee is or is not relevant to suitability. This leaves open which outcome is actually recommended – a lower-cost like-for-like switch, or a higher-cost switch justified by other factors. The rationale for Standard Life switch in the report cites only lifestyling drift, not the cost advantage documented in file notes, so the most material justification for the recommendation is absent from the report. The Aviva switch rationale focuses on lifestyling drift but does not explain why HSBC and CT funds (two separate entities) are preferable to alternatives or how they resolve the diversification concern raised about Zurich. File notes also contain a risk rating inconsistency (5 vs 6) that affects whether all recommendations are even aligned to the same agreed profile. The report does not resolve this. The charge arithmetic in the extracted data shows multiple inconsistencies (e.g. EA5 impact calculated at 0.26% of Zurich's value, not Standard Life's value), which undermines confidence in the cost justifications, but these are internal model issues rather than presentation of unresolved options. The key gap is not that options are left open, but that options considered and rejected are not presented in the report's own recommendation text, and cost comparisons that contradict each other are not resolved.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts five recommendations with the following actions and implementation details: (1) Retain People's Pension (action: Retain, responsible party: Client); (2) Encash Scottish Widows (action: Encash, responsible party: Adviser, timescale: 10-15 working days); (3) Switch Aviva (action: Switch, responsible party: Adviser, timescale: 10-15 working days); (4) Switch Zurich (action: Switch, responsible party: Adviser, timescale: 10-15 working days); (5) Switch Standard Life (action: Switch, responsible party: Adviser, timescale: 10-15 working days). The report states an overall expected timescale of '10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes' and names the adviser as responsible party for switches. Initial adviser charge of £3,305.55 (3% of transfer values) will be 'paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer'. Ongoing adviser charge of 1% per annum (circa £1,101.85 first year) will be 'paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme'. The report specifies that a nomination of beneficiaries form 'will be provided to you after the transfer process is begun' and recommends completion. The report discloses out-of-market risk and notes the client 'is comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds'.
    File:     The Aviva Platform Terms and Conditions [P1-P8] detail operational timescales but do not corroborate the adviser's specific implementation timescale or responsibility allocation. [P1] states that for income drawdown changes, the adviser must provide notice 'at least 10 working days before' an existing payment date. [P7] specifies that withdrawal instructions 'will not take place on a non Business Day and will wait until the next Business Day', and for Structured Products, 'if You wish to withdraw monies before the end of the fixed term, then You must notify Us, and the full Investment will need to be encashed.' [P4] and [P8] detail death benefit nomination processes but do not specify when nomination forms are provided or when they must be completed relative to transfer. [P5] confirms adviser charge deduction mechanisms: initial adviser charge is deducted 'before We invest them on Your behalf' and ongoing adviser charge is deducted 'from Your Pension Portfolio'. The Scottish Widows Policy Info [P10] states 'Money4Life Plans do not support ongoing advisor charging' and does not support 'the transferring of Servicing Rights or Commission'. No document specifies the 10-15 working day timescale for transfers or disinvestment as an absolute commitment rather than a service standard. No document retrieved specifies who arranges or implements each individual recommendation (client vs adviser for retention, adviser for switches, adviser for encashment).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report assigns the 'Retain' action for People's Pension to the Client as responsible party, but provides no timescale, completion date, or further instruction for client action on this arrangement.
      - The report states adviser will effect switches and encashment but does not specify whether the same adviser who signs the report will perform these actions or whether a different entity (e.g. Aviva, the platform provider) will execute them.
      - The report names a global 10-15 working day timescale for all switches and encashment but does not break this down by individual arrangement or identify which steps (disinvestment, banking, reinvestment) apply to each.
      - The report states initial adviser fee will be 'paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer' but does not specify which pension schemes or whether all five schemes have been instructed to do so.
      - The report states Scottish Widows charge is currently '0% ongoing' (implied by file [P10] stating 'Money4Life Plans do not support ongoing advisor charging') but simultaneously recommends an ongoing adviser charge of 1% to be paid via Aviva Platform, creating a material change in charging structure not explicitly explained.
      - The report recommends nomination of beneficiaries completion 'after the transfer process is begun' but does not specify whether this must be completed before funds are invested, before income is drawn, or at any other defined point material to suitability.
    The requirement tests whether recommendations clearly state what action will be taken, when it will be taken, and by whom where that is material to suitability. The report does name each action (Retain, Encash, Switch×3), assigns responsibility (Client for Retain; Adviser for others), and provides a timescale (10-15 working days). However, material gaps exist: (1) The retention of People's Pension is assigned to the client with no implementation instruction, timescale, or stated outcome—leaving it unclear whether the client must take any action or whether it is simply to be left in place. (2) The adviser is named as responsible party but not the specific adviser entity; the Aviva terms [P5] contemplate adviser designation and deduction, but the report does not confirm whether the signing adviser will execute or whether Aviva will do so on instruction. (3) The 10-15 working day timescale is presented as global to all transfers but the Aviva terms [P7] distinguish between Business Days and non-Business Days and reserve the right to delay, making the timescale aspirational rather than guaranteed. (4) The initial adviser fee is stated to be 'paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer' but no evidence shows that each of the five schemes has been instructed to make this payment or that the fee will not fall due from the client if schemes do not pay. (5) The Scottish Widows arrangement is assigned both an encashment action and an ongoing adviser charge of 1% per annum via Aviva Platform, but [P10] confirms Scottish Widows Money4Life Plans 'do not support ongoing advisor charging', so the charge cannot apply to the existing Scottish Widows holding—it can only apply post-transfer to Aviva, yet the report does not distinguish this clearly. (6) The nomination of beneficiaries instruction lacks a defined completion deadline material to the switch: whether it must be done before transfer, before reinvestment, or before first benefit claim is not stated. (7) The report asserts client comfort with 10-15 working days due to liquid cash reserves, but does not name the amount, source, or accessibility of those reserves, making it difficult to verify the suitability of the timescale for this client. The guards permit 'a timescale quoted as a provider service standard' and 'naming the adviser firm rather than an individual'; the report names the adviser firm generically and quotes what may be Aviva's service standard, satisfying the guard. However, the gaps in implementation specificity on Retain (no action for client), on fee payment (no confirmation schemes will pay), and on charging on Scottish Widows post-transfer (no clarity on what applies when) go beyond missing tactical detail and affect the clarity of the recommendation itself.
      · Aviva Platform Terms and Conditions [H] (P5)
        "Initial Adviser Charge - We will deduct any initial Adviser charge You have agreed with Your Adviser, from Your Payment(s) before We invest them on Your behalf. The Initial Adviser Charge is a one off amount."
      · Aviva Platform Terms and Conditions [H] (P5)
        "Ongoing Adviser Charge - We will deduct any ongoing Adviser charge You have agreed with Your Adviser from Your Pension Portfolio and will pay it to Your Adviser."
      · Scottish Widows Policy Info [E] (P10)
        "Money4Life Plans (Plans starting with ZU) do not support ongoing advisor charging, the transferring of Servicing Rights or Commission."
      · Aviva Platform Terms and Conditions [H] (P7)
        "Any instruction to withdraw Your Investment will not take place on a non Business Day and will wait until the next Business Day. This could mean that You may not receive Your withdrawal proceeds straight away."
      · Aviva Platform Terms and Conditions [H] (P1)
        "If You want to cancel Your regular income payments once they have started, then Your Adviser will need to do so at least 10 working days before Your existing regular income payment date."

  [G6.9] Ancillary recommendations — beneficiary nomination, Power of Attorney, will review — are stated with an owner and are consistent with what the file records
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts: (1) 'I recommend that you complete the nomination of beneficiaries' form that will be provided to you after the transfer process is begun' (page 16, Nominations and death benefits section). This recommendation names the client as the actor ('you complete') and the adviser as provider of the form ('provided to you'). (2) Canonical model records client has a will (hasWill: true) but it is not up to date (isWillUpToDate: false). (3) Canonical model records client does NOT have a Power of Attorney granted (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: false).
    File:     Fact Find [P1-P8] records marital status as 'Married' and spouse details (age 65, financially dependent, living with client). No explicit reference to will status in the fact find. No reference to Power of Attorney status. No estate planning section completed beyond the data model fields. Zurich policy info [P10] contains a generic Pension Wise letter with no client-specific estate planning advice. No other documents in the evidence pack contain will or Power of Attorney records.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts client has a will that is not up to date (isWillUpToDate: false); file contains no evidence of will existence or status. Report recommends completion of beneficiary nomination form post-transfer; file records no pre-existing beneficiary nominations or any response to this recommendation. Report states client does not have Power of Attorney (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: false); file contains no corroborating evidence of this status.
    The requirement asks whether ancillary recommendations are stated with an owner and consistent with file records. The report makes one explicit ancillary recommendation: beneficiary nomination. This recommendation states a clear action (complete the form), timing (after transfer begins), and ownership (client completes; adviser provides). This is sufficiently specified. However, the comparison reveals three gaps: (1) The report asserts the client has a will but it is not up to date. This is recorded in the canonical model (hasWill: true, isWillUpToDate: false) but no supporting evidence appears in the file. The fact find does not include an estate planning section capturing will status; the adviser has recorded a conclusion in the model without documentary evidence of will existence or currency. (2) The report asserts the client does NOT have Power of Attorney (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: false). Again, no evidence in the file supports or refutes this statement. (3) The beneficiary nomination recommendation itself is clear and actionable, but the file records no existing beneficiary nominations, no client response to the recommendation, and no follow-up. The report does not recommend will review or Power of Attorney setup, so those omissions are not failures to implement; however, the report's assertion of will status and absence of Power of Attorney stands unsupported by any document in the pack. The guard noting that 'An adviser recommending the client set up a Power of Attorney is a different claim from the client already having one' applies here: the report is claiming the client does NOT have one, not recommending the client obtain one. That assertion appears nowhere in the evidence. Under the decision logic, where the report asserts something no document supports, this is a potential concern — the claim is unsupported.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). Platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies a fund value of £110,185.71, which does not match any single arrangement value (People's Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00, or total £116,998.47). Investment management charge £253.43 at 0.22% implies a fund value of £115,195.45, which does not match any single arrangement value or total. Total ongoing charge £484.81 at 0.44% implies a fund value of £110,184.09, which does not match any single arrangement or total. 17 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report discloses platform charge (0.21% = £231.39), investment management charge (0.22% = £253.43), and fund charges for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (0.18%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced (0.29%). Total ongoing charge stated as 0.44% (£484.81). Initial advice fee disclosed as 3% (£3,305.55). Transaction costs explicitly excluded with stated reason: 'Transaction costs are not fees paid to the asset manager; instead, they include expenses like broker commissions, taxes, and the cost of executing trades. Despite their variability, these costs are already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges.' The report states aggregated total disclosed and cumulative effect disclosed as true in the canonical model.
    File:     P3 (Illustration) confirms platform 0.21% (£231.39), investment management 0.22% (£253.43), and total charge 0.44% (£484.81). P1 (KIID for HSBC) confirms fund ongoing charge 0.18% with entry/exit charges 0.00%, no performance fee. P8 (Illustration) confirms CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc at 0.29% OCF/TER. P8 also shows weighted average charge on two funds as 0.23% (not 0.44% platform+management combined, but fund charges only). P6 (Key Features) and P8 (Illustration) describe adviser charges as 'You'll agree the cost of the advice you receive with your financial adviser' but do not state the 3% figure or £3,305.55. P17 (Report itself, category I) confirms 'initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement. In monetary terms this is £3,305.55'. No passage from independent evidence (categories E, G, H) corroborates the 3% fee or £3,305.55 figure. P3 and P8 show illustrations include adviser charges per the Key Features document reference, but no independent evidence document presents the 3% rate or £3,305.55 amount outside the report itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - Platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies a fund value of £110,185.71, which does not match any single arrangement value (People's Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00, or total £116,998.47).
      - Investment management charge £253.43 at 0.22% implies a fund value of £115,195.45, which does not match any single arrangement value or total.
      - Total ongoing charge £484.81 at 0.44% implies a fund value of £110,184.09, which does not match any single arrangement or total.
      - The 3% initial advice fee of £3,305.55 is stated only in the report (P17, category I) and is not corroborated by any independent evidence document (categories E, G, or H). No client agreement, fee schedule, or supporting documentation in the evidence pack contains this figure.
      - The report states fund charges for HSBC as 0.18% and CT as 0.29%, but does not state a blended or weighted average in the main charge table (P3); P8 illustration shows 'Weighted Average charge' of 0.23% for the two funds combined, yet the report's total of 0.44% does not clearly decompose as platform (0.21%) + management (0.22%) + blended fund (0.23%), which would equal 0.66%, not 0.44%.
      - The Standard Life existing arrangement charge is stated as 0.52% on P3 (arrangements table), but the Pension Switch Charges table on P3 shows 'Current Annual Recurring Charge of Existing Plan' for Standard Life as 0.18%, creating an internal discrepancy within the report itself (£186.19 at 0.18% does not match the 0.52% stated for the same arrangement elsewhere).
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: The fund value on which the recommended platform and investment management charges are calculated is not stated in the evidence pack. The charges of £231.39 and £253.43 imply different values (£110,185.71 and £115,195.45 respectively), neither of which corresponds to any identified source of funds being transferred or consolidated., No independent evidence (from provider illustrations, client agreement, or factsheet outside category I) corroborates the 3% initial advice fee or the £3,305.55 monetary amount. Only the report itself (P17) asserts this., The actual fund value into which the client's consolidated pension is to be placed is not clearly stated. The sum of transfer values is £116,998.47, but the charge calculations imply ~£110,000–£115,000, a discrepancy of approximately £2,000–£7,000 that is unexplained.
    The report asserts that all material costs are disclosed and aggregated, but the comparison cannot be reliably performed due to fundamental arithmetic inconsistencies. The platform charge (£231.39 at 0.21%) and investment management charge (£253.43 at 0.22%) each imply different fund values. When combined (0.21% + 0.22% = 0.43%) and then added to the stated fund charges for the two recommended funds (0.18% on 50% of portfolio + 0.29% on 50% would average 0.235%), the aggregate would be ~0.665%, not 0.44%. Nor do the monetary figures reconcile: if total fund value is £116,998.47 (the sum of all five existing arrangements' transfer values), then 0.44% would equal £514.79 annually, not £484.81. The report does not explain which fund value is used for charge calculations or how the charges aggregate. The 3% initial advice fee is disclosed in the report but lacks independent corroboration in any evidence document outside the report itself (category I). The canonical model extraction notes that the adviser fees 'match the client agreement' (implicit assumption), but no independent client agreement is present in the evidence pack to verify this. The report excludes transaction costs with a stated reason (P3), which is a valid disclosure guard. However, the missing fund value and inconsistent charge calculations prevent verification that the 0.44% total is arithmetically sound or that all layers are properly accounted for. Internal inconsistency within the report (Standard Life charge 0.52% vs. 0.18%) is flagged in the extraction but remains unresolved in the evidence. The report's statement that cumulative effect is disclosed is supported by reference to 'personal illustration' (P3, P8), which does show charges over time, but the underlying charge percentages do not reconcile with the primary suitability report table.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested, which in monetary terms is £3,305.55 with current transfer values. This fee is paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer. An ongoing annual adviser fee of 1% per annum is charged on fund value, equating to circa £1,101.85 for the first year based on current values and expected contributions minus the initial fee. Both fees are stated to be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme. The report specifies the services provided for the ongoing fee: reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss, reviewing ongoing suitability of the investment portfolio, market updates and legislative changes, access to a professional advisory team for ad-hoc advice, and access to full holistic financial planning.
    File:     The client agreement [P12] confirms fees are set out in the suitability report and subsequent charges confirmation, requiring client approval by express consent or written approval. The agreement states two principal fees apply to investments: a fee based on work to arrange the initial investment, and a fee for ongoing reviews. General terms [P13] specify the typical initial advice fee is 3% of the value of funds advised on, with a minimum initial advice fee of £1,350 for Pensions and Investment advice. The ongoing fee is 1% per annum as stated in service documents [P3] covering review meetings at least annually, statements twice per year, cash flow analysis, family benefit advice, market updates, quarterly e-magazine, online portfolio access, and ad-hoc adviser access. The client agreement [P12] confirms that fees are to be paid as set out in the suitability report. Terms [P2] clarify that if a client becomes non-advised while fees are being paid over time, the client remains responsible for outstanding adviser charges. Passage [P5] from the suitability report itself reiterates both fees: 3% initial (£3,305.55) and 1% ongoing (£1,101.85 first year).
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states the initial fee is £3,305.55, calculated as 3% of current transfer values (£116,998.47). However, the stated figure £3,305.55 as 3% implies a base of £110,185 approximately, not the stated total transfer value. Arithmetic check: 3% × £116,998.47 = £3,509.95, not £3,305.55. The derived figure from the canonical model shows the Aviva platform investment management charge of £253.43 at 0.22% implies a fund value of £115,195.45, and the platform charge of £231.39 at 0.21% implies £110,185.71, neither of which is the full £116,998.47 total of all arrangements.
      - The ongoing fee is stated as 1% of fund value, circa £1,101.85 for the first year 'based on current values and expected contributions, minus the initial fee'. The derived figure shows that £1,101.85 at 1% implies a fund value of £110,185. However, this does not match the current total transfer value of £116,998.47. The discrepancy arises because the ongoing fee calculation appears to exclude certain arrangements or uses a different valuation basis than the total stated transfer value.
      - No client agreement document establishing the specific maximum fee percentage or monetary cap is provided in the evidence pack. While general fee terms appear in TFAS marketing documents [P13] stating 3% with a minimum of £1,350, and ongoing 1% as standard, these are generic terms and not a signed client-specific agreement confirming the fees charged in this case.
    The report asserts an initial fee of 3% (£3,305.55) and an ongoing fee of 1% per annum (£1,101.85 first year). The percentage rates themselves are corroborated: 3% is supported by [P1], [P12], and [P13]; 1% is supported by [P1], [P3], and [P5]. However, critical arithmetic inconsistencies emerge when checking the monetary amounts against the stated transfer value basis. The initial fee of £3,305.55 does not equal 3% of the stated total transfer value of £116,998.47 (which would be £3,509.95). The derived figures in the canonical model show the Aviva platform charges (0.21% and 0.22%) imply fund values around £110,185, suggesting the fee calculation may be based on a subset of arrangements or a different valuation point, yet the report claims it is based on 'current transfer values' without clarifying which values or whether some arrangements are excluded. The ongoing fee basis is similarly opaque: £1,101.85 at 1% implies a £110,185 fund value, not the £116,998.47 total. The report qualifies this as 'based on current values and expected contributions, minus the initial fee', which provides some explanation for the variance but lacks transparency about exactly which arrangements are included in the ongoing fee calculation or whether the fee applies to all consolidated pensions or only the Aviva solution. The services described for the ongoing fee are disclosed and appear aligned with typical ongoing adviser service standards [P3], [P5], [P12]. However, without a signed client-specific agreement confirming the exact fee rates, maximums, and which arrangement values they are calculated on, the consistency of the stated fees with the client agreement cannot be fully established. The generic TFAS terms support the percentages but not the specific monetary application to this client's situation. No evidence shows these specific fees were agreed in writing by this client, though the general standards suggest they fall within normal parameters. The guard regarding agreements stating maximums is relevant here: the general documents suggest 3% with a £1,350 minimum and 1% ongoing are standard, but no document confirms these were the agreed terms for this specific client or caps on what could be charged.

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report presents RIY and critical yield figures across three growth scenarios (Low, Mid, High) at age 75. For the recommended solution under mid-growth (2.95%), RIY is stated as 2.2%, critical yield as 4.8%, and effect of switching as £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. The report also states: 'Like for Like: our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' and 'Including Adviser Fee: our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans.' The report discloses platform charge 0.21%, investment management charge 0.22%, total charge 0.44%, with monetary estimates £231.39, £253.43, and £484.81 respectively. For existing arrangements, Standard Life (EA5) is shown as 0.52% fund charge on the arrangements table. On the Pension Switch Charges assessment (page 11), Standard Life shows 'Current Annual Recurring Charge of Existing Plan' as 0.18% and 'Impact of Recommended Alternative' as -0.26% with monetary term £268.94.
    File:     Evidence [P1] corroborates the RIY percentages (0.3% existing low, 2.1% recommended low; 0.3% existing mid, 2.2% recommended mid; 0.3% existing high, 2.3% recommended high) and critical yields (1.9% low scenario, 4.8% mid scenario, 7.8% high scenario), effect of switching values (£10,400 low, £12,000 mid, £15,000 high) and percentages (9.6%, 9.4%, 10.0%). Evidence [P3] and [P18] show Aviva platform charge 0.21%, investment-related charges for CT Universal MAP Balanced 0.29% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 0.18%, with weighted average 0.23%. Evidence [P19] confirms total charge 0.44% composed of 0.21% platform and 0.22% investment management. Evidence [P21] shows Standard Life existing charge as 0.52% (matching page 5 of report). However, the derived figures computed from the canonical model reveal: (1) Aviva platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value £110,185.71, which matches neither any single arrangement nor the consolidated total; (2) Aviva investment management charge £253.43 at 0.22% implies fund value £115,195.45, again matching no arrangement; (3) the annual charge difference £268.94 at 0.26% implies fund value £103,438.46, which is Zurich's current value, not Standard Life's; (4) total NP1 ongoing £484.81 at 0.44% implies fund value £110,184.09, matching no value; (5) Standard Life EA5 row in the comparison on page 11 uses a £268.94 figure computed on Zurich's value (£103,438.46), meaning the Standard Life row is calculated on the wrong plan entirely.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Standard Life existing charge 0.52% (page 5 arrangements table); same plan shown as 0.18% Current Annual Recurring Charge on page 11 Pension Switch Charges table — these cannot both be the full recurring charge
      - Report's Aviva platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% calculates to fund value £110,185.71, which does not match any single existing arrangement value or the consolidated total of £116,998.47
      - Report's Aviva investment management charge £253.43 at 0.22% calculates to fund value £115,195.45, matching no arrangement or total
      - Report states £268.94 impact at 0.26% for Standard Life (EA5) on page 11; this figure at 0.26% calculates to fund value £103,438.46, which is Zurich's (EA4) current value, not Standard Life's £3,002.00 — the row is computed on the wrong plan
      - Report's total NP1 ongoing charge £484.81 at 0.44% calculates to fund value £110,184.09, which matches neither any arrangement nor the consolidated £116,998.47
      - Report states 'Like for Like: 0.24% less expensive' and immediately below 'Including Adviser Fee: 4.24% more expensive' — same recommendation is simultaneously cheaper and more expensive depending on adviser fees, indicating the cost comparison is incomplete without explicit reconciliation of both layers
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Explicit charge-by-charge reconciliation showing how the 0.44% total charge (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management) applies to the consolidated transfer value of £116,998.47 or to the individual arrangement values being consolidated, Clarification of whether the £268.94 comparison row for Standard Life should have been calculated on Standard Life's £3,002.00 value (implying 0.26% cost = £7.81 difference) or whether the row actually references Zurich, Aggregated all-in cost figure showing cumulative adviser fees (£3,305 initial + 1.00% ongoing), product charges, platform charges, and fund charges combined, with net cost-benefit analysis across the projection horizon, Documented adviser fee schedule confirming whether the 1.00% ongoing charge stated in the report matches any client agreement or terms of engagement document not provided in this pack, Detailed reconciliation of the critical yield requirement (4.8% mid scenario) back to the specific charge layers and switching costs, demonstrating how this figure was derived from the charge data
    The requirement is not met. Multiple material inconsistencies prevent reliable comparison of cumulative cost impact. First, the Standard Life charge contradiction: the report states 0.52% on page 5 but uses 0.18% on page 11. These cannot both represent the full recurring charge, and no explanation is given for the divergence. The extraction metadata notes this ambiguity but does not resolve it. Second, the derived figures reveal systematic misalignment between stated monetary charges and the fund values they purport to apply to. The platform and investment management charges are quoted in pounds but calculate backwards to fund values (£110k-£115k) that do not correspond to any arrangement or total in the case. This suggests either the charges were estimated on a different basis or the denominator changed between calculation and quotation without disclosure. Third, and most serious, the Standard Life comparison row on page 11 uses the £268.94 figure, which at the stated 0.26% difference calculates to Zurich's value (£103,438.46), not Standard Life's (£3,002.00). This means the report has compared Standard Life against the new solution using Zurich's fund size as the base. This is arithmetically impossible and renders that row's conclusions (£268.94 saving, cost reduction) unreliable. Fourth, the report simultaneously states that the recommendation is 0.24% cheaper 'like for like' but 4.24% more expensive 'including adviser fee', without reconciling these contradictory conclusions or explaining the bridge. The critical yield of 4.8% is stated but its derivation from the charge layers is not transparent. The guard permits real-terms and nominal comparisons to differ, and illustration growth rates may differ from cashflow assumptions; neither applies here. The discrepancies are not rooted in different projection methodologies but in charge figures that do not reconcile with the values they should apply to. The cumulative impact — the clearest expression of long-term cost required by this check — is not presented as an aggregated figure with clear pathway from existing charges through adviser fees to the all-in cost of the recommendation. Instead, piecemeal figures are given with internal contradictions and computational errors that undermine confidence in the total.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts a 'Like for Like' cost comparison showing the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than current plans (page 11), stating 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' and 'your proposed plan can underperform your current plan by £264 in the first year'. Per-arrangement comparisons are provided: EA3 (Aviva) -0.06% impact (-£2.25); EA4 (Zurich) -0.49% impact (-£506.85); EA5 (Standard Life) +0.26% impact (+£268.94). Existing annual charges are stated as: EA3 0.50% (£18.72), EA4 0.93% (£961.98), EA5 0.52% (£186.19). The recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension has total recurring charges of 0.44% comprising platform 0.21% (£231.39) and investment management 0.22% (£253.43). Existing charges for all five arrangements are detailed on page 5: People's Pension 0.50% fund + £4.50 admin; Scottish Widows 0.19% fund + 0.10% platform; Aviva 0.50% fund; Zurich 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin; Standard Life 0.52% fund.
    File:     Evidence from provider documentation and the pension switch report show: (1) Zurich Policy Info [P2] states annual management charge of ¾% (0.75%) per year, not 0.93%; (2) Standard Life Policy Illustration [P8] shows fund charges of 1.0% with -0.5% discounts = 0.5% net, not 0.52%; (3) Pension Switch Report [P13] shows critical yield of 6.3% (not stated in main report comparison), reduction in yield 0.6% existing and 0.6% new, and 'Effect of switching' as £3,000 or 2.1% decrease at mid-growth, contradicting the like-for-like saving; (4) SIPP Research Report [P24] shows CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund total aggregated cost as 0.37% (0.24% ongoing + 0.13% transaction costs), not matching the 0.22% investment management stated; (5) Peoples Pension Policy Info [P7] confirms 0.50% management charge but this is net of rebates that vary with pot size — the report does not disclose this variable nature or the rebate structure, treating it as a fixed percentage.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states EA4 (Zurich) existing charge as 0.93%; Zurich Policy Info [P2] evidences annual management charge of 0.75% (¾%). Difference of 0.18 percentage points — a material discrepancy in a like-for-like comparison.
      - Report states EA5 (Standard Life) existing charge as 0.52%; Standard Life Policy Illustration [P8] shows fund charges of 1.0% with discounts of -0.5%, netting to 0.5%, not 0.52%. The report's figure cannot be derived from the stated components and does not match the illustration.
      - Report asserts a like-for-like saving of -0.24% overall and -£264 in year one; Pension Switch Report [P13] shows 'Effect of switching' as -£3,000 or -2.1% decrease at mid-growth rate (2.95%), indicating the new solution underperforms, not outperforms. The direction and magnitude contradict.
      - Report states Aviva Platform investment management charge as 0.22% (£253.43); SIPP Research Report [P24] shows CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund (one of two recommended funds) with total aggregated cost of 0.37%, including 0.24% ongoing costs and 0.13% transaction costs. The 0.22% figure does not align with the fund's documented cost structure.
      - Report states People's Pension charge as 0.50% on page 5 without disclosure that this is subject to rebates that vary with pot size [P7]. The charge is presented as a fixed percentage when it is variable, obscuring the true cost.
      - Report per-arrangement impact for EA4: -0.49% (-£506.85). Zurich current value £103,439.24; at 0.93% stated charge, annual cost would be £961.98 (matches report); at 0.75% actual charge per [P2], annual cost would be £775.79. The impact calculation cannot be verified as internally consistent because the basis charge is wrong.
      - Report per-arrangement impact for EA5: +0.26% (+£268.94). Standard Life current value £3,002; at 0.52% stated charge, annual cost would be £156.10, not £186.19. The £186.19 figure quoted (per page 11) does not reconcile to the stated 0.52% of £3,002, indicating either the charge percentage or the monetary amount is transposed or erroneous.
      - Derived figures analysis: EA5 impact amount £268.94 at 0.26% implies a fund value of £103,438.46, which is Zurich's value, not Standard Life's (£3,002). The row for EA5 is computed on the wrong plan's value. This is a material error that renders the EA5 comparison unreliable.
      - Monetary vs. percentage internal inconsistency for EA3: £18.72 at 0.50% implies fund value of £3,744; Aviva current value is £3,744.36. This is consistent. However, the figure of -£2.25 impact represents only 0.06% of this, and if the recommendation removes all 0.50%, the impact should be -£18.72, not -£2.25. The impact is understated by a factor of 8.3x.
      - Comparison basis inconsistency: Page 11 states 'Like for Like: 0.24% less expensive' and separately 'Including Adviser Fee: 4.24% more expensive'. The same recommendation cannot be simultaneously cheaper and more expensive on a like-for-like basis. This indicates the comparison is not on a consistent basis throughout, though both scenarios may be valid separately.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Standard Life existing charge basis: the report states 0.52% but the illustration shows 1.0% fund charge with -0.5% discount; the precise charge methodology is unclear from the evidence provided., Recommended fund charges for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio: the report states platform 0.21% and investment management 0.22% totaling 0.44%, but provider documentation for this fund is not in the evidence pack. Only CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund costs are documented [P24]., Adviser fee basis: the report states an initial 3% fee of £3,305.55 [P23] but does not disclose whether this is included in the 'Like for Like' comparison or only in the 'Including Adviser Fee' scenario. The £4.24% cost increase stated when adviser fees are included is not reconciled to the £3,305.55 or any ongoing fee., Variable rebate structure for People's Pension: [P7] confirms rebates vary with pot size, but the precise rebate applicable to this client's pot (£6,601.55) is not stated in the evidence., Transaction costs: the report [P3] explicitly excludes transaction costs, stating they are 'variable and subject to change' and 'already accounted for in the fund's performance figures'. However, the SIPP Research Report [P24] includes transaction costs (0.13%) in the total aggregated fund cost. Whether transaction costs should be included in the comparison is not clarified.
    A cost comparison cannot reliably be performed because multiple material figures in the report's cost table do not reconcile to provider documentation or are computed on incorrect plan values. The guard 'Recompute' identifies three critical failures: (1) EA4 Zurich charge stated as 0.93% but evidenced as 0.75% — a transposition or error affecting the entire EA4 comparison; (2) EA5 Standard Life charge of 0.52% stated but the monetary amount £186.19 does not reconcile to £3,002 fund value, implying a £35,805.77 fund value was used (as computed analysis shows); (3) EA5 impact amount £268.94 at 0.26% implies Zurich's value (£103,438.46), not Standard Life's, confirming the row is computed on the wrong plan. These are not rounding differences or minor inconsistencies; they are structural errors in the comparison table. The guard 'Where the report states the same plan's charge in two of its own tables' requires checking whether EA5 charge of 0.52% on page 5 agrees with the 0.52% on page 11: both report the same figure, so the internal consistency check passes, but this masks the fact that the 0.52% itself may be wrong (no provider illustration for Standard Life current plan in the evidence confirms 0.52%; [P8] Standard Life illustration for a different scenario shows 1.0% before discounts). The report's own derivations contradict the switch analysis: page 11 states like-for-like saving of 0.24%, yet [P13] Pension Switch Report shows effect of switching as -£3,000 (9.4% decrease at mid-growth). The guard 'Comparisons on different bases (with and without adviser fees)' applies: the report does offer both, but the bases are not clearly separated in the initial assertion, and the £264 'like-for-like' saving is undermined by the switch report showing loss. The report does not disclose that People's Pension charges are subject to variable rebates [P7], which materially affects the accuracy of the 0.50% comparison figure. The absence of provider documentation for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio means half the recommended fund allocation cannot be verified against the 0.22% investment management charge stated. The report explicitly excludes transaction costs yet [P24] shows CT Universal MAP Balanced includes 0.13% transaction cost; whether this should be included changes the 0.24% fund cost to 0.37%, a material difference of 0.13 percentage points. The like-for-like comparison of -0.24% is contradicted by the switch report's effect of switching showing loss, indicating the comparison is not truly comparing equivalent bases or is arithmetically unsound. The report's internal statement that the plan 'can underperform your current plan by £264' is internally contradictory: if the plan is 0.24% cheaper, it should outperform (after inflation and growth), yet this phrasing suggests underperformance. The critical yield requirement of 4.8% [P19] to match the existing plan after adviser fees confirms the recommendation requires outperformance, not underperformance. The comparison is unreliable due to wrong plan values, wrong charge percentages, variable charges undisclosed, and contradictions between the cost comparison and the switch analysis.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts five client-specific benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower Costs — 'The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension, meaning more of your savings remain invested for growth rather than being lost to charges' (page 7); (2) Lifestyling avoidance — existing lifestyling funds will drift outside agreed risk range, avoided by the recommendation; (3) Greater Investment Choice — wider range of options better suited to risk profile and retirement goals (page 7); (4) Adviser Charging Facilitation — 'The new scheme allows adviser charges to be deducted directly, ensuring ongoing financial planning support' (page 7); (5) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments — adviser will actively monitor and adjust investments (page 7). The report also states generic benefits about Aviva's financial strength (A rating, £407bn AUM), fund choice (6,900+ funds), investment expertise, transparent charges, online convenience, and governance. On page 11, the report presents a switching analysis comparing costs: 'Like for Like: our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans.' However, immediately below: 'Including Adviser Fee: our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans.' The report discloses an initial adviser charge of £3,305.00 and an ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% (page 11). A critical yield of 4.8% per annum is stated as required to offset the adviser fees and make the switch neutral (page 11, Appendix).
    File:     The evidence pack contains multiple sources with charge and benefit information. Pension switch report (P14) shows: at mid growth rate (2.95%), projected value at age 75 is £127,000 (existing) vs £125,000 (new), a decrease of £2,000 or 1.9%; at high growth rate (5.88%), values are £150,000 (existing) vs £147,000 (new), a decrease of £3,000 or 2.0%. Critical yield for new solution shown as 0.3% (mid growth) and 3.2% (high growth) in the comparison table. The report's own performance data (P1, P9) shows: Aviva existing fund (My Future Focus Consolidation) 5-year cumulative return 15.58%; Zurich Managed fund 5-year return 44.32%; Standard Life At Retirement Universal 5-year return 24.56%. Recommended funds show: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 40.06% (5-year, P3, P7); CT Universal MAP Balanced 44.73% (5-year, P3). The recommended funds have NOT outperformed the existing Zurich holding (44.32% vs 40.06% and 44.73%). The report itself states (P1, P2): 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds.' The Zurich arrangement has a current value of £103,439.24, making it by far the largest holding. The derived figures show the Aviva Platform Personal Pension charge calculations are internally inconsistent: platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value £110,185.71; investment management charge £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195.45; total ongoing £484.81 at 0.44% implies £110,184.09 — none of these match any actual arrangement value or the aggregate total of £116,998.47. The Standard Life charge is stated as 0.52% on page 5 but 0.18% on page 11, and the EA5 impact row appears to be computed on Zurich's value (£103,438.46) not Standard Life's (£3,002.00). The fund recommendation table (P8) shows two funds at 50/50 allocation with charges of 0.18% (HSBC) and 0.29% (CT), totalling an average of 0.235%, yet the switch comparison uses different figures.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' as a primary benefit, but switching analysis shows costs INCREASE by 4.24% including adviser fees, and projected values DECREASE at both mid and high growth rates, contradicting the benefit claim
      - Report states Like-for-Like cost saving of 0.24%, but this excludes the adviser fee entirely; when adviser fees are included the plan becomes 4.24% MORE expensive, making the initial benefit statement misleading
      - Report claims recommended funds have 'performed well in comparison with their peers' and asserts competitive charges, but Zurich existing fund (44.32% over 5 years) has outperformed both recommended funds (HSBC 40.06%, CT 44.73%), contradicting the performance justification for switching the largest holding
      - Standard Life existing charge stated as 0.52% on page 5 (arrangements table) but 0.18% on page 11 (Pension Switch Charges table), a 0.34% discrepancy in the same document used for cost comparison
      - Recommended investment charges (0.235% blended average of 0.18% + 0.29%) do not reconcile with platform charge calculations showing 0.21%, investment management 0.22%, and total 0.44%, creating arithmetic inconsistency in the cost basis for recommendations
      - Report states benefit of 'Greater Investment Choice' and access to 6,900+ funds, but existing Zurich holding (highest performer, £103k) is being replaced with Aviva's multi-asset funds (Balanced risk 5), narrowing actual choice to a single 50/50 portfolio allocation
      - Critical yield of 4.8% per annum required to justify the switch is substantially higher than mid-term growth rate (2.95%) and even exceeds the high growth rate (5.88%) assumption used in projections, meaning the switch is unlikely to break even on stated assumptions
      - Report claims adviser fees are a convenience benefit ('ensuring ongoing financial planning support without the need for separate payments'), but the ongoing 1.00% charge is a material cost increase that must be outperformed annually, not a benefit
      - Performance comparison on page 2 (P2) explicitly states 'they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds,' directly contradicting the 'well performed' assertion used to justify the recommendation
    The core tension is between the report's assertion of 'Lower Costs' as a primary benefit and the switching analysis that proves costs increase materially when adviser fees are included. The report discloses both scenarios but does not reconcile the contradiction or clearly explain that the switch REQUIRES 4.8% annual outperformance just to match existing value. The comparison can be performed because charge figures, growth assumptions, and projected values are all stated. However, the charge calculations are internally incoherent: the platform and investment management charges shown (0.21% + 0.22% + facility charge implicit = 0.44%) do not equal the blended fund charges stated in recommendations (0.235%), and the EA5 impact row uses Zurich's value not Standard Life's, making that row unreliable for the cost comparison. The benefit of 'Lower Costs' is directly contradicted by the switching analysis. Like-for-like (without adviser fees) the plan is 0.24% cheaper, but this comparison omits the £3,305 initial fee and 1.00% ongoing fee, which are material. The report discloses these fees but does not prominently state that including them reverses the cost saving. The performance justification is also undermined: the report's own data shows the Zurich holding outperformed both recommended funds over 5 years, and the report itself acknowledges 'they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' — yet the largest holding (Zurich, £103k, 44.32% performance) is being switched to funds that underperformed it (HSBC 40.06%, CT 44.73%). The report asserts that recommended funds 'remain aligned with your agreed risk tolerance' and states this rationale for the switch, but this is not a benefit relative to cost; it is a risk-management assertion that does not justify increased costs or lower projected values. The adviser charging benefit is described as 'convenience' but is in fact a cost structure that requires annual outperformance to recoup. The report does not clearly state that the client must achieve 4.8% growth per annum just to avoid net detriment. The vulnerability overlay: the extraction notes record no actual or potential drivers of vulnerability affecting this assessment. The fact find shows the client has modest income (£1,430/month net, £1,200 employed + £230 state pension), expenditure exceeds income by £288/month (P21), and the recommendation will charge ongoing 1.00% plus transaction costs, which may place material pressure on an already deficit cashflow. However, the adviser charging rationale and flexibility to adjust do not materially mitigate this; they are restated as benefits when they are features of the platform that come at cost. Summary: The report claims a cost benefit that its own switching analysis contradicts once adviser fees are included. The performance benefit is contradicted by the report's own data showing Zurich outperformance. The cost figures are arithmetically inconsistent across the document. The required outperformance to break even (4.8% p.a.) is not clearly highlighted as a critical assumption.

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states two contradictory positions on cost comparison: (1) 'Like for Like' the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than current plans (page 11); (2) 'Including Adviser Fee' the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans (page 11). The report quantifies the required outperformance as 4.24% per annum to justify the switch when adviser fees are included.
    File:     No supporting documents were retrieved to corroborate or contradict the cost comparison figures. The assessment is confined to internal consistency of the suitability report itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report presents two cost comparisons that point in opposite directions: -0.24% (cheaper without adviser fees) and +4.24% (more expensive with adviser fees). While both figures can coexist, the report does not explain why a client should accept a 4.24% annual cost increase or what benefits justify that outperformance requirement.
      - The derived figure calculations reveal that the platform charge of £231.39 at 0.21% implies a fund value of £110,185.71, which matches neither the total recommended fund value nor any single arrangement. Similarly, the investment management charge of £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195.45, which also does not reconcile with stated values.
      - The 'required outperformance' figure of £4,672.00 at 4.24% derives from a fund value of £110,188.68, which matches no stated arrangement value or total. The 5 arrangements sum to £116,998.47, creating an arithmetic inconsistency.
      - The report states the annual charge difference is £268.94 at 0.26%, which derives from Zurich's current value (£103,438.46). However, this comparison row in the switching analysis (page 11, Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment) is labelled as applying to Standard Life (EA5), whose current value is £3,002.00. The calculation appears to be performed on the wrong arrangement.
    The comparison can be performed because both cost figures (0.24% and 4.24%) are stated in the report itself. The report correctly discloses that without adviser fees the recommendation is cheaper, but with adviser fees it becomes more expensive. However, the report does not adequately explain the value or benefits that would justify the 4.24% annual cost increase, nor does it address whether the critical yield (4.8% per annum, stated in Appendix) is realistic for a client aged 55 with balanced risk rating 5 and modest means. The derived figure calculations expose a more serious flaw: the cost arithmetic does not reconcile. The platform and investment management charges, when reversed from percentages to fund values, do not match the stated total fund value of £116,998.47 or the recommended Aviva total of approximately £110,000 (implied but not explicitly stated as a single figure). The 'required outperformance' calculation rests on a fund value (£110,188.68) that is neither the current total nor the recommended total. The switching analysis row for Standard Life (EA5) appears to use Zurich's fund value in its calculation (£103,438.46), suggesting a data mapping error. These arithmetic failures undermine confidence that the cost comparison itself is sound. The report does not explain whether the benefits of consolidation, platform access, or investment strategy justify the ongoing 0.44% charge on the new Aviva plan relative to the blended charge across five separate existing plans. No evidence of service proposition, platform benefits, or investment performance advantage is cited to support the higher-cost recommendation when adviser fees are included.

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that alternatives to the recommended Aviva Platform solution were considered and rejected. For Aviva (existing plan), the report states 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR. Discounted switch.' For Zurich, the report notes 'Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds' and their alternative blend 'have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally. For these reasons we would not recommend an internal fund switch.' For Standard Life, it states 'Discounted as all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva.' Scottish Widows is described as lacking 'a single multi-asset fund that aligns with your risk rating of 5' and the report recommends encashing it. The report also states 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' for both Zurich and Standard Life recommendations.
    File:     The evidence shows multiple cost comparisons in the pension switch report. P2 and P5 present projected maturity value comparisons across multiple scenarios (low, mid, high growth rates). P8 shows the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension charges at 0.44% total (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management). P2 compares the new solution (0.6%) to retained Aviva plan (0.59%) and Zurich (0.29%), with a total projected maturity of £127,000. P8 states 'Like for Like: ...our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' but 'Including Adviser Fee: ...our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans.' P1 shows the new solution day one value as £106,880.60 versus existing pension of £110,185.60, a shortfall of £3,305.00. P10 and P11 provide narrative justifications citing fund range, lifestyling concerns, investment flexibility, retirement options, and adviser charging facilitation. The file notes (P18) document why alternatives were rejected: Scottish Widows discounted for 'no servicing and reduced flexibility'; Aviva alternative discounted as only risk-4 fund available; Zurich blend of 7 and 2 funds 'have not performed as well'; Standard Life funds 'more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims lower overall cost structure for the Aviva recommendation, but the evidence shows the recommendation is 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees (P8)
      - The report states Standard Life funds are 'more expensive' but P8 shows Standard Life existing charge of 0.52% (page 5) versus recommended Aviva at 0.44% total, a difference of only 0.08%, not a clear cost advantage
      - Zurich existing charge stated as 0.93% (EA4, P2) but the recommendation at 0.6% represents a saving, yet the report emphasises non-cost reasons for the switch (fund range, flexibility)
      - The immediate impact of the switch is a shortfall of £3,305.00 in death benefit (P1), but this negative financial consequence is not addressed in the cost-benefit analysis
      - The like-for-like comparison shows 0.24% savings without adviser fees, but the critical yield of 4.8% (per P8 reference to Appendix) is required to break even, which is not clearly compared to projected growth rates
      - No comparative cost analysis is provided for Scottish Widows encashment (£211.32, receiving £170 net per P7) versus retention or alternative consolidation options
    The requirement asks whether alternatives provide better cost or benefit. The report identifies alternatives considered (Aviva internal switch, Zurich internal switch, Standard Life, Scottish Widows) and rejects them with stated reasons: fund range/risk alignment, performance, cost, and flexibility. The comparative cost evidence shows: (1) Like-for-like recurring charges: Aviva 0.44% recommended versus Zurich 0.93% existing (0.49% saving) and Standard Life 0.52% existing (0.08% saving). (2) Including adviser fees: Aviva becomes 4.24% more expensive, meaning the client pays more in total cost despite lower ongoing charges. (3) Immediate impact: switching incurs a £3,305 death benefit shortfall with no articulated replacement benefit. (4) For Scottish Widows, the report recommends encashment for non-cost reasons (fund range, withdrawal flexibility) but does not cost-compare this against retention or transfer to the Aviva platform. The non-cost reasons given (risk alignment, flexibility, performance) are supported by evidence (P18 file notes, P10/P11 narrative). However, the report's headline claim of 'Lower Costs' is contradicted by the adviser fee analysis. The guard permits rejection of cheaper alternatives for evidenced non-cost reasons; here, the report rejects Zurich internal switch (0.93% vs 0.44% = cheaper) on performance and flexibility grounds (supported by P18: 'blend of funds have not performed as well' and 'main priority...consolidate...be ready for retirement with all the options'). Standard Life funds are rejected as 'more expensive' but the actual difference (0.52% vs 0.44%) is marginal and the real driver is fund range constraints. Scottish Widows encashment is not cost-compared; the decision is driven by fund availability, not cost analysis. The comparative cost research exists but does not clearly support the 'Lower Costs' narrative when adviser fees are included. The critical yield of 4.8% required to justify the switch is mentioned but not compared to realistic growth assumptions in the switching report's own projections (low 0%, mid 2%, high 4% per P2). The maturity value projections (P2, P5) show that at the high growth rate of 4%, the new solution still underperforms the existing plan for Zurich alone (page 11, Appendix would show this, but the specific figures in the truncated text are not visible). The analysis supports rejection of alternatives on non-cost grounds (risk alignment, flexibility, performance) but contradicts the stated 'Lower Costs' benefit when adviser fees are properly accounted for.

  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts RIY and critical yield figures across three growth-rate scenarios (Low 0.00%, Mid 2.95%, High 5.88%), all projected to age 75. For the Low scenario: existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%, effect of switching £10,400 or 9.6% decrease. For the Mid scenario: existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%, effect of switching £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. For the High scenario: existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%, effect of switching £15,000 or 10.0% decrease. The report states (page 18) that based on mid-growth of 2.94% including adviser fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per annum to meet the same value as the current plan, and asserts this is achievable based on past fund performance.
    File:     The Pension Switch Report (P2, the principal supporting illustration) presents an identical cost-and-yield comparison table for the same three scenarios, with matching RIY, critical yield, and switching effect figures at the identical growth rates and retirement age. The table footnotes (P2) define RIY as 'the impact of product, platform and fund charges on the investment growth rate', critical yield as 'the growth rate required from the new solution to match the projected maturity value of the existing pension', and effect of switching as 'the change in the maturity value of the new solution assuming the fund experiences the same investment growth'. A second Pension Switch Report version (P1) presents a different comparison table with materially lower RIY figures for the recommended solution (0.6% Low, 0.6% Mid, 0.6% High) and correspondingly lower switching impacts (£2,000 or 1.9% Low, £2,000 or 1.6% Mid, £3,000 or 2.0% High). The Aviva platform illustration (P13) shows cumulative cost impact reducing projected value from £129,000 before all charges to £115,000 after all charges over the full pension term, representing a reduction in growth rate from 2.9% to 0.8%, a reduction of 2.1% — which aligns with the recommended RIY of 2.1% in the report's Low scenario (P17), not the Mid-scenario RIY of 2.2%. Fund-level charges are stated in P12 as CT Universal MAP Balanced 0.29% OCF and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced 0.18% OCF, weighted average 0.23%, plus Aviva platform charge 0.21%, totalling 0.44% ongoing.
    Discrepancies:
      - Two different supporting illustrations exist with materially different RIY and switching-impact figures for the same recommendation: P1 shows recommended RIY of 0.6% (all scenarios), P2 shows recommended RIY of 2.1% (Low), 2.2% (Mid), 2.3% (High). The report's assertions match P2 exactly, not P1.
      - The report's Mid-scenario critical yield of 4.8% is supported by P2 and P16, and the report states this is required to 'meet the same value as your current plan'. However, the Aviva illustration (P13) shows that product and investment charges reduce projected growth from 2.9% to 0.8%, a reduction of 2.1%, not 2.2%. The 2.1% figure appears in the Low-scenario recommended RIY (P17), creating internal misalignment between scenarios.
      - The report's assertion that the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per annum to match the existing plan value (page 16, P16) relies on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%. The Aviva illustration (P13) assumes 2.9% growth before charges. The 0.04% difference is immaterial to the critical yield outcome but signals potential rounding or source divergence.
      - The switching effect figures (£10,400, £12,000, £15,000 in the report; matching exactly in P2 and P17) cannot be arithmetically verified from the fund values provided. The derived figures note that the fund values used to calculate charge percentages do not match the totals: the Aviva platform charge of £231.39 at 0.21% implies a fund value of £110,185.71, which matches neither the individual arrangement values nor the total £116,998.47. This undermines confidence in the switching-effect calculations.
      - P1 and P2 both state growth assumptions are 0.00%, 2.95%, and 5.88%, but P1 also references additional scenarios ('Scottish Widows only', 'Zurich only', 'Standard Life only') with different figures, whereas the report asserts a single unified comparison. The evidence contains multiple switching scenarios, but the report does not explain which existing arrangements are being compared in the headline figures.
    The comparison can be performed because RIY and critical yield figures from the report (P17, P16) are directly stated in the supporting Pension Switch Report (P2). However, a critical problem emerges: two different versions of the pension switch illustration exist (P1 and P2), presenting fundamentally different cost profiles for the same recommendation. P1 shows a recommended RIY of only 0.6% across all scenarios, implying minimal cost impact; P2 shows 2.1%–2.3%, implying substantial cost impact. The report's figures align exactly with P2, but P1 is equally present in the evidence pack and presents a contradictory cost picture. This is not a rounding difference or a scenario distinction — it is a material variance (0.6% vs 2.1% in the Low scenario) in the core cost metric that drives the switching recommendation. The guard states that RIY should be compared only at the same growth basis, and that critical yield figures must be at the same retirement age. Both conditions are met here: all figures are at age 75 and aligned by growth-rate scenario. However, the guard does not excuse two different supporting illustrations with conflicting results. The report cites one (implicitly, through exact figure matching with P2) but does not address the existence of P1 or explain why its lower cost figures should be disregarded. Additionally, the Aviva platform illustration (P13) states that 'All charges could reduce this pension fund from £129,000.00 to £115,000.00 — this is the same as reducing the investment growth after inflation from 2.9% to 0.8%. This is a reduction in the growth rate of 2.1%.' This 2.1% figure matches the report's Low-scenario recommended RIY (P17), not the Mid-scenario 2.2% or High-scenario 2.3%. The Mid scenario, which carries the critical yield of 4.8% and is described in the report as the primary basis for the recommendation (P16: 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%...'), does not align with any illustration's stated all-in cost reduction. The derived figures show that the Aviva platform charge of 0.21% and investment charges of 0.22% sum to 0.43%, which rounds to 0.44% (stated in P12 and consistent with the report's recommendation summary), but this total does not reconcile with the 2.2% RIY claimed for the Mid scenario in P17. A 0.44% platform-and-fund charge cannot produce a 2.2% reduction in yield unless additional costs (adviser fees, transfer costs, or opportunity costs) are embedded in the RIY figure without disclosure. The report states (P16) that the required outperformance is £4,672 in monetary terms, which the derived figures confirm is computed at 4.24% against a fund value of £110,188.68 — close to but not exactly matching any single existing arrangement. This suggests the comparison may be aggregating multiple plans, but the report does not clearly state which arrangements are being switched. The presence of two different switch-comparison illustrations (P1 and P2), the misalignment between the Mid-scenario RIY (2.2%) and the Aviva platform illustration's all-in cost reduction (2.1%), and the ambiguity around which existing arrangements form the comparison baseline all point to incomplete or inconsistent documentation. The report's own figures are internally consistent with P2 and P17, but the evidence pack contains a contradictory first version (P1) that is not reconciled or explained.
