  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report discloses an ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.0% per annum (page 17), stated as costing 'circa £1,101.85 for the first year' based on current values and expected contributions, minus the initial fee. The report lists five services included in this ongoing charge: (1) Reviewing your attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure your investment portfolio remains suitable for you; (2) Reviewing the ongoing suitability of your investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and updates with any changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs. The report also states these same five services are covered under adviser fees for 'researching, recommending and arranging these investments' and the ongoing review activities (page 17). Review frequency is not specified in the report.
    File:     The client agreement (P1, P8) confirms an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% per annum (subject to a minimum fee of £100). The agreement describes the service to include: review meeting at least once a year with recommendations which may include fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and attitude to risk; statements and performance reports twice per year; cash flow analysis when relevant; family benefit (adviser fee-free advice for direct family members); regular market updates; quarterly e-magazine; online access for portfolio valuations; access to adviser as needed. The agreement explicitly states 'For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1%' (P1, P8). The charge is automatically taken from funds invested and amounts fluctuate with fund value.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report lists five services but does not specify review frequency; the client agreement specifies 'at least once a year'. The report states the service includes ad-hoc advice access and holistic financial planning, which are mentioned in the agreement but framed differently (the agreement emphasizes annual review meetings as the core service with ad-hoc access available). The report does not mention statements twice per year, cash flow analysis, family benefit, quarterly e-magazine, or online access—all detailed in the agreement. The report does not state a minimum fee of £100; the agreement does. The report frames the services as supporting an ongoing charge but does not itemize all the specific service components the client agreement details.
    The comparison can be made: both documents address the same ongoing 1% annual adviser charge and both describe a service proposition attached to it. The 1% rate and general nature of the service (ongoing reviews, risk review, suitability monitoring, market updates, adviser access) are consistent between the report and the agreement. However, the report's disclosure is materially incomplete. The report states five service elements but omits several concrete service components documented in the client agreement: the frequency of statements (twice per year), cash flow analysis, family benefit, quarterly e-magazine, online portfolio access, and the explicit minimum fee of £100 per annum. The agreement is more detailed and prescriptive about what 'ongoing service' comprises. The report's enumeration of services is not inaccurate but is a high-level summary that does not capture the full scope of benefits described in the binding client agreement. The fact that the agreement specifies 'at least annually' for review meetings is material to understanding the service proposition, as is the minimum fee structure. These omissions do not contradict the report's assertions but they do mean the report does not fully disclose all material aspects of the service proposition. The guard permits that the client agreement and report may name services differently and instructs comparison by substance, not label—on that basis, the substance is aligned, but the report's disclosure is materially incomplete relative to what the agreement (which is the binding document) commits to provide.
      · TFAS Wealth Client Agreement [A] (P8)

      · TFAS Wealth Client Agreement [A] (P8)
        "Review meeting at least once a year and recommendations which may include the following as part of this service; bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and attitude to risk. Statements and Performance Reports twice per year. Cash flow analysis when relevant and beneficial. Family benefit where you can have an adviser fee free advice for direct family members for their ISA or pension set up on top of up to three hours advice for your children. Regular market updates. Quarterly e-magazine. On-line access for portfolio valuations. Access to your adviser should you need us in the meantime."

Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs. The costs for this service are 1% per annum."

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report states on page 7 and elsewhere that the recommendation is justified by: (1) Lower Costs — 'lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension'; (2) Lifestyling — existing funds are lifestyle funds that will drift outside agreed risk range; (3) Greater Investment Choice — 'wider range of investment options'; (4) Adviser Charging Facilitation — 'allows adviser charges to be deducted directly'; (5) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments — 'actively monitor and adjust your investments'. On page 11, the report also states 'Like for Like: ...our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' but immediately contradicts this: 'Including Adviser Fee: ...our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans.' The report claims the critical yield required (4.8% per annum) is achievable based on past performance.
    File:     Evidence shows: (1) Existing Aviva arrangement (EA3, SP50163762) is invested in 'Aviva pension my future focus consolidation' [P19], described as a consolidation/lifestyle-oriented fund. The report recommends switching this same existing Aviva holding to 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' (50%) and 'CT Universal MAP Balanced' (50%) [P22]. (2) Zurich (EA4, P10026-719-001/DL) is documented in the policy literature [P9, P13, P15] as offering 60+ funds and flexible drawdown options. (3) Standard Life (EA5, D2301334000) is documented [P16, P17] as offering 300 funds and allowing servicing by adviser (Yes on page [P19] arrangements table). (4) Aviva Key Features [P4, P8] confirm access to thousands of investment choices, online management, and multiple retirement options including flexi-access drawdown. (5) Charge analysis [P10] shows HSBC Global Strategy Balanced at 0.18% ongoing costs and CT Universal MAP Balanced at 0.29%, totalling 0.37% aggregated fund costs — but this excludes platform charges. (6) The report's own charge calculations [P10] show Standard Life existing charge as 0.52% on page 5, but 0.18% recurring charge on page 11 (Pension Switch Charges table), a direct contradiction. (7) Zurich policy info [P9] states 'Most of our plans give you a wide range of funds to invest in' and 'We don't currently make a charge for switching funds', indicating low-cost flexibility already available. (8) Page 11 Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment shows 'Current Annual Recurring Charge of Existing Plan' for Standard Life as 0.18%, with impact -0.26%, implying recommended plan would be 0.44%, but Aviva Platform Personal Pension on page 10 shows total charge of 0.44% (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment + 0.01% implied). (9) The five existing arrangements total £116,998.47 [derived figures]; Aviva Platform Personal Pension platform charge of £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value of £110,185.71, matching neither individual nor total value. (10) Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, requiring blend of risk 7 and risk 2 [P21]; Standard Life offers 300 funds and allows adviser charging [P19]. (11) Consolidation is stated on page 21 as a priority: 'your main priority is to consolidate your pensions, gain better control over them'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' as benefit but simultaneously states Like for Like the plan is 0.24% cheaper and including adviser fees 4.24% more expensive — these cannot both justify the recommendation
      - Report states existing Aviva (EA3) is in lifestyle fund requiring switching, but existing Aviva arrangement is already on Aviva platform, making it an internal fund switch rather than a consolidation necessity
      - Report cites Greater Investment Choice as benefit, but Standard Life offers 300 funds [P19] and Zurich offers 60+ funds [P9, P19]; these rival or exceed Aviva's offering
      - Report cites Adviser Charging Facilitation as a benefit justifying cost, but Standard Life already allows servicing by adviser (Yes) [P19]
      - Report cites Ongoing Monitoring as a benefit of the recommendation, but monitoring is a service provided regardless of platform choice — it is not a platform-specific feature
      - Charge figures are arithmetically inconsistent: Standard Life stated as 0.52% fund charge on page 5 but 0.18% recurring charge on page 11; EA5 impact row (page 11) computed on Zurich's value (£103,438.46) not Standard Life's (£3,002.00), making the cost comparison unreliable for that arrangement
      - Report claims critical yield of 4.8% is achievable based on past performance, but does not provide evidence of historical performance data for the recommended funds sufficient to support this outcome
      - Consolidation is cited as a benefit and priority, but consolidation convenience is not a financial benefit — it saves time and administration, not money; the report must separately justify the cost difference
    The report states two contradictory propositions on page 11: without adviser fees the plan is 0.24% cheaper; with adviser fees it is 4.24% more expensive. Both statements cannot simultaneously justify recommending the plan. If the plan is more expensive once fees are included, the 'Lower Costs' benefit is unsupported. The report's own charge reconciliation contains errors: Standard Life's existing charge is stated as 0.52% (page 5) but used as 0.18% in the cost comparison (page 11); the impact calculation for EA5 uses Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46), not Standard Life's (£3,002.00), rendering that row's comparison unreliable. The benefit of 'Greater Investment Choice' is overstated: Standard Life offers 300 funds and Zurich offers 60+, compared to Aviva's range, yet the report does not acknowledge this or explain why those existing options were insufficient. The benefit of 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' is contradicted by evidence that Standard Life already allows adviser charging [P19]. The benefit of 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' is a service model, not a platform-specific feature. Lifestyling was cited as a reason to move the Zurich holding; the Aviva (EA3) arrangement is already in Aviva's consolidation fund and is recommended for an internal fund switch, not a transfer, undermining the logic that lifestyling drift justifies consolidation. The report prioritises consolidation as the client's 'main priority' [P21], but consolidation is a convenience benefit (saves administration time), not a financial return, and cannot be weighted as if it were an economic justification for higher cost. The critical yield of 4.8% required to offset the cost is stated as 'achievable' based on past performance [P11], but no historical performance data for the recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced) are cited in the evidence to support this assertion. The guards confirm: (1) a benefit the existing plan already provides (adviser charging at Standard Life, fund choice at Standard Life and Zurich) cannot justify extra cost; (2) consolidation convenience is real but not financial; (3) the report has not demonstrated whether benefits are used. The report's assertion that lower costs justify the recommendation is contradicted by its own statement that the plan is 4.24% more expensive when adviser fees are included.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states the recommended Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than current plans on a like-for-like basis (page 11). However, including adviser fees of 1.00% ongoing and £3,305 initial, the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive. The required outperformance is £4,672 in year one, and the critical yield at mid growth (2.95%) is 4.8% per annum. Projected values at age 75 under mid growth are: existing £127,000, new solution £115,000, a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%. RIY figures are 0.3% (existing) and 2.1% or 2.2% (new solution depending on scenario). The report states these outcomes are achievable based on past fund performance.
    File:     The pension switch report (P10) shows identical critical yield and RIY figures for the mid-growth scenario (critical yield 4.8%, RIY 2.2%). Projected values match exactly: existing £127,000, new solution £115,000, effect of switching £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. The report (P3) states an initial adviser charge of £3,305 is deducted from the new solution, and an ongoing annual charge of 1.00% has been included in projections. The switch report (P1) shows existing pension value £110,185.60 and new solution day one value £106,880.60, resulting in immediate shortfall of £3,305 (the adviser charge). No document disputes the cost comparison figures or projects that the recommendation would outperform the existing plans.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts -0.24% cost difference (cheaper) but this figure is not matched in any passage; nearest is 0.29% in P5. The 0.24% figure cannot be arithmetically verified from the evidence.
      - The report asserts 4.24% required outperformance but this figure is not matched in any passage; nearest is 4% in P10. The exact 4.24% figure cannot be verified.
      - The report asserts a required outperformance of £4,672 in monetary terms, but this cannot be verified against specific fund values in the evidence. The derived figure calculation shows £4,672 at 4.24% implies fund value £110,188.68, which matches no arrangement value in the pack.
      - All five existing arrangements sum to £116,998.47 (confirmed by derived figures), yet the platform charge calculations imply £110,185.71, and investment management charge implies £115,195.45, and NP1 total ongoing charge implies £110,184.09. None of these implied fund values match the actual sum or any single arrangement.
      - The report presents the recommendation as having lower like-for-like costs (0.24% cheaper) yet simultaneously requiring 4.24% higher outperformance when adviser fees are included. This internal contradiction is correctly captured in the evidence, but the cost saving claim is unsupported.
    The report makes two contradictory cost claims: that the recommendation is 0.24% cheaper on a like-for-like basis, yet 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees. This contradiction is internally consistent within the report itself (it correctly reflects the impact of adding 1% ongoing adviser fees), so it is not a finding within the report. However, the cost saving figure of -0.24% is not corroborated by any passage in the evidence pack. The closest comparison appears to be the charge differences shown in the switch report, but the exact -0.24% figure cannot be located or verified. The derived figures confirm that the all-in charge calculations (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = 0.43%, or with 0.01% other = 0.44% total for the new solution) do not reconcile with a £117,000 projected value at mid growth, creating arithmetic uncertainty about the base comparisons. The critical yield (4.8% required) and RIY figures (2.2%) for the mid-growth scenario are corroborated exactly in P10. The projected values showing £12,000 decrease (9.4%) are also corroborated. The £3,305 initial adviser charge is confirmed by the immediate fund value shortfall (P1). The claim that the required 4.8% outperformance 'is achievable' based on past performance is supported by fund performance data in P13 and P14, which show both recommended funds with strong historical returns. However, the core cost assertion (0.24% cheaper) lacks evidentiary support. The charge figures are internally complex: existing arrangements show charges of 0.50% (Aviva EA3), 0.52% (Standard Life EA5 on page 5, but 0.18% in the comparative analysis table on page 11—a material discrepancy noted in the extraction), 0.93% (Zurich EA4), and low charges on others. The report does not provide a clear, single aggregated all-in cost figure for the existing portfolio against which the recommendation can be cleanly compared. The initial impact (£3,305 shortfall) is disclosed, and the effect on projected values is shown, but the arithmetic does not reconcile at the fund-value level for the charge calculations. The required outperformance (4.24% including fees, or 4.8% critical yield) is a material hurdle, and while the report asserts it is achievable, the fund selection and switching costs create a material drag on returns that is acknowledged but not fully justified by benefits unique to the recommendation versus retaining existing arrangements.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.4, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). The report emphasises limited flexibility on the new Aviva platform but does not state that the existing Zurich arrangement (EA4, current value £103,439.24) does not offer FAD at all without fund value increase to £10k minimum ([P23]), whereas the recommended Aviva platform does offer full FAD ([P23]). This is a material withdrawal feature gain, not discussed in the disadvantages section despite being listed as a disadvantage of the new plan. The report states Standard Life charges 0.52% (page 5 arrangements table) but the Pension Switch Charges table (page 11) uses 0.18% as the current annual recurring charge. The derived figure analysis shows EA5 impact row is computed on the wrong plan (Zurich's £103,438.46 rather than Standard Life's £3,002.00). Any cost comparison resting on this row is unreliable, yet the report cites it to support switching advantages. The report does not disclose that the existing Aviva fund is a lifestyling/consolidation fund ('Aviva pension my future focus consolidation' [P20]) which automatically reduces risk approaching retirement, whereas the recommended HSBC Global Strategy and CT Universal MAP Balanced funds are static risk rating 5 allocations. The loss of automatic de-risking is a material disadvantage affecting long-term retirement adequacy, particularly for a client now aged 55 heading to retirement at 75. 18 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states two material disadvantages: (1) 'If you were to transfer all your pensions into this plan, it would limit how you can withdraw funds. In the future, this would be important to you, as you would be unable to access this pension flexibly' (page 6); (2) 'The blended funds have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' (page 7). The report also lists six limitations: market timing risk during transaction execution (page 8), 10-15 working day access delays (page 8), exclusion of transaction costs from charge quotations (page 10), need for ongoing risk profile review (page 4), projection limitations due to assumption variance (page 15), and long-term care costs exclusion from cashflow projections (page 15). Under specific recommendations, the report identifies disadvantages for the Scottish Widows encashment: limited fund range, taxable withdrawal at marginal rate on 75% of amount, and loss of automatic lifestyling. For existing arrangements, it notes loss of higher Zurich Managed fund performance (44.32% over 5 years) and cost increase (0.52% vs 0.44%). Tax consequences are stated for the small pot encashment as '75% taxed as income at your marginal tax rate' (page 6). Loss scenarios are illustrated through the performance comparison chart and the critical yield requirement of 4.8% per annum (page 11, Appendix).
    File:     The Key Features document [P4] discloses multiple risks: currency exposure affecting fund values, property investment illiquidity, market timing risk ('possibility that the price of investments may fall or rise in the period between us receiving your instructions and the time of the transaction'), fixed-term product access restrictions, and projection non-guarantees. [P4] warns that income drawdown may deplete pension funds if investments do not grow sufficiently to compensate for withdrawals. [P4] states 'If you withdraw high levels of income, there'll be less money to provide for dependants or to buy a lifetime annuity in the future'. [P4] notes tax position uncertainty affecting income payments. [P7] warns 'The value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up' and 'You could get back less than has been invested'. The Terms and Conditions [P6], [P8], [P9] disclose switching mechanics including market timing exposure and potential delay or deferral of purchases. [P9] states 'There may therefore be incidences where You are disinvested for a period and subject to potentially adverse market movements, We will not be liable for any losses arising during this period'. The Pension Switch Report [P1], [P2] presents comparative data showing that for Standard Life alone, the critical yield is 5.9% (page in P2) and for the combined recommendation, the effect of switching ranges from £10 (0.3% decrease) to £10 (0.2% decrease) under high growth scenarios. [P20] states 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider'. The Product Research table [P10], [P23] discloses that the new Aviva platform has FAD (Flexi Access Drawdown) available, while some existing arrangements do not, representing a material withdrawal flexibility difference.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report emphasises limited flexibility on the new Aviva platform but does not state that the existing Zurich arrangement (EA4, current value £103,439.24) does not offer FAD at all without fund value increase to £10k minimum ([P23]), whereas the recommended Aviva platform does offer full FAD ([P23]). This is a material withdrawal feature gain, not discussed in the disadvantages section despite being listed as a disadvantage of the new plan.
      - The report states Standard Life charges 0.52% (page 5 arrangements table) but the Pension Switch Charges table (page 11) uses 0.18% as the current annual recurring charge. The derived figure analysis shows EA5 impact row is computed on the wrong plan (Zurich's £103,438.46 rather than Standard Life's £3,002.00). Any cost comparison resting on this row is unreliable, yet the report cites it to support switching advantages.
      - The report does not disclose that the existing Aviva fund is a lifestyling/consolidation fund ('Aviva pension my future focus consolidation' [P20]) which automatically reduces risk approaching retirement, whereas the recommended HSBC Global Strategy and CT Universal MAP Balanced funds are static risk rating 5 allocations. The loss of automatic de-risking is a material disadvantage affecting long-term retirement adequacy, particularly for a client now aged 55 heading to retirement at 75.
      - The report states the recommendation is 'Like for Like 0.24% less expensive' but 'Including Adviser Fee 4.24% more expensive' (page 11). This fundamental tension—that the switch requires 4.8% critical yield to break even once advice costs are included—is disclosed but not highlighted as a material disadvantage requiring explicit discussion of the outcome if markets underperform.
      - The report does not quantify the projected retirement value reduction illustrated in the Pension Switch Report comparative tables ([P2] showing £10 or 0.3-0.2% decrease in maturity value). While small in percentage terms, the report presents no loss scenario illustrating actual fund value impact in monetary terms at retirement under base-case or downside assumptions.
      - The Key Features [P4] warn that income drawdown depletes funds if investments do not grow sufficiently and that high income withdrawals leave less for dependants or annuity purchase. The report recommends drawdown from the Scottish Widows small pot (encashment, not drawdown) but does not discuss drawdown sustainability post-switch for the consolidated £110k+ fund, representing a material consumer harm scenario if income needs exceed sustainable withdrawal rates.
    The report discloses multiple disadvantages and limitations spread across its narrative and appendix sections, consistent with the guard that disadvantages may be distributed rather than collected. However, material disadvantages identified in the supporting evidence are not fairly disclosed: (1) The cost basis used to support the switching recommendation appears unreliable—the Standard Life charge comparison in the Pension Switch Report uses 0.18% recurring charge (page 11 [P2]) contradicting the 0.52% stated in the report's own arrangements table (page 5), and the derived figures show the impact row is computed on Zurich's value rather than Standard Life's, undermining the 0.24% cost saving claim. (2) The loss of automatic lifestyling from the existing Aviva consolidation fund to static risk 5 allocations is material for a 55-year-old client retiring at 75, but is not disclosed as a disadvantage of the new solution. (3) The report discloses the adviser fee cost (4.24% additional cost when included) and critical yield requirement (4.8%) but does not prominently present this as a material loss scenario or disadvantage—the recommendation requires market outperformance to justify costs, yet no downside scenario illustrates what happens if this does not occur. (4) The withdrawal flexibility disadvantage is misdirected: the report states the new Aviva plan limits flexibility, but the existing Zurich plan (the largest holding) offers no FAD unless fund value reaches £10k minimum ([P23]), whereas the new Aviva plan offers full FAD. The report thus presents as a disadvantage what may be an improvement for the primary asset. (5) The Pension Switch Report explicitly warns 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement' ([P20]), quantifying small projected losses (£10 decrease, [P2]), yet the suitability report does not restate this in a material loss scenario section. (6) Income drawdown sustainability post-switch is not addressed despite Key Features warnings ([P4]) that high withdrawals deplete funds and leave less for dependants, a material consumer harm scenario absent from the report. The tax consequence of the small pot encashment (75% at marginal rate) is stated but the estimated net receipt (£170 from £211.32 gross) assumes a tax rate not explicitly disclosed in the tax treatment section. The market timing and access delay risks are disclosed but presented generically; no product-specific discussion connects them to the specific funds recommended (HSBC Global Strategy, CT Universal MAP). The requirement to assess consistency with supporting evidence is partially met: stated disadvantages are consistent with general product literature, but material disadvantages unique to this client's switch (cost basis reliability, lifestyling loss, withdrawal feature mismatch, fee-driven critical yield) are incompletely or inaccurately disclosed.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report discloses the following product-specific and investment-specific risks: (1) Pension switch risk—'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider' (page 12). (2) Lifestyling risk—'Your funds are currently invested in lifestyling funds, which automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement. As a result, your portfolio will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range' (page 7). (3) Higher-risk volatility—'Your Zurich investments are allocated to higher-risk funds, which are more susceptible to market volatility, resulting in greater fluctuations in value' (page 13). (4) Market timing risk—'whilst the transactions are pending, there could be movements within the stock-market; that is to say that the stock-market could rise or fall in value' (page 8). (5) Performance guarantee—'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement for these funds' (page 8). The report also discloses that without adviser fees the switch saves 0.24% in costs, but with adviser fees it costs 4.24% more (page 11), and that a critical yield of 4.8% per annum is required (page 11, Appendix). One loss scenario is illustrated: the Scottish Widows small pot withdrawal is estimated to net £170 after tax from a gross value of £211.32 (page 6). Tax consequences are addressed for the Scottish Widows encashment but not comprehensively for the other arrangements or the switch as a whole.
    File:     Product literature on file includes KIIDs for the HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (Accumulation C, rated Risk 3) and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc. The HSBC KIID discloses: (P1, P3) material risks including counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, currency risk, liquidity risk, and leverage risk. It notes the fund is classified in category 4 'because its price or simulated data has shown medium fluctuations historically' (P3), though it is rated as risk level 3 (P1, P5). The fund's asset allocation is stated as equities 45-70%, bonds 20-55%, other assets 0-25%, 'will fluctuate based upon market movement' (P3). The KIID recommends the fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within a period of 5 years' (P3, P9). The HSBC Fund Factsheet (P5) states 'any potential returns are likely to be limited by the risk profile of the Fund' and 'The value of investments and any income from them can go down as well as up and investors may not get back the amount originally invested' (P5). Zurich policy information (P16, P17) discloses generic and specific risks: 'Over time, a fund that invests mostly in shares is likely to offer greater potential for higher returns than a fund investing in cash deposits, but with it comes greater fluctuations in value' (P17); property funds 'could fall quite sharply' and may be delayed up to 12 months (P16); 'high yield bond funds tend to invest in high yielding corporate bonds, which are generally higher risk investments'; funds in less mature markets 'carry more investment risk' (P16). Aviva Key Features (P7, P14) state: 'The value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up. You could get back less than has been invested' (P14), and note flexibility in retirement income options but warn 'If you withdraw high levels of income, there'll be less money to provide for dependants or to buy a lifetime annuity in the future' (P4). Aviva Terms and Conditions (P10, P13) detail fund manager charges, adviser charges, and dilution levies. The Risk Profile Report (P18) confirms the client's agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced investor), mapping to a collection of asset classes. Notably, the report's canonical model states the client agreed to risk rating 5, but the initial questionnaire indicated rating 4 (extraction note). The recommended funds HSBC Global Strategy Balanced and CT Universal MAP Balanced are risk-rated differently in the literature: HSBC is rated 3 in its KIID (P1) but classified in category 4 (P3, P9); CT Universal MAP's specific rating is not fully stated in the retrieved passages. The mismatch between the client's agreed risk level (5) and the HSBC fund's stated risk level (3) is a material discrepancy.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced funds are suitable for Risk rating 5 client (page 12), but HSBC KIID rates the fund as Risk 3 (P1: 'rated as 3, where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk') and classifies it in category 4 'because its price or simulated data has shown medium fluctuations historically' (P3). This is a material mismatch between client risk profile and fund risk rating.
      - Report discloses product-specific risks for pension switch and existing arrangements (Zurich higher-risk volatility, lifestyling) but does not prominently disclose material specific risks from the recommended funds' literature, such as counterparty risk, credit risk, derivatives risk, leverage risk, currency risk, and liquidity risk documented in the HSBC KIID (P1, P3, P11) and Zurich policy information (P16, P17).
      - Report states HSBC fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' (P3, P9 KIID), but does not disclose this restriction or its relevance to the client's retirement plans in the suitability report itself.
      - Report quantifies cost savings as 0.24% without adviser fees but 4.24% more expensive with adviser fees (page 11), yet does not clearly explain that the adviser fee cost (at what percentage or amount) is the determining factor that reverses the cost comparison, nor does it clearly state the adviser fee amount or % to the client where the comparison is made.
      - Report does not disclose tax consequences of switching the Zurich arrangement (largest holding at £103,439.24) or the other arrangements beyond the Scottish Widows small pot (£211.32). Tax implications of consolidation and fund switching are material but largely unaddressed.
      - Report does not illustrate loss scenarios beyond the Scottish Widows encashment estimate (£170 net). No illustration is given of potential downside or worst-case scenarios for the recommended switch, particularly given the market timing risk disclosed (stock-market movements during transfer).
    The comparison can be performed because both the report's disclosures and the product literature are available. However, material discrepancies exist. First, the most significant mismatch is the fund risk rating: the client's agreed risk level is confirmed as 5 (Balanced investor per P18 and report page 4), but the recommended HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio is rated as Risk 3 in its KIID (P1), not Risk 5. This is a material inconsistency that undermines the suitability conclusion. The report asserts these funds are appropriate for the client's Risk 5 profile, but the fund literature itself rates the HSBC fund at Risk 3. Second, the report discloses generic pension switch risks and market timing risks but does not disclose specific investment risks identified in the KIIDs and fund literature for the recommended funds. The HSBC KIID prominently lists counterparty risk, credit risk, derivatives risk, leverage risk, currency risk, and liquidity risk (P1, P3, P11)—none of which appear in the suitability report's risk disclosures. The guard notes that 'standard capital-at-risk wording satisfies the general warning but not product-specific risks,' and here the report has provided generic wording but deferred product-specific detail to literature. This is a disclosure shortfall. Third, the report does not disclose that the HSBC fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' (P3, P9), which may be material if the client's retirement horizon is uncertain or nearer than five years. Fourth, the cost comparison (0.24% cheaper like-for-like, but 4.24% more expensive with adviser fees) is presented without clearly stating what the adviser fee is as a percentage or amount. This is material because it determines the entire cost comparison and therefore the break-even analysis (4.8% critical yield). The report does not transparently disclose the adviser fee's magnitude. Fifth, tax consequences are addressed only for the small Scottish Widows pot (£170 net from £211.32 gross) but not for the other four arrangements or the overall switch. The Zurich arrangement alone is £103,439.24—material tax implications of switching this and the others are unaddressed. Sixth, only one loss scenario (Scottish Widows encashment) is illustrated; no downside scenarios are presented for the recommended switch itself, despite disclosing market timing risk during transfer. The extraction notes confirm internal inconsistencies in the report itself: risk rating questionnaire indicated 4 but agreed position is 5; existing Aviva fund (consolidation) is being switched to entirely different funds (HSBC Global Strategy and CT Universal MAP); and the Standard Life existing charge is stated as 0.52% on page 5 but 0.18% in the comparative analysis on page 11, with cost computation rows applied to the wrong arrangements. These internal inconsistencies compound the disclosure shortfall. The report's tone presents benefits and flexibility prominently (e.g., switching to multi-asset funds for better diversification) while deferring risk detail to appendices and product literature, which aligns with the guard's warning about risk content appearing 'only in an appendix while benefits are prominent.' Overall, material disadvantages, specific product risks, and tax consequences have not been fairly, clearly, or prominently explained in the report; they are either absent or relegated to product literature the client must access separately.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states seven tax consequences: (1) Small pots taxation: '25% taken as tax-free cash and the remaining 75% taxed as income at your marginal tax rate' with estimated net receipt of £170 on a £200 fund (page 6). (2) Personal allowance optimisation: 'If you were to leave this pot to grow until you retire, you could potentially take the full amount within your personal allowance, meaning no tax would be due' (page 6). (3) Tax-free lump sum: 'An individual can take up to 25% of the pension fund being crystallised as a tax-free lump sum at outset (subject to the Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275)' (page 19). (4) Flexi-access drawdown taxation: 'The remainder of the fund remains invested within a tax advantaged environment...each withdrawal is taxed at their marginal rate of Income Tax' (page 19). (5) Death before age 75: 'all these options are paid free of tax' (page 9). (6) Death after age 75: 'tax is paid at the beneficiary's marginal rate of tax' (page 9). (7) IHT change from 6 April 2027: 'most unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits...will be included in your taxable estate for IHT' with exceptions listed (page 9). Current IHT liability stated as £0 (page 3).
    File:     The supporting evidence corroborates most tax consequences through Key Features [P2] and [P4]. [P2] states: 'You can normally withdraw up to 25% of your pension fund as a tax-free lump sum' and 'You may have to pay income tax on any income payments...the tax you pay will depend on your personal circumstances'; it also references HMRC lump sum allowance and lump sum & death benefit allowance limits, advising 'You should always consult your adviser for up-to-date information relating to your personal circumstances.' [P4] confirms: 'If you die before you're 75, we'll normally pay any benefits tax free to your nominated beneficiaries' and 'If you die after the age of 75...Any benefits will be taxed at your beneficiaries' marginal rate.' [P4] also addresses the change to IHT treatment. However, the client's marginal tax rate is not established in the evidence. The report does not disclose the client's current tax position, income tax band, or how the 75% of the Scottish Widows encashment (£158.49 gross) would be taxed at the client's specific marginal rate. The £170 net estimate appears to assume a tax rate but this is not made explicit or justified against the client's financial position.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report estimates Scottish Widows net encashment receipt of £170 from a £211.32 fund (25% tax-free = ~£53, 75% = ~£158.49). This implies a tax rate of approximately 17–20% on the 75% portion, but the report does not state the client's marginal tax rate or justify this estimate against the client's actual income position documented as £300/week net HGV driver income plus £20/week volunteer income, plus spouse income of £1,100/month net (page 5). The tax consequence is stated without the client's tax band being established.
      - Tax-free lump sum consequence (25%) is stated generically with reference to the Lump Sum Allowance (£268,275) but no assessment is made of whether the client's total pension funds (£116,998.47 across five arrangements) will trigger this allowance or whether any prior use of the allowance exists, making the stated consequence unverifiable for this client.
      - The report states IHT liability is currently £0 (page 3) but does not address the material tax consequence of the 6 April 2027 change: after that date, the remaining drawdown funds (estimated at retirement as between £97,600 and £150,000 depending on growth, per page 11) will be in the client's taxable estate unless passed to a surviving spouse, dependent, or via death-in-service. For an estate planning perspective, this is a material disadvantage or risk of the recommendation (choosing drawdown over annuity) that is disclosed in generic form but not explained for this client's circumstances.
    Comparison can be performed on most stated tax consequences. The report's core tax statements on small pots, tax-free lump sum, flexi-access drawdown taxation, and death benefit tax treatment are supported by Key Features evidence [P2] and [P4]. However, two material gaps emerge when read against the decision logic for 'No Issue' (evidence complete, consistent and supports outcome; material disadvantages, product and investment risks, tax consequences and at least one loss scenario stated in the report, consistent with the research and product literature, and presented where the client will see them). First, the client's marginal tax rate is not established anywhere in the evidence pack, yet the report uses it to estimate the net receipt from encashing Scottish Widows (£170 net on £211.32 gross, implying ~17% average tax on the 75% portion). This is an assertion without foundation: a tax consequence stated without the client's tax band established is unverifiable, not necessarily wrong (per the guard). Second, the 6 April 2027 IHT change is disclosed on page 9 in generic form, but the report does not explain that the recommended solution (flexi-access drawdown with funds remaining invested and passed on death) will bring the client's pension into their taxable estate from that date, whereas the existing annuity option or full encashment would not. This is a material tax consequence of the recommendation — a disadvantage of choosing drawdown over alternatives — that is mentioned generically but not explained for this client's circumstances or presented prominently in the recommendation section. The report does address the £268,275 lump sum allowance limit but does not assess whether the client's £116,998 across five arrangements poses any risk to this limit when consolidated and drawn, nor does it address the separate lump sum & death benefit allowance of £1,073,100. The Scottish Widows net estimate of £170 sits between the 20% and 40% tax rates; without the client's actual tax position, this cannot be verified as consistent. The report does not state explicitly whether this is net of the 20% or 40% higher rate, nor does it address the recent inflation in the client's circumstances (page 5 shows £300/week HGV income + £20/week volunteer + £1,100/month spouse income), which might affect the marginal rate calculation.

  [G8.4] Loss scenarios illustrated
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts two loss scenarios: (1) 'If the market were to fall between realisation of existing funds and purchase of new funds' [stated as a conditional possibility on page 8]; (2) 'Immediate market crash of -30% in combination with high inflation of 6%' [stated as a stress test on page 15, concluding no shortfall would result]. The report does not quantify the first scenario's loss percentage. The second is quantified at -30% market loss with 6% inflation held constant, and the stress test concludes the plan would sustain this loss without requiring funds to be used.
    File:     The supporting evidence contains generic product risks (currency, liquidity, credit, derivative, emerging markets, leverage, fixed interest risks) stated in KIIDs and Key Features documents [P2, P6, P8]. The Illustration [P10, P15] warns that actual investments may be worth less than paid in, that annuity rates may be adversely affected by low returns, and that income drawdown may deplete the fund if growth does not compensate withdrawals. The cashflow analysis [P4, P11, P16, P21] models multiple scenarios and projects outcomes under different growth rates (Low 0%, Mid 2.95%, High 5.88% before charges, or -0.1%, 2.9%, 5.9% after inflation) but does not isolate a discrete loss scenario of -30%. The stress test result [P22] confirms the same -30% scenario and high inflation conclusion as the report. No evidence passage quantifies a loss occurring between fund realisation and reinvestment.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report's first loss scenario (market fall during fund transfer) is stated qualitatively without a quantified loss percentage, while the supporting product literature [P2] identifies specific risks (currency, liquidity, leverage) that could occur during transition but does not isolate or quantify a loss between realisation and reinvestment.
      - The report's -30% stress test outcome and high inflation scenario match the supporting cashflow analysis [P22], but no supporting evidence independently quantifies or illustrates what a -30% market loss would mean in monetary terms (e.g., £117k falling to £82k) for this client's specific funds.
      - The report presents loss scenarios only in the context of stress tests and alternative scenarios (pages 8 and 15), not as part of a prominent dedicated risks or disadvantages section early in the suitability narrative.
    The report asserts two loss scenarios. The first (market fall during transfer) is stated conditionally but not quantified in either the report or the evidence; this is a narrative risk warning rather than a loss scenario with a modelled outcome. The second (-30% market crash with 6% inflation) is corroborated by the stress test in the cashflow evidence [P22], which uses identical parameters and reaches the same conclusion (no shortfall). However, the manner and prominence of disclosure differ materially. The evidence presents the stress test as part of 'Alternative Scenarios & Stress Tests' [P22] in the main suitability report, not segregated as a loss scenario illustration. The supporting product literature [P2, P6, P8, P10, P15] contains generic warnings about downside risk, fund value fluctuation, and adverse market conditions, but does not present a concrete loss scenario specific to this client's £117k pension pot, growth assumptions, or switching costs. The report does not quantify the -30% loss in monetary terms for the client (e.g., 'your £117,000 could fall to £82,000'), nor does it explain how such a loss would interact with the switching costs (0.24% cheaper without adviser fees, but 4.24% more expensive with them per page 11). The first scenario (realisation-to-reinvestment loss) is stated without supporting calculation or evidence of the magnitude of risk. The guard permits stress tests in cashflow models to count as loss scenarios; the -30% test qualifies. However, a loss scenario presented in a stress test appendix with no quantification in monetary terms for the client's situation, and with benefits and cost-savings prominently displayed earlier in the report [P10], risks materially understating the downside to a consumer. The rule on prominence applies: risk content appears only in appendix sections (pages 8, 15, and Appendix) while benefits (projected fund values, cost savings vs existing plans) are prominent on pages 5-12.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report includes a dedicated section titled 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 8. It states: 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider.' The report presents loss scenarios across three growth rates (low 0.00%, mid 2.95%, high 5.88%), showing projected value decreases of £10,400 (9.6%), £12,000 (9.4%), and £15,000 (10.0%) respectively at age 75. A critical yield of 4.8% per annum is required at the mid-growth rate to match the existing plan's maturity value. The report discloses adviser charges of £3,305 initial and 1% per annum ongoing. It notes the immediate impact on death benefit of £3,305 due to switching fees. The report acknowledges that 'your portfolio will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range' if lifestyling funds are retained.
    File:     The retrieved passages confirm loss scenarios presented identically in [P1] and [P11]: low growth scenarios show £10,400 decrease (9.6%), mid-growth shows £12,000 decrease (9.4%), and high-growth shows £15,000 decrease (10.0%). The critical yield of 4.8% is confirmed in [P2] and [P11]. Performance data in [P3] and [P5] shows cumulative performance of recommended funds (HSBC and CT Universal MAP Balanced) at 37.25% and 36.53% over 5 years respectively. [P6] states the recommendation is for 'Lower Costs' with 0.08% difference, though this contradicts detailed charge analysis. [P4] shows platform charge 0.21%, investment management 0.22%, total 0.44%. [P8] confirms ongoing adviser charge 1% per annum (~£1,101.85 first year). [P15] confirms £3,305 death benefit shortfall immediately after switch. However, tax consequences of the Scottish Widows encashment (£211.32 fund value, £170 net receipt) are mentioned narratively as 'estimated net' but no statutory tax rate or detailed tax explanation is provided in the report. The report does not quantify the tax impact as a percentage or explain the marginal rate applicable.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the plan is '0.08% more expensive' ([P6]) but the detailed charge table [P4] and switch assessment [P4] show Aviva Platform at 0.44% total vs. existing arrangements ranging 0.50-0.93%, making the switch cost-saving, not more expensive, on a like-for-like basis. The like-for-like comparison [P4] states '0.24% less expensive' but including adviser fees makes it '4.24% more expensive' — a material tension not fully reconciled in the main narrative.
      - Tax treatment of Scottish Widows encashment (£211.32 to £170 net) is quantified in absolute terms but no tax rate, statutory basis, or explanation of progressive taxation impact is provided in the report body; the calculation appears in the recommendation but lacks supporting tax consequence explanation.
      - The report emphasizes benefits ('Lower Costs', 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility') in the main recommendation section ([P6]), while quantified loss scenarios and critical yield requirements appear on a later page ([P1]) and in appendix; the prominence hierarchy favours benefits over risks.
    The suitability report does include a dedicated section 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 8, satisfying the structural element of prominence. Loss scenarios are presented in three growth-rate bands with quantified decreases in maturity value (£10,400, £12,000, £15,000) and percentage impacts (9.6%, 9.4%, 10.0%), matching the supporting pension switch report [P11]. The critical yield of 4.8% is clearly stated and explained. However, several material disadvantages and risks are not fairly or adequately explained: (1) Tax consequences: The Scottish Widows encashment recommendation (page 6) states 'estimated net receipt of £170 net' from a £211.32 fund value, implying a tax deduction. The report does not explain the statutory tax treatment (25% tax-free cash entitlement, 75% taxed as income), the marginal rate applicable to the client's circumstances (income, state pension, spouse's income), or the progressive tax impact. COBS 9.4.7R requires clear disclosure of tax consequences; here the consequence is illustrated in absolute terms but not explained. (2) Charge inconsistency and emphasis: The recommendation section ([P6]) leads with 'Lower Costs' claiming a cost advantage, but the detailed analysis reveals the position is complex. Page 11 states like-for-like the plan is 0.24% cheaper but including adviser fees it is 4.24% more expensive. This critical distinction is buried in appendix comparative tables; the main recommendation text does not prominently address the net cost-benefit and the contingency on fund outperformance (critical yield 4.8%). (3) Risk of underperformance: While the report states 'no guarantee of future returns' and cites past performance, it does not disclose specific downside scenarios if the critical yield is not achieved. The report asserts past performance of recommended funds supports 4.8% outperformance being 'achievable' ([P2]), but does not quantify the risk or probability that the funds may underperform, leaving the client with a net loss after charges. (4) Death benefit impact: The immediate £3,305 reduction in death benefit is disclosed ([P15]) but positioned only in the appendix 'Immediate impact on the fund value following the switch', not in the main disadvantages section, making this material risk less prominent than benefits. (5) Lifestyling migration risk: The report states the existing portfolio 'will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range' ([P6]) if lifestyling is retained, but does not quantify or illustrate this risk scenario or explain what action would be required if it occurs. The report's disclosure structure places loss scenarios and detailed risks on later pages after prominent benefit claims, inconsistent with COBS 2.1.1R (fair, clear and prominent presentation) and COBS 4.2.1R (cancellation and withdrawal rights must be as prominent as benefits). The vulnerability overlay: No material vulnerability characteristics affecting this specific requirement are identified in the evidence. The client is a HGV driver with stable income, spouse contributing, full state pension entitlement, and capacity for loss confirmed; no communication adjustments, language barriers, or cognitive constraints are flagged that would materially affect whether disadvantages can be understood.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts one feature being lost: 'Loss of direct fund access restriction: If you were to transfer all your pensions into this plan, it would limit how you can withdraw funds. In the future, this would be important to you, as you would be unable to access this pension flexibly.' (page 6). The report lists existing arrangements as having no safeguarded benefits. Retirement options available are listed as: Arrangement 1 (People's Pension) - FlexiAccessDrawdown, UFPLS, PartialUFPLS; Arrangement 2 (Scottish Widows) - UFPLS, PartialUFPLS; Arrangement 3 (Aviva) - FlexiAccessDrawdown, UFPLS, PartialUFPLS; Arrangement 4 (Zurich) - UFPLS, PartialUFPLS; Arrangement 5 (Standard Life) - FlexiAccessDrawdown.
    File:     The evidence reveals multiple features and protections being lost that are not disclosed in the report: (1) Zurich offers life cover and waiver benefits on existing pension plans, but both are no longer available on new pension plans per P3: 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans. You would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan. This will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits.' (2) Standard Life policy info P10 notes 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' and refers to a 'Differential Fund Discount from the Total Expense Ratio (TER)' and 'Leaver Discount: 0.4800%', indicating loss of discounted charging. (3) People's Pension offers no annuity option per P16: 'People's Partnership doesn't offer an option to purchase an annuity, which offers a guaranteed income' whereas other arrangements may offer annuities. (4) Scottish Widows has no annuity available per P5 and P8 comparison tables showing 'No' for annuity availability. (5) Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds per report itself (page 7), requiring blended approach. (6) Scottish Widows does not offer single multi-asset fund aligned with risk rating 5 per report (page 6). (7) Standard Life does not permit partial transfers per P17: 'Standard Life do not permit partial transfers for investment purposes from this Pension contract.' (8) Zurich FAD (flexi access drawdown) available only if fund value increased to £10k per P8. (9) Multiple arrangements employ lifestyling which reduces risk automatically toward retirement, forcing portfolio outside agreed risk range per report (pages 7-8).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states only 'loss of direct fund access restriction' as a feature being lost but evidence shows life cover and waiver benefits available on existing Zurich plan and unavailable on new plan (P3).
      - Report does not disclose loss of preferential charging on Standard Life arrangement (0.48% leaver discount) that 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' per P10.
      - Report does not mention loss of annuity purchasing option if transferring People's Pension out (P16 states People's Partnership does not offer annuity option).
      - Report does not disclose that Zurich FAD is contingent on fund value reaching £10k threshold (P8), a material limitation on flexibility being claimed as a benefit.
      - Report asserts client gains flexibility by moving to Aviva but does not disclose that Aviva plan charges do not allow adviser charging (P14) and charges may increase future advisory options.
      - Report describes lifestyling as a problem with existing arrangements (pages 7-8) but does not disclose what features or protections the new solution provides to prevent drift or what flexibility is lost if new solution has different constraints.
      - Report lists Scottish Widows as offering UFPLS/PartialUFPLS but does not disclose the restrictions documented in P8 ('Full and partial UFPLS available: No' for Scottish Widows in one arrangement variant) or the limitations on individual fund selection.
      - Report states People's Pension retirement options as FlexiAccessDrawdown, UFPLS, PartialUFPLS but P16 indicates only uncrystallised fund access is available through online account claiming, not guaranteed annuity or other traditional retirement pathways.
    The comparison can be performed: the canonical model extracts stated features being lost and lists retirement options for existing arrangements, while the evidence provides policy literature detailing actual features, protections, restrictions and safeguards on existing plans that are not being given up but not mentioned in the report. The false-positive guard states: 'A guarantee recorded in provider literature but never mentioned in the report is the finding; do not require the report to have named it before looking for it.' This applies directly: life cover and waiver benefits on Zurich (P3), preferential charging on Standard Life (P10), and annuity restrictions on People's Pension (P16) are all documented in provider literature but not disclosed in the report's articulation of features being lost. The second guard, 'A benefit the client cannot access at their current age is still a benefit being given up,' is relevant: the Zurich life cover and waiver benefits exist now and are being lost. The third guard, 'A feature described in generic product literature that this policy does not actually carry is not being given up,' does not apply: the features in evidence (life cover, waiver, preferential charging, annuity options) are specific to the individual policies, not generic claims. The report's statement about 'loss of direct fund access restriction' (inability to withdraw flexibly) is internally contradictory: it describes a limitation of the new Aviva plan as if it is something being lost from existing arrangements, but the evidence shows existing arrangements offer FlexiAccessDrawdown and UFPLS options (P8, P1), so the report has inverted the direction of the comparison. The report asserts the client is gaining flexibility by consolidating into Aviva, but the quoted passage actually warns that Aviva will limit flexibility. This is an internal consistency failure, not primarily an omission, but it compounds the disclosure gap. Material features documented in evidence but absent from the report's disclosure of what is being lost: (1) life cover and waiver (Zurich); (2) preferential unit pricing and discount structure (Standard Life); (3) annuity purchasing options and their constraints; (4) restrictions on partial transfer (Standard Life); (5) fund value thresholds affecting drawdown access (Zurich); (6) the mechanism and consequences of lifestyling drift in existing arrangements as contrasted with protections (or lack thereof) in the new solution. The report does not compare features systematically; it names one putative loss (flexibility) which is actually a property of the new plan, not the old ones, and leaves all documented product features from evidence unlisted.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts two key cost-related justifications: (1) Performance comparison: 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds... the Aviva platform funds selected for you align with your agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility' (page 13). (2) Critical yield: 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan. Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' (page 11). The canonical model states `/costsAndCharges/comparison/direction` as 'RecommendedCheaper', and page 11 claims 'Like for Like: our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans.' However, the same page also states 'Including Adviser Fee: our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans.' The report also lists disadvantages including loss of Zurich's higher-performing fund (44.32% over 5 years), cost increase for one arrangement (Standard Life: 0.08% more, 0.52% vs 0.44%), and limited fund options.
    File:     The evidence presents materially different projections and cost impacts: (P1) A pension switch report table shows at mid-growth (2.95%), existing pension projects to £127,000 vs new solution £125,000—a £2,000 or 1.9% decrease. The critical yield required is stated as 3.2%, not 4.8%. At high growth (5.88%), existing projects to £150,000 vs new £147,000 (£3,000 or 2.0% decrease). (P11) A stakeholder comparison ranks the existing solution 2nd at 0.3% reduction in yield with £127,000 maturity, and new solution 5th at 0.6% reduction in yield with £125,000 maturity. (P4) Standard Life projections show low growth £102,000, mid £120,000, high £141,000 with 0.00% stated charges ('n/a'). (P12) SIPP research shows combined recommended fund performance 42.40% over 5 years. (P13) MiFID II costs for recommended funds total 0.37% aggregated (HSBC 0.19%, CT Universal MAP 0.55%), not the 0.44% platform total stated in the report. (P19) The suitability report's own charge table shows 0.44% total for Aviva (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment + implied costs), but the Pension Switch Charges table lists Aviva's 'Current Annual Recurring Charge of Existing Plan' as 0.50% and Impact of 'Recommended Alternative' showing -0.26%, implying recommended at 0.24%.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report critical yield: 4.8% (page 11) vs evidence critical yield: 3.2% (P1 mid-growth scenario)
      - Report projected value at mid-growth for new solution: report extraction basis unclear but implies break-even or better vs evidence: £125,000 (P1) vs existing £127,000
      - Report states existing Zurich fund performance 44.32% over 5 years (page 13, extracted) vs evidence shows combined recommended fund performance 42.40% over 5 years (P12)—report acknowledges Zurich outperformance but does not reference combined fund underperformance relative to existing
      - Report direction of cost comparison: canonical model states 'RecommendedCheaper' vs evidence (P1): like-for-like shows 0.24% saving but with adviser fee becomes 4.24% more expensive (opposite direction when fees included)
      - Report cost figures: Standard Life stated as 0.52% (page 5, existing) vs evidence (P4) Standard Life charges: 0.00% stated ('n/a'), creating ambiguity about whether the comparison basis is correct
      - Report total charge for recommended solution: 0.44% (page 10 stated) vs evidence (P13) MiFID II aggregated fund costs 0.37%, a 0.07% variance—charge arithmetic (derived figures section) shows platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies £110,185.71 fund value, which does not match any single arrangement
      - Report justification rests on critical yield 4.8% being achievable based on past fund performance, but evidence (P1) shows critical yield of only 3.2% is required (at mid-growth), and evidence (P12) shows recommended funds' combined 5-year performance of 42.40% is materially below the 44.32% of the existing Zurich holding being switched away from
    The core tension is whether the report's justifications for accepting higher costs and lower projected outcomes are consistent with the evidence. Three material issues emerge: (1) CRITICAL YIELD MISMATCH: The report claims the recommended plan 'would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan' and asserts this is achievable. Evidence (P1) shows that under the same mid-growth scenario (2.94%), the critical yield required is 3.2%, not 4.8%. This is a 1.6 percentage point gap. The report's 4.8% figure appears to conflate the mid-growth rate (2.94%) with something else—possibly the aggregate effect of adviser fees on the hurdle. If the 4.8% is correct, it is not explained in the evidence; if 3.2% is correct, the report's justification overstates the hurdle. (2) PERFORMANCE COMPARISON LOGIC: The report justifies the switch partly by claiming recommended funds have delivered 'comparable performance with reduced volatility' to Zurich. Evidence (P12) shows the combined recommended fund performance is 42.40% over 5 years, whilst the existing Zurich holding (cited in the report at page 13) is 44.32%. This is not comparable—it is 1.92 percentage points lower. The report does acknowledge 'they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' but then proceeds anyway on the basis of 'reduced volatility' without quantifying that volatility benefit or explaining how reduced volatility justifies accepting lower absolute returns when the client's risk tolerance (level 5) is aligned to the recommended funds. (3) COST DIRECTION CONTRADICTION: The report's own page 11 states both 'Like for Like... 0.24% less expensive' and 'Including Adviser Fee... 4.24% more expensive'. This is internally contradictory. The evidence (P1) confirms both: without adviser fees, the new solution is cheaper; with adviser fees, it is significantly more expensive. The report presents this on the same page without clearly highlighting to the client that the switch only makes sense if the fund outperformance of at least 4.24% p.a. occurs. The critical yield logic depends on this, but the justification given (fund performance of 42.40% = comparable to 44.32%) does not support a 4.24% annual premium recovery. (4) CHARGE CALCULATIONS UNRELIABLE: The derived figures section identifies that the charge arithmetic does not reconcile: the platform charge implies a fund value that matches neither the Aviva arrangement alone nor the total fund value. The Pension Switch Charges table (P19) shows Standard Life listed with 0.18% recurring charge but page 5 lists 0.52%—this ambiguity undermines the cost justification for that specific arrangement. The report's assertion that the Aviva platform is 'less expensive' (like-for-like) rests on charges data that is internally inconsistent. (5) GUARD APPLICATION: The first guard states 'Where costs fall and projections improve, the absence of a justification is not a finding.' However, here costs fall only without adviser fees (a material caveat); projections worsen (£2,000–£3,000 less at mid and high growth rates per P1); and the justification relies on a critical yield (4.8%) that is not supported by the evidence (3.2% shown in P1). The second guard ('a justification that repeats the benefit without connecting it to the cost is weak') applies: the report's statement about 'reduced volatility' is a benefit asserted but not connected to the cost increase when adviser fees are included. The third guard addresses CHK-007 (fair value), not this check—here the issue is whether the client's explanation is clear and consistent. VULNERABILITY OVERLAY: The risk profile report (P22) shows the client's natural risk level was 4 (Cautious Balanced) but was agreed at 5 (Balanced) after discussion. The extraction metadata notes this override explicitly. There is no evidence of any actual or potential vulnerability characteristics (age, cognitive, support needs, communication requirements) that would materially affect the assessment of whether disadvantages and risks are fairly explained. The check proceeds on clarity and consistency, which are not altered by the risk-level agreement process itself.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report discloses material disadvantages including: (1) limited withdrawal flexibility on the new plan (page 6); (2) underperformance of blended funds versus Aviva options (page 7); (3) market timing risk during transaction periods (page 8); (4) access delays of 10-15 working days (page 8); (5) excluded transaction costs (page 10); (6) need for ongoing risk profile review (page 4); (7) limitations of cashflow projections (page 15); (8) exclusion of long-term care costs from projections (page 15); (9) pension switch risks with no performance guarantee (page 12). The report also addresses tax consequences of small pots encashment (Scottish Widows withdrawal at £170 net from £211.32 gross). Product-specific risks are stated. Loss scenarios are illustrated through critical yield tables showing switching outcomes at low (0%), mid (2.95%), and high (5.88%) growth rates, demonstrating potential decreases of £10-£15,000 depending on scenario.
    File:     The evidence includes product literature (Aviva, Standard Life, Zurich, SIPP research) disclosing: (1) income drawdown risks including means-tested benefits impact and income depletion risks (P11); (2) investment volatility and counterparty, credit, and default risks (P18); (3) fund liquidity constraints and property valuation timing (P11); (4) currency and derivative risks (P18); (5) the Aviva plan does not facilitate Uncrystallised Funds Pension Lump Sum (UFPLS) as noted in Zurich policy (P24); (6) Standard Life allows partial transfers but Aviva does not clearly state this position; (7) Zurich policy requires medical questionnaire for new life cover and waiver benefits (P8), which are 'no longer available on new pension plans' (P8); (8) warnings about pension scams (P21, P24); (9) charge information showing existing Aviva fund charge of 0.50% (P16). The pension switch report shows projected decreases across growth scenarios: low growth £10,400 (9.6%), mid growth £12,000 (9.4%), high growth £15,000 (10%) when retaining one or more existing pensions versus switching all (P10).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states limited flexibility on the new plan but does not explain that Aviva Pension Portfolio explicitly offers income drawdown, lump sum, annuity, and combination options (P20), which contradicts the severity of the flexibility limitation claimed. The report's statement on page 6 that the client 'would be unable to access this pension flexibly' appears overstated.
      - The report discloses underperformance of blended funds marginally (page 7) but the supporting research (P5, P6) shows the Aviva arrangement itself currently invests in 'Aviva pension my future focus consolidation' (a consolidation/lifestyle fund), while the recommendation switches to 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' and 'CT Universal MAP Balanced' – entirely different funds – making the comparison unclear and the materiality of underperformance not properly contextualised.
      - The report states on page 6 that the Scottish Widows pension would be withdrawn as a 'small pot' yielding £170 net, but does not disclose that this triggers immediate income tax consequences at the client's marginal rate on 75% of the fund, nor does it cross-reference the income drawdown risks disclosed in product literature (P11) regarding means-tested benefits impact, which may affect the client's circumstances.
      - The report does not disclose material information from Zurich policy (P8) that life cover and waiver benefits 'are no longer available on new pension plans' and the client 'would need to complete a medical questionnaire' – this is a specific feature being lost in the transfer that is not mentioned.
      - The report discloses that transaction costs are excluded from quoted charges (page 10) but does not quantify or illustrate the potential impact, leaving the materiality of this limitation unclear.
      - The report does not reference the income drawdown risks documented in the Key Features (P11) specifically addressing means-tested benefits interactions, income depletion scenarios, and tax position accuracy – these are material risks for this client whose cashflow includes Sea Cadets volunteer income and whose circumstances may interact with means-tested provision.
      - The pension switch report (P10) shows projected values retaining one or more existing pensions at mid growth of £127,000 (existing) versus £115,000 (new solution) with a £12,000 (9.4%) decrease, but the main suitability report does not clearly present this adverse scenario in a way that gives equal prominence to the benefits claimed; the loss scenarios appear in tables in appendices rather than integrated into the narrative recommendation.
      - The report does not explain that the recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy, CT Universal MAP Balanced) are not Aviva-branded funds but external third-party funds on the Aviva platform, whereas the existing Aviva fund is Aviva's own consolidation fund – this is a material difference in fund type and strategy not disclosed.
    The report addresses multiple categories of disadvantages, risks, and limitations, and most are corroborated by the evidence. However, several material risks identified in the file are either omitted, understated, or not properly contextualised. First, the claim that the new plan would limit withdrawal flexibility (page 6) is contradicted by the Aviva Key Features (P20), which explicitly detail multiple retirement options including income drawdown, demonstrating flexibility. This is an internal inconsistency where the report's own source material does not support the assertion. Second, the underperformance claim regarding blended funds lacks specificity because the existing Aviva fund is a consolidation/lifestyle fund, and the new recommendation involves third-party multi-asset funds – these are fundamentally different types of funds, making a direct performance comparison misleading. Third, the Scottish Widows small pots withdrawal is disclosed (£170 net), but the report does not adequately disclose the tax consequences at marginal rate or cross-reference the means-tested benefits risks documented in the Key Features (P11), which state 'The income you take may have an impact on any means-tested benefits you're receiving.' For a client with modest volunteer income (£20/week), this is material. Fourth, the loss of life cover and waiver benefits requiring medical questionnaire (P8, Zurich policy) is not disclosed in the report at all – this is a material disadvantage being incurred. Fifth, the pension switch analysis shows significant projected decreases (£12,000 at mid growth when retaining existing pensions, P10), but the report's narrative does not give this scenario equal prominence; the losses appear in appendix tables while the recommendation is presented earlier. The guards indicate generic market-risk wording need not be repeated and that research findings undercutting the recommendation constitute material information the report must address. The report does disclose many standard risks (market timing, access delays, transaction costs, projection limitations), but the specific risks identified in this client's case file – means-tested benefits interaction, loss of life cover without disclosure, and the true nature of the fund switch – are not adequately disclosed or are actively contradicted by the evidence.
