CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Aviva charge comparison: report states '0.06% more expensive' for Aviva existing (0.50%) vs new (0.44%), but 0.50% - 0.44% = 0.06% difference, which is arithmetically consistent; however, the report also assesses this as 'Better' for the new solution, which is correct directionally. Zurich charge comparison: report asserts existing 0.93% vs recommended 0.44%, but the pension switch report [P3] shows Zurich only comparison with existing 0.29% reduction in yield (not 0.93%) at one point, creating internal inconsistency across documents. Standard Life charges: extraction states 0.52% fund charge on page 5 of suitability report; however, the charge arithmetic analysis (derived figures) notes on page 11 (Pension Switch Charges table) the 'Current Annual Recurring Charge' appears as 0.18% with impact -0.26%, implying a confusion between gross fund charge and net recurring charge after credits. 33 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts 10 product/investment comparison dimensions across 5 existing arrangements being consolidated into 1 new Aviva Platform solution. For Aviva (EA3): existing 0.50% fund charge vs recommended 0.44% (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment mgmt), assessed as 'Better'; existing 'Risk level 2; lifestyling funds' vs recommended 'Risk level 5 - Balanced; HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (50%) + CT Universal MAP Balanced (50%)', assessed as 'Better'; existing 'Aviva pension my future focus consolidation; limited range' vs recommended '80+ funds available', assessed as 'Better'; existing 'Does not allow adviser management' vs recommended 'Allows adviser charging facilitation', assessed as 'Better'; existing retirement options 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS' vs recommended 'Flexi Access Drawdown with greater flexibility', assessed as 'Equivalent'. For Zurich (EA4): existing 0.93% total charge vs recommended 0.44%, assessed as 'Better'; existing 'Risk level 7 (Managed AP fund); does not offer risk level 5 funds; blend of level 7 and level 2 required' vs recommended 'Risk level 5 - Balanced; HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (50%) + CT Universal MAP Balanced (50%)', assessed as 'Better'; existing '60+ funds available' vs recommended '80+ funds available', assessed as 'Better'; existing 'Does not allow adviser management' vs recommended 'Allows adviser charging facilitation', assessed as 'Better'; existing 'Full and partial UFPLS only' vs recommended 'Flexi Access Drawdown with enhanced flexibility', assessed as 'Better'. For Standard Life (EA5): existing funds 'SL At Retirement Universal (PP 10 Year) Pension Fd' vs recommended Aviva platform funds; existing charges 0.52% fund charge vs recommended 0.44%; existing retirement options 'FAD' vs recommended 'Flexi Access Drawdown'. Performance data presented: Aviva existing 15.58% (5y cumulative), Zurich existing 44.32% (5y cumulative), Standard Life existing 24.56% (5y cumulative). The report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Aviva and shows charge comparison for Zurich as '0.93% / £961.98 → -0.49% / -£506.85'. Quote on page 7: 'Your funds are currently invested in lifestyling funds, which automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement. As a result, your portfolio will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range.' Quote on page 7: 'Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, so to achieve this profile, a blend of a risk level 7 and a risk level 2 fund has been used'. Quote page 11: 'Like for Like: ...our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans. Including Adviser Fee: ...our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans.' Critical yield for mid-growth: 4.8% per annum (page 11, Appendix).
    File:     [P1] Policy Research table shows retirement options for 5 arrangements: People's Pension has FAD and Full/partial UFPLS; Scottish Widows has Full/partial UFPLS only (no FAD listed); Aviva has FAD and Full/partial UFPLS; Zurich has Full/partial UFPLS only (no FAD); Standard Life has FAD only. Fund range: People's Pension 7 funds, Scottish Widows 29 funds, Aviva 80+ funds, Zurich 60+ funds, Standard Life 300 funds. Adviser charging: People's Pension No, Scottish Widows No, Aviva No, Zurich No, Standard Life Yes. [P3] Pension Switch Report shows comparison summary table ranking 'Existing Solution' (0.3% reduction in yield, £127,000 projected value at mid-growth) vs 'New Solution' (0.6% reduction in yield, £125,000 projected value). Effect of switching: -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease at mid-growth. Critical yield required: 3.2%. [P16] Suitability Report existing arrangements table states: Aviva (SP50163762) charges '0.50% fund and £4.50 annual admin charge'; Scottish Widows (ZU4225575) charges '0.19% fund and 0.10% platform'; Zurich (P10026-719-001/DL) charges '0.93% platform'; Standard Life (D2301334000) charges '0.52% fund charge'. Retirement options: People's Pension 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS'; Scottish Widows 'Full and partial UFPLS'; Aviva 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS'; Zurich 'Full and partial UFPLS'; Standard Life 'FAD'. Number of funds: People's Pension 7, Scottish Widows 29, Aviva 80+, Zurich 60+, Standard Life 300. Adviser servicing: all 'No' except Standard Life (not stated in row but implied from context as having adviser capability). [P18] Aviva Policy Info confirms plan SP50163762 invests in 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2', has 'Lifestage Investment Approach', no guarantees, no Guaranteed Minimum Pension. [P6] Suitability Report performance analysis states: Aviva existing (15.58%), Zurich existing (44.32%), Standard Life (24.56%) over 5 years as at 13/10/2025. Chart shows 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds.' [P7] Key Features document describes FAD, UFPLS, capped drawdown, annuity options available on recommended scheme. [P12] Pension Switch Report new solution details: CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%); initial adviser charge £3,305.00; ongoing adviser charge 1.00%; immediate death benefit shortfall £3,305.00. [P3] Switch Report shows 'Existing pensions vs. New solution' comparison at medium growth (2.95%) projects existing £127,000 vs new £125,000 (effect of switching -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease). [P19] Suitability Report cashflow projections (real terms, retirement age 75): existing £127,000 vs new solution £115,000 at mid-growth (2.95%), effect of switching -£12,000 or -9.4% decrease; critical yield 4.8%. [P9] Pension Switch Report stakeholder pension comparison shows new solution projected value £125,000 vs existing solution £127,000; new solution reduction in yield 0.6% vs existing 0.3%; critical yield 3.2%. [P17] Pension Switch Report Alternative comparisons shows Aviva plan only: existing £4,280 vs new £4,260 at mid-growth (2.94%); Zurich only: existing £127,000 vs new £125,000 at mid-growth (2.95%), effect -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease; Standard Life only: existing £3,440 vs new £3,420 at mid-growth (2.95%), effect -£20 or -0.6% decrease.
    Discrepancies:
      - Aviva charge comparison: report states '0.06% more expensive' for Aviva existing (0.50%) vs new (0.44%), but 0.50% - 0.44% = 0.06% difference, which is arithmetically consistent; however, the report also assesses this as 'Better' for the new solution, which is correct directionally.
      - Zurich charge comparison: report asserts existing 0.93% vs recommended 0.44%, but the pension switch report [P3] shows Zurich only comparison with existing 0.29% reduction in yield (not 0.93%) at one point, creating internal inconsistency across documents.
      - Standard Life charges: extraction states 0.52% fund charge on page 5 of suitability report; however, the charge arithmetic analysis (derived figures) notes on page 11 (Pension Switch Charges table) the 'Current Annual Recurring Charge' appears as 0.18% with impact -0.26%, implying a confusion between gross fund charge and net recurring charge after credits.
      - Performance comparison: report states Zurich existing cumulative return 44.32% over 5 years; however, on page 6 of suitability report it later shows Zurich Managed Portfolio performance as 46.14% (not 44.32%), creating a date/source ambiguity.
      - Risk level for Aviva existing: report states 'Risk level 2; lifestyling funds' but extraction metadata notes this fund has 'Lifestage Investment Approach' which is auto-switching, not a fixed Risk level 2; the risk level drifts over time as client approaches retirement, so stating it as 'Risk level 2' is incomplete.
      - Retirement options for Zurich existing: report comparison states 'Full and partial UFPLS only' (no FAD), but [P1] policy research table lists Zurich as having 'Full and partial UFPLS' with FAD marked as '1' (yes), creating contradiction.
      - Fund range for Standard Life existing: report states 300 funds available on page 5 suitability report table [P16], but this is not reflected in the comparison dimension array, which omits Standard Life fund-range comparison despite that being a key 'Better' dimension for other arrangements.
      - Adviser charging capacity: report states existing arrangements do not allow adviser charging (listed as 'Does not allow adviser management' for Aviva and Zurich), but [P1] and [P16] show Standard Life as having adviser charging capability ('Yes' / 'Can adviser charging be applied: Yes'), and the recommendation notes ongoing adviser charge of 1.00%, implying the new solution does facilitate this; however, Standard Life existing was also supposed to already support this per the evidence.
      - Retirement options for Standard Life existing vs new: report comparison shows existing 'FAD' vs new 'Flexi Access Drawdown with enhanced flexibility', but both FAD and Flexi Access Drawdown (FAD) are the same product; the distinction drawn is unclear and potentially spurious.
      - Like-for-Like vs Including Adviser Fee contradiction: report page 11 states the new plan is '0.24% less expensive' without adviser fees but '4.24% more expensive' with adviser fees, meaning the switch is simultaneously cheaper and more expensive depending on fee inclusion; the report does not clearly reconcile why the recommendation stands given the advised cost is materially higher.
      - Projected maturity value discrepancy: suitability report [P19] at mid-growth shows existing £127,000 vs new solution £115,000 (effect -£12,000 or -9.4% decrease in real terms); however, pension switch report [P3] shows existing £127,000 vs new solution £125,000 (effect -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease). These are two different comparisons of the same scenario and cannot both be correct; the suitability report uses real-terms projections while the switch report uses nominal, but this critical difference is not flagged or explained to the client.
      - Critical yield inconsistency: suitability report states critical yield 4.8% per annum (page 11 Appendix); switch report shows critical yield 3.2% for the same arrangement at mid-growth; these are materially different and not reconciled.
      - Fund names for new solution: report lists recommended funds as 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' and 'CT Universal MAP Balanced', but the switch report [P12] names them 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (GBP)' and 'CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (GBP)'; while these refer to the same funds, the omission of the share class detail in the comparison is incomplete.
      - Scottish Widows treatment: report recommendation encashes Scottish Widows at small pots (£211.32 gross → £170 net), but no comparison is provided between retaining this arrangement and encashing it; it is treated as 'Retain and Encash' per [P1] rather than switched, yet the charge/benefit analysis does not address whether encashment is justified or whether retaining in a drawdown vehicle would be better.
      - Performance claim vs actual outperformance: report states [P6] 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds.' But the comparison dimensions do not include a 'Performance' row showing Zurich existing vs new solution performance, only stating that recommended funds align with risk tolerance 'with reduced volatility'; the recommendation relies on a 'comparable performance with reduced volatility' narrative without quantifying whether this trades off favorably against the 44.32% Zurich return.
    The requirement is to assess whether a recommendation to replace five existing pension arrangements into one new Aviva Platform solution is consistent with and supported by evidence. A comparison has been performed across multiple dimensions. However, several material inconsistencies and gaps emerge. First, the critical yield required for the new solution to match the existing arrangements is stated inconsistently: the suitability report asserts 4.8% per annum, while the pension switch report supporting the same recommendation shows 3.2% for a materially identical scenario. This is not a guard-explainable difference; both documents purport to analyze the same consolidation, and the lower critical yield in the switch report materially understates the investment growth required to justify the switch. Second, the projected maturity values diverge substantially between the two analyses: the suitability report (using real-terms projections, retirement age 75, mid-growth 2.95%) shows existing £127,000 vs new £115,000 (effect -£12,000 or -9.4% decrease), while the pension switch report (same mid-growth, same term) shows existing £127,000 vs new £125,000 (effect -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease). The suitability report's figure implies the client is materially worse off; the switch report's figure implies near-equivalence with a small cost. These cannot both be accurate projections of the same recommendation. The report does not explain or reconcile this discrepancy to the client, creating a material risk that the client has not seen the true detriment. Third, the charge comparison for Zurich contains an internal error: the report states the existing charge as 0.93%, but the derived figures note that the EA4 (Zurich) existing annual charge of £961.98 at 0.93% implies a fund value of £103,438.71 (which matches Zurich's actual current value), yet the same document's pension switch report shows Zurich only comparison with a 'Reduction in Yield' of 0.29% on the existing arrangement, not 0.93% recurring charge. This suggests the 0.93% may be overstated or computed differently. Fourth, the recommendation is justified in part on switching costs being lower with the new provider (like-for-like 0.24% cheaper), but when adviser fees of 1.00% ongoing are included, the plan becomes 4.24% more expensive. The report states this directly but does not clearly articulate why a client should accept a materially more expensive outcome, nor does it quantify the years of outperformance required to recoup the adviser fee and initial charge deduction (£3,305). The critical yield of 4.8% (or 3.2%, depending on document) is stated but not explained in client-friendly terms. Fifth, the retirement options comparison contains a spurious distinction: the report compares existing 'FAD' against recommended 'Flexi Access Drawdown with greater/enhanced flexibility', but FAD and Flexi Access Drawdown are the same thing; the suggestion that the new solution offers more flexibility is unsupported and misleading. The policy research evidence [P1] and Key Features [P7] show both allow the same drawdown options. Sixth, the Zurich retirement options are stated in the comparison as 'Full and partial UFPLS only' (no FAD), but the policy research table [P1] lists FAD as available ('1' = yes); this is a direct contradiction between the comparison assertion and the evidence. Seventh, for Standard Life, the report does not present a side-by-side fund-range or adviser-servicing comparison dimension, despite Standard Life having 300 funds and adviser charging capability already; the omission of this comparison for the smallest holding (£3,002) is not material individually but reflects selective comparison framing. Eighth, the Scottish Widows arrangement is recommended for encashment under small pots rules, but no explicit cost/benefit analysis of encashment vs retention is provided; the report shows an estimated net receipt of £170 but does not compare this outcome against retaining the funds in the new solution or another vehicle, nor does it address the tax efficiency of encashment vs retention. Ninth, performance data is presented for existing arrangements but the new solution's projected performance relative to existing Zurich is not quantified; the report asserts Zurich funds (44.32% over 5 years) have outperformed the recommended funds but does not show the recommended portfolio's 5-year performance track record. The narrative 'comparable performance with reduced volatility' is qualitative and not supported by a numeric comparison. Tenth, the report asserts the Aviva existing arrangement has 'limited range' and a lifestyling fund that will 'move outside of your agreed risk range' as retirement approaches, but the lifestyling approach is designed to reduce risk in line with proximity to retirement; the report does not evidence why this automatic de-risking is unsuitable or worse than a fixed Risk 5 allocation that may prove too risky closer to retirement. All of these discrepancies are material to the client's decision. The evidence base is substantial (pension switch report, policy research, fund factsheets), but the internal inconsistencies within and between the suitability report and switch report create genuine uncertainty about the true cost, benefit, and detriment of the recommendation. The client has not been presented with a single, consistent financial comparison that justifies consolidation. The recommendation may be sound, but the evidence pack does not demonstrate it coherently.

        "You can choose to take your retirement benefits in one of the following ways: take all of your pension pot as a cash lump sum; take up to 25% tax free and then take the remaining pension pot as and when you need it through drawdown; use your entire pension pot to purchase an annuity; take up to 25% tax free and use the remaining pension pot to purchase a smaller annuity."
      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (P2)
        "The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution"

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts multiple benefits of switching: Lower Costs (receiving scheme has lower overall cost structure); Greater Investment Choice (wider range of investment options); Adviser Charging Facilitation (adviser charges deducted directly); Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments (active monitoring and alignment with changing needs); Multi-asset fund selection (Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, Aviva blend performs better); Enhanced Retirement Flexibility (more flexible retirement options). On costs, the report states for People's Pension: annual charge difference -0.06% / -£2.25 (favours new scheme); for Scottish Widows: no switching costs disclosed; for Aviva: charges not separately broken down; for Zurich: annual charge difference -0.49% / -£506.85 (favours new scheme); for Standard Life: annual charge difference +0.26% / +£268.94 (new scheme more expensive). The report also states exit penalties are £0 (no charges for transfer/switching). On justifying higher costs, the report claims: 'Like for Like: the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans. Including Adviser Fee: the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans.' The critical yield required is 4.8% per annum. The report asserts this outperformance is achievable based on past performance of recommended funds.
    File:     The pension switch report (P2, P3, P6, P12, P15) provides plan-by-plan comparisons showing critical yields and effects of switching: For Zurich alone (P3): low growth 0.3% critical yield with £2,000 or 2.0% decrease; mid-growth 2.6% critical yield with £2,000 or 1.6% decrease; high-growth 5.0% critical yield with £3,000 or 1.9% decrease. For Standard Life alone (P2): low growth 6.0% critical yield with £30 or 0.6% decrease; mid-growth appears in table but values truncated; high-growth appears truncated. For all three pensions combined (P6): low growth 0.3% critical yield with £2,000 or 1.9% decrease; mid-growth 3.2% critical yield with £2,000 or 1.6% decrease; high-growth 6.2% critical yield with £3,000 or 2.0% decrease. The switch report (P12) for Zurich shows: at 0% growth £10,400 or 9.6% decrease; at 2.95% growth £12,000 or 9.4% decrease; at 5.88% growth £15,000 or 10.0% decrease — these are materially worse outcomes than other comparisons in the same document. Immediate impact shows existing pension fund value £110,185.60 vs new solution day one £106,880.60, a shortfall of £3,305 (P7, P12). The new solution investment comprises CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C 50% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50% (P7). Charges data from policy documents: Zurich transfer value £0 charge (P9); People's Pension no transfer information; Scottish Widows fund switch £0 charge (P14); Standard Life transfer charge £0 (P16). Aviva existing charge stated as 0.50% fund charge (canonical model page 5); Standard Life existing stated as 0.52% fund charge on page 5 but 0.18% recurring charge used in comparative analysis on page 11 (extraction ambiguity noted).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims Like-for-Like the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive, but for one of the three plans being switched (Standard Life per P2), the switch report shows higher reduction in yield for new solution (0.6%) than existing (0.6% at low growth, 0.6% at mid-growth) with a critical yield of 6.0% required at low growth, indicating the new solution is not less expensive for this plan.
      - Report claims -0.49% / -£506.85 annual charge difference for Zurich (favours new scheme by 0.49%), but figures computed from canonical model show £268.94 at 0.26% implies fund value £103,438.46, which is Zurich's current value, yet the row is labelled for EA5 (Standard Life, £3,002), indicating the charge comparison is computed on the wrong plan.
      - Report states annual charge difference for Standard Life as +0.26% / +£268.94 (new scheme more expensive), but P2 switch report shows Standard Life vs new solution with reduction in yield 0.6% for both at low/mid-growth and critical yield 6.0%, suggesting the new solution has materially higher charges, not just 0.26% more.
      - Canonical model shows Aviva existing charge 0.50% fund charge and £4.50 annual admin charge totalling ~0.50%, but P2 switch report for all three pensions combined shows new solution (Aviva Platform Personal Pension) with 0.6% reduction in yield at mid-growth, and earlier extraction notes state Standard Life charge stated as 0.52% on page 5 but 0.18% recurring charge on page 11 — the comparison bases are inconsistent.
      - Report claims benefits include 'Greater Investment Choice' and 'Multi-asset fund selection' to overcome Zurich's lack of risk level 5 funds, but P12 switch report for Zurich alone shows new solution produces £10,400-£15,000 decrease (9.4%-10% reduction) even at 5.88% growth, directly contradicting the claimed benefit of better fund selection justifying the switch.
      - Report asserts critical yield of 4.8% is 'achievable' based on past performance of recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced), but P12 shows Zurich switch requires 4.8% critical yield at mid-growth to avoid 9.4% loss, while P6 (all three plans) shows 3.2% critical yield at mid-growth, indicating the 4.8% figure applies only to one scenario (Zurich alone) not the aggregate recommendation.
      - Report claims no exit penalties (£0), supported by P9 Zurich (fund switch £0), P16 Standard Life (transfer charge £0), and P14 Scottish Widows (no transfer charge mentioned), but does not disclose the immediate shortfall of £3,305 (P7, P12) arising from switching, which reduces the fund value on day one of the new solution from £110,185.60 to £106,880.60, a material switching cost not addressed.
      - Report asserts 'Lower Costs' as primary benefit but canonical model and switch report (P6) show at mid-growth 2.95% the new solution has 0.6% reduction in yield vs existing 0.3%, a doubling of the charge impact, contradicting the 'lower costs' claim for the aggregate portfolio.
    The requirement is to establish whether benefits claimed for switching are consistent with and supported by evidence, and where costs are higher, whether the report explains why benefits justify them. Comparison can be performed: the switch report provides plan-by-plan and aggregate comparisons with critical yields and projected outcomes. Four material discrepancies emerge: (1) The aggregate claim of 'lower costs' (0.24% cheaper like-for-like) contradicts the switch report showing the new solution has 0.6% reduction in yield vs 0.3% for existing plans at mid-growth, a doubling of charge impact. (2) The charge comparison for Standard Life (EA5) is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438), not Standard Life's (£3,002), making that row's -0.49% / -£506.85 figure unreliable and the benefit claim for that plan unsupported. (3) The Zurich switch produces a £10,400-£15,000 projected decrease (9.4-10% reduction) even at 5.88% growth (P12), yet the report claims this switch solves the 'Multi-asset fund selection' problem — the evidence shows it worsens the outcome materially. (4) The immediate fund value shortfall of £3,305 (2.9% loss at point of switching) is not disclosed as a switching cost, though it reduces the benefit base for future growth. The report does attempt to justify costs via critical yield analysis (4.8% required at mid-growth to break even) and past performance of recommended funds, but this justification is incomplete: it assumes the client will achieve 4.8% growth, which is neither guaranteed nor evidenced by stated fund performance. The switch report shows the new solution delivers a 0.6% reduction in yield (charge drag), meaning gross returns must exceed 4.8% merely to match the existing 0.3% drag at current levels — a material hurdle not addressed as part of the 'benefits outweigh costs' reasoning. The report does not explain why the new solution's higher charge impact (0.6% vs 0.3% reduction in yield) is acceptable despite the lower running costs claim. The vulnerability overlay (noted in the check instruction) is not evident from the evidence provided; no support needs, communication requirements or reasonable adjustments are recorded that would materially affect this analysis.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states one lost feature: 'Loss of direct fund access restriction: If you were to transfer all your pensions into this plan, it would limit how you can withdraw funds. In the future, this would be important to you, as you would be unable to access this pension flexibly.' (page 6). The report states all five existing arrangements have no safeguarded benefits (page 5, repeated for each): 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits'. No employer contributions are reported as lost across the four analysed arrangements.
    File:     Provider documentation confirms: Scottish Widows (EA2, ZU4225575) contains no Guaranteed Annuity Rates, Guaranteed Minimum Pension, life cover, or safeguarded benefits [P2, P10]. Aviva (EA3, SP50163762) has no guaranteed rates, no Guaranteed Minimum Pension, no life cover, no waiver of payment [P17]. Standard Life (EA5, D2301334000) has no Guaranteed Annuity Rates, no Guaranteed Minimum Pension, and no safeguarded benefits [P4, P23]. Zurich (EA4) confirms 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan' [P15]. People's Pension (EA1) provides no statement of safeguarded benefits in retrieved documentation. However, Zurich policy information [P1] states: 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans. You would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan. This will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits.' This indicates life cover and waiver benefits may have existed on Zurich but are not available on the new plan. Additionally, [P1] asks whether client has 'Guaranteed rates?' with 'Some of our plans have guaranteed annuity rates when you retire which may give you a higher pension than you could get based on current rates.' This raises whether Zurich had a GAR that would be lost.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report discloses loss of withdrawal flexibility on Scottish Widows (EA2) but does not address that Zurich (EA4) policy info indicates life cover and waiver benefits are 'no longer available on new pension plans', suggesting these may be valuable features being given up that are not analysed in the suitability report.
      - The report states all five existing arrangements contain no safeguarded benefits, but Zurich policy documentation contains questions about guaranteed annuity rates and implies some plans do have such guarantees; the report does not reconcile whether Zurich's specific plan held a GAR.
      - The report does not analyse whether the encashment of Scottish Widows (EA2) under small pots rules involves loss of any features (such as potential protected tax-free cash or future flexibility), nor does it reference provider documentation on this point.
    All five existing arrangements are stated to have no safeguarded benefits, corroborated by provider documentation from Scottish Widows [P2, P10], Aviva [P17], Standard Life [P4, P23], and Zurich [P15]. The report discloses one lost feature: withdrawal flexibility on Scottish Widows consolidation. However, the evidence raises a material discrepancy. Zurich's own policy information [P1] contains explicit warnings about life cover and waiver benefits, stating 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans' and asking the client whether they had guaranteed annuity rates. This language indicates such features may exist on the current Zurich plan (EA4, value £103,439.24—the largest arrangement by value). The report does not address whether Zurich's existing plan (EA4) contains life cover, waiver of premium, or guaranteed annuity rates that would be lost on transfer to Aviva Platform. The report's blanket assertion that 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits' for all five arrangements, whilst consistent with the narrow regulatory definition of 'safeguarded benefits' (which has a specific technical meaning under pension law), does not address the more general category of valuable guarantees, options, and protections that the Zurich provider documentation explicitly identifies as at risk. The report also encashes Scottish Widows (EA2, £211.32) without discussing any features lost in that process, beyond the withdrawal flexibility point. The provider documentation on Scottish Widows [P7] notes 'Transfer of the plan or claiming UFPLS may result in any right to a protected tax free cash or protected pension age being lost,' but the report does not reference this. The report's analysis of lost features is therefore incomplete: it discloses one feature (withdrawal flexibility on SW) but fails to analyse the potentially significant loss of life cover, waiver benefits, and guaranteed rates on the largest ceding plan (Zurich, EA4), and does not address whether encashment of Scottish Widows involves loss of protected tax-free cash or pension age rights.

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states that two alternatives to switching were considered: (1) 'Retain existing Aviva pension with internal fund switch' was considered and rejected because 'Your funds are currently invested in lifestyling funds, which automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement. As a result, your portfolio will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range. We would not recommend remaining invested in these types of funds.' (2) 'Retain Zurich with internal fund switch within risk range' was considered and rejected on two grounds: (a) 'Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, so to achieve this profile, a blend of a risk level 7 and a risk level 2 fund would be required. As there are no multi-asset funds available within your risk range, the blend consists of the Managed AP fund (in which you are already invested) and an asset-specific fund, Interest Deposits. We would not recommend investing in single asset types, as this reduces diversification.' and (b) 'Your main priority is to consolidate your pensions, gain better control over them, and be well-positioned for retirement with all available drawdown options.' Both rejections are stated as client-specific. No alternatives beyond these two in-situ switches are documented as having been considered.
    File:     The evidence in [P1] confirms that the report states 'I do not recommend that you consolidate into this plan as the funds do not meet your risk profile; Currently, Scottish Widows does not offer a single multi-asset fund that aligns with your risk rating of 5.' It also confirms retention and encashment advice for Scottish Widows and People's Pension, and a switch recommendation for Aviva and Standard Life. [P3] shows a table of existing schemes with advice columns indicating 'Retain', 'Encash', and 'Switch to Aviva Platform' for different plans. [P6] restates the Zurich rejection: 'Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, so to achieve this profile, a blend of a risk level 7 and a risk level 2 fund has been used. As there are no multi-asset funds available within your risk range, the blend consists of the Managed AP fund (in which you are already invested) and an asset-specific fund, Interest Deposits. We would not recommend investing in single asset types, as this reduces diversification, and the blended funds have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally. For these reasons, we would not recommend an internal fund switch, as your main priority is to consolidate your pensions, gain better control over them, and be well-positioned for retirement with all available drawdown options.' The pension switch report [P9], [P12], [P13], [P16], [P18], [P19] shows detailed charge and maturity value comparisons for all three switching scenarios (Aviva only, Standard Life only, Zurich only, and consolidated), with critical yield analysis and effect of switching calculations. However, no documented consideration of alternatives such as: retaining all pensions as-is without any switching; retaining some pensions and switching others on an individual basis (other than what was recommended); consolidating into a different platform or provider; or phased switching to mitigate timing risk.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states that only two alternatives (internal fund switches for Aviva and Zurich) were considered and rejected. No evidence in the file documents consideration of the alternative to take no action at all—that is, retaining all existing arrangements unchanged. While the switch report [P9] compares switching against retention implicitly in its 'effect of switching' calculations, there is no explicit statement in the suitability report that the option of doing nothing was considered and compared.
      - The report does not document consideration of alternative consolidation targets. The switch report [P9] shows comparisons for Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life individually, implying analysis of switching each plan separately, but the suitability report does not explicitly state whether consolidation into a different platform (e.g., Scottish Widows or other providers) or a different form of arrangement (e.g., a workplace scheme if available, or a SIPP outside Aviva) was considered.
      - The report rejects the Zurich internal switch partly on the ground that 'the blended funds have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' [P6]. This claim is not substantiated in the evidence: the pension switch report shows projected maturity values for Zurich switching scenarios, but no historical performance comparison between the blended Zurich funds and the recommended Aviva funds is presented in the file.
    The requirement asks whether alternatives to switching are considered and the consistency between the report's assertion and the evidence. The report asserts that two alternatives were considered and rejected: an internal Aviva fund switch and an internal Zurich fund switch. Both rejections are documented and corroborated by the evidence at [P1] and [P6]. The Aviva rejection (lifestyling drift) is stated and evidenced. The Zurich rejection is stated in both the report and [P6], citing limited fund range and the primacy of the consolidation objective. The pension switch report [P9] provides comparative analysis (critical yield, effect of switching, projected maturity values) for each plan individually and consolidated, demonstrating that quantitative comparison was undertaken. However, the suitability report does not explicitly document consideration of the null alternative—retaining all pensions unchanged—despite that being the baseline against which any switch recommendation should be measured. The switch report's 'effect of switching' calculations do implicitly compare the switched scenario against the existing one, and these show losses of varying magnitude (£3,000–£15,000 depending on growth rate for the three-plan consolidation at [P13]), yet the suitability report does not discuss whether these detriments were outweighed by non-quantified benefits such as consolidation, control, and drawdown flexibility. The Zurich rejection states performance of the blended funds 'have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' but no comparative performance data is cited. The guard (Options assessed once for the whole case may legitimately cover several plans — check whether the rejection reasoning is plan-specific where the plans differ materially) does apply: the Aviva and Zurich rejections are materially different (lifestyling drift vs. limited fund range) and therefore plan-specific, which is appropriate. However, the absence of an explicit documented consideration of the no-action baseline, and the absence of performance substantiation for the Zurich claim, means the alternatives analysis is incomplete. The suitability report's statement of considered alternatives is partially supported but not comprehensively evidenced.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts four switching analyses with specific cost and benefit profiles: (1) Scottish Widows £211.32 (encashment with net receipt £170): no exit penalty, charge difference -0.06% / -£2.25, benefits stated as lower costs and greater choice. (2) Zurich £103,439.24: no exit penalty, charge difference -0.49% / -£506.85, benefits include lower costs, investment choice, multi-asset fund selection. (3) Standard Life £3,002.00: no exit penalty, charge difference +0.26% / +£268.94, higher costs justified by multi-asset and greater investment choice. (4) Aviva existing arrangement £3,744.36 mentioned with no formal cost analysis provided. Report states 'Like for Like' comparison shows Aviva plan 0.24% less expensive; 'Including Adviser Fee' shows 4.24% more expensive. Critical yield for Zurich analysis: 4.8% pa required to break even. Benefits cited across all plans: lower costs, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, lifestyling adjustment, enhanced retirement flexibility. Report claims benefits justify switching despite adviser fees increasing overall cost.
    File:     Pension switch report (P1-P8) corroborates the following: Three main comparisons (Zurich, Aviva, Standard Life) with side-by-side projected maturity values at low, mid and high growth rates. Zurich comparison (P7): Existing pension RIY 0.3%, New solution RIY 2.1% at low growth; RIY 2.3% at high growth. Critical yield 4.8% at mid growth (P7). Effect of switching: £10,400 decrease (9.6%) at low growth, £12,000 decrease (9.4%) at mid growth, £15,000 decrease (10.0%) at high growth. Standard Life comparison (P6): Existing pension RIY 0.6%, New solution RIY 0.6% (matching). Critical yield 6.0% for Standard Life only analysis. No figures matching report's claimed -0.49%/£506.85 for Zurich or +0.26%/£268.94 for Standard Life charge differences. Aviva comparison with all three retained: overall projected value £124,000 (P3). Immediate impact statement (P7): Fund shortfall of £3,305.00 on day one. No mention of Scottish Widows encashment analysis. Charge comparison on page P4 (Existing pensions vs. New solution table) shows new solution platform at 0.6% charge; Aviva retained at 0.59%; Zurich retained at 0.29%; all figures differ from report's switching cost assertions.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts Zurich charge difference -0.49%/£506.85 annual saving; evidence (P7) shows Zurich existing RIY 0.3%, new solution RIY 2.1% (low growth), 2.2% (mid growth), 2.3% (high growth), implying new solution is MORE expensive, not less. The 0.26% increase claimed by report for Standard Life aligns directionally with evidence but the magnitude and basis are not independently verified in the passages.
      - Report asserts Scottish Widows -0.06%/£2.25 saving; no corresponding analysis found in evidence passages for this plan.
      - Report states 'Like for Like 0.24% less expensive' overall across all plans; evidence does not contain a consolidated charge comparison table matching this figure.
      - Report states 'Including Adviser Fee 4.24% more expensive' with derived figure of £4,672.00 required outperformance at 4.24%; this calculation basis is not shown in evidence.
      - Report asserts critical yield 1.9% for Scottish Widows and 7.8% for unspecified plan; evidence (P7) confirms 4.8% and 7.8% for Zurich but does not show 1.9% matching any named plan.
      - Immediate impact: Report does not explicitly disclose the £3,305.00 day-one shortfall in fund value on switching (noted in P7) as a cost of switching.
      - Evidence shows Zurich new solution projected values are materially lower than existing (£97,600 vs £108,000 at low growth; £115,000 vs £127,000 at mid growth), contradicting the report's assertion of cost savings as a primary benefit for this plan.
    The comparison can be performed because the evidence pack contains detailed switching analyses. However, material contradictions emerge when the report's stated charge differences are checked against the passages. (1) For Zurich (the largest plan by value at £103,439.24): The report claims a -0.49% annual charge saving (£506.85), yet the evidence shows the new solution has a higher Reduction in Yield (RIY) than the existing plan across all growth scenarios (existing RIY 0.3%, new RIY 2.1-2.3%). RIY is the reduction in investment return caused by charges and fees; a higher RIY means higher costs, not lower. The critical yield of 4.8% confirms the new solution must outperform by this margin merely to match the existing plan's maturity value—this is a cost, not a saving. The report's own figures contradict its stated benefit. (2) The Standard Life analysis is also problematic. The report asserts +0.26% cost increase (£268.94), which aligns directionally with evidence, but the evidence shows RIY of 0.6% for both existing and new solution at certain growth rates, suggesting equivalence, not clear cost detriment. The justification that 'multi-asset fund selection' outweighs cost is present but not quantified. (3) Scottish Widows encashment (£211.32, net receipt £170): The report claims -0.06% saving but provides no detailed comparison in the evidence. This is the smallest plan and is being encashed entirely, so comparative fund-level charges are not applicable; the 'saving' is unclear. (4) The claim that benefits justify cost increases ('Like for Like' 0.24% cheaper vs 'Including Adviser Fee' 4.24% more expensive) creates a logical tension: the adviser fee itself erodes the projected savings by 4.48 percentage points. The critical yield analysis shows that for Zurich, a mid-growth scenario requires 4.8% annual outperformance—a demanding hurdle that is not explicitly presented to the client as a contingency for the switch to be worthwhile. (5) The guard 'Non-financial benefits can justify net cost increase where argued explicitly' applies partly here. The report does articulate benefits (investment choice, monitoring, flexibility), but the scale of cost increase for Zurich is not proportional to these benefits, and the evidence's own maturity value projections show consistently lower projected values for the new solution. (6) Safeguarded benefits and guarantees: The report does not address whether the existing Zurich, Aviva or Standard Life arrangements contain guarantees, bonuses, or protected benefits that would be lost. The evidence mentions 'lifestyling' in Zurich (reducing risk as client approaches retirement), which is a valuable feature being replaced by manual portfolio selection on Aviva; this trade-off is noted in passing but not fully analysed. (7) Alternatives: The evidence shows alternative comparisons (Aviva-only, Standard Life-only, Zurich-only), but these are presented as scenarios within the main recommendation, not as independently considered alternatives to consolidation.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states on page 5 that all five existing arrangements have 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' and asserts no exit penalties. The report does not explicitly extract or analyse safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features across the existing arrangements that might be lost on switching. The canonical model shows exitPenalties as false for all five arrangements but contains no field for safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp, indicating either silence or extraction failure on this material point.
    File:     Scottish Widows (EA2, ZU4225575) documentation [P13] confirms: 'I can also confirm that there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan.' The Standard Life illustration [P11] and Aviva illustration [P9] contain no statement of guarantees, bonuses or valuable features. The Zurich pension documentation is not provided in the case file. The Fact Find [P14] template includes fields for 'Guaranteed Annuity Rate / Pension (or similar)', 'EFI/Loyalty Bonus/Terminal Bonus', 'Guaranteed Growth Rates', and 'Death in service spousal benefits', but the entries are blank or show £0.00 for death in service. The report's own Appendix analysis does not systematically disclose safeguarded benefits across all five arrangements.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts no exit penalties for all five arrangements but does not address whether any carry safeguarded benefits, guarantees, bonuses or other valuable features that would be forfeited on switch or transfer.
      - Scottish Widows is documented as having no guarantees, discounts or bonuses [P13], but this confirmation is not cited in the report's assertion.
      - Zurich (EA4, £103,439.24 current value—the largest arrangement) has no provider documentation in the case file confirming the presence or absence of safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features.
      - The Fact Find template [P14] includes prompts for guaranteed annuity rates, bonuses and death benefits, but these fields are blank or empty, leaving unclear whether investigation occurred and found none, or was incomplete.
      - The report recommends switching Aviva (EA3) and Standard Life (EA5) into a new Aviva solution, and encashing Scottish Widows (EA2), but provides no plan-specific analysis of safeguarded benefits being given up for any of the three.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Confirmation from Zurich documentation of safeguarded benefits, guarantees, bonuses or valuable features on EA4 (Zurich pension, current value £103,439.24—the largest single arrangement)., Systematic disclosure in the report of whether each of the five existing arrangements carries safeguarded benefits, guarantees, enhanced tax-free cash, annuity options, death benefits, bonuses or other valuable features, and if so, whether these are being given up on switch/transfer/encashment and how that disadvantage (if any) has been addressed or justified., Provider confirmation for Aviva (EA3) and Standard Life (EA5) of safeguarded benefits or guarantees, if any.
    The requirement is that where safeguarded benefits or guarantees exist and are being forfeited, the report must either demonstrate no disadvantage results, or evidence the client's informed acceptance of the loss. The report does not perform this analysis. It confirms Scottish Widows has no guarantees [P13], which satisfies the requirement for that arrangement. However, for Zurich—the largest holding at £103,439.24—no provider documentation is present in the case file to confirm the presence or absence of safeguarded benefits. The Fact Find [P14] template prompts for guaranteed annuity rates, bonuses and death in service benefits, and these are left blank, raising uncertainty whether the adviser investigated these features and found none, or omitted the investigation. The report's recommendation to switch Aviva and Standard Life, and encash Scottish Widows, proceeds without extracting or citing safeguarded benefits for any of them. The absence of Zurich provider documentation is material: this is the plan whose value accounts for approximately 88% of the total portfolio (£103,439 of £117,000). Without evidence confirming whether Zurich carries safeguarded benefits, the report's claim that switching causes no disadvantage cannot be verified. For Aviva and Standard Life, provider illustrations are present but do not systematically address safeguarded benefits. The extraction metadata notes that /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent, indicating the report did not extract or disclose this field. This is a material omission when recommending switches affecting over £116,000 in pension assets.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report considers four main alternatives: (1) Retain existing Aviva pension with internal fund switch—rejected because lifestyling funds would eventually move outside the agreed risk range of 5; (2) Retain Zurich with internal fund switch within risk range—rejected because Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, and a blend of risk 7 and risk 2 funds would reduce diversification, plus the client's priority is consolidation with full drawdown options; (3) Switch into employer's pension arrangement—rejected because employer contributions would be lost, funds do not meet risk profile, plan is 0.06% more expensive, and has limited fund range with no multi-asset funds; (4) Set up new pension plan (Aviva Platform)—recommended. The report also considers: Scottish Widows (retain and encash under small pots rules), People's Pension (workplace scheme, current contributions continue), and Standard Life (switch to Aviva recommended). Cost analysis states 'Like for Like' the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive; 'Including Adviser Fee' the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive. Critical yield required is 4.8% per annum at mid growth rate.
    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Zurich comparison—switching Zurich alone: at mid-growth 2.95%, existing projected value £127,000 vs new solution £115,000 (£12,000 or 9.4% decrease); critical yield 4.8%; reduction in yield existing 0.3% vs new 2.2%. Overall position retaining Zurich and switching others: new solution £4,260 + Zurich retained £120,000 + Standard Life retained £3,440 = £127,000 total. (2) Aviva comparison—switching Aviva alone: at mid-growth, existing £4,280 vs new £4,260 (£10 or 0.3% decrease); critical yield 2.9%; both have 0.6% reduction in yield. Overall retaining Aviva: new solution + Aviva retained £4,280 + Zurich retained £120,000 + Standard Life retained £3,440 = £127,000 total. (3) Standard Life comparison—at mid-growth existing £3,440 vs new £3,430 (minimal difference); critical yield 3.2%. (4) Overall comparison all five schemes: existing £127,000 vs new solution £115,000 (£12,000 or 9.4% decrease) at mid-growth, with critical yield 4.8%. (5) Research shows death benefit shortfall immediately after switch: existing £110,185.60 vs new day one £106,880.60 = £3,305 shortfall. (6) Performance data shows Aviva Pension—My Future Focus Consolidation (existing) cumulative 5-year performance 15.58% vs Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (recommended funds) 37.25%, but report warns past performance is no guarantee of future results.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Like for Like' Aviva is 0.24% less expensive but immediately below states 'Including Adviser Fee' Aviva is 4.24% more expensive—these are contradictory directional claims about the same recommendation.
      - Report states cost comparison is 0.24% cheaper without adviser fees, but all material projections in the evidence include the £3,305 initial adviser charge and 1.00% ongoing adviser fee; the like-for-like scenario is not evidenced in the maturity value projections.
      - Report claims Zurich switch is justified partly because 'Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds' and recommends a blend to reduce diversification risk, yet evidence shows consolidated position retaining Zurich (£120,000 at 0.29% RiY) with Aviva new solution (£4,260 at 0.6% RiY) yields identical total maturity value of £127,000 as retaining all existing arrangements untouched.
      - Report states consolidation and 'all available drawdown options' are client priorities justifying the switch, but evidence shows Scottish Widows encashment (no drawdown), People's Pension workplace scheme retention, and Zurich retention are all factored into final recommendation—consolidation is incomplete.
      - Report's claim that employer scheme is rejected because 'funds do not meet your risk profile' and is '0.06% more expensive' is not substantiated in the evidence provided; no detailed cost comparison table for employer scheme appears in the pension switch report.
      - Report projects £110,185 transfer value for new Aviva consolidation but calculation basis notes (charge arithmetic) show investment management charge at 0.22% implies £115,195 fund value and platform charge at 0.21% implies £110,185—the figures do not reconcile to a single actual fund value.
      - Report presents critical yield of 4.8% (requiring average real growth 4.8% p.a. to match existing arrangement) but this is at mid growth rate 2.95% on consolidated all-schemes scenario; individual scheme switches show lower critical yields (2.9% for Aviva alone, 3.2% for Standard Life alone, 0.3% for Zurich alone), suggesting risk profile varies materially by arrangement yet report treats all five as a single unit.
      - Report states Standard Life existing charge is 0.52% on page 5 arrangement table but page 11 Pension Switch Charges table shows Standard Life 'Current Annual Recurring Charge' as 0.18%—a £0.34% variance that affects any comparison of relative costs.
    The requirement tests whether alternatives considered provide better outcomes. The comparison can be performed: the report explicitly considers five named alternatives, and the pension switch evidence provides detailed maturity value projections, critical yields, and charges for each. The guard permits consolidation where the client requested it; evidence in P19 shows 'consolidation' and 'gain better control' are stated client priorities (P10, P17 confirm this). However, critical tensions emerge. First, the report's headline claim—that Aviva is 0.24% cheaper on a 'like for like' basis—is immediately contradicted by the statement that including adviser fees it costs 4.24% more. The like-for-like comparison excludes the £3,305 initial charge and 1% ongoing fee, yet these are material and built into all maturity value projections in the evidence (P13, P14 show initial charge reduces new solution fund to £106,880.60; P14 states ongoing 1% adviser charge is 'considered as part of the new solution projections'). The report presents both scenarios but the maturity values given throughout reflect only the adviser-fee-inclusive version. Second, the Zurich alternative illustrates the core tension: switching Zurich alone yields a £12,000 (9.4%) projected decrease at mid-growth (P3 and P13), requiring a 4.8% critical yield just to break even. Yet the overall position (P3, P8) shows retaining Zurich and switching only the three smaller pots (Aviva, Standard Life, People's Pension) yields an identical total maturity value of £127,000 as the recommended all-in-one consolidation. The report does not explain why the marginal benefit of consolidating Zurich outweighs its performance drag. Third, the report's rationale for rejecting Zurich in-situ switch (lifestyling fund drift, limited multi-asset range) is sound, but the evidence shows Zurich's reduction in yield is only 0.3% versus Aviva Platform's 0.6% (P3 Zurich comparison table), making retention cheaper on an ongoing basis. The consolidation objective appears to override cost and outcome analysis for this largest holding. Fourth, regarding death benefits: the evidence (P4, P13) discloses an immediate £3,305 shortfall on switching (existing £110,185.60 → new £106,880.60), but the report does not quantify whether this is recovered over time or represents a permanent detriment. The report's stated benefits—'greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation'—are qualitative and are not contrasted against the 9.4% projected maturity decrease or critical yield hurdle. Fifth, performance data (P15) shows the existing Aviva fund (My Future Focus Consolidation) returned 15.58% over 5 years while recommended funds returned 37.25%, yet the report correctly warns past performance is no guarantee. However, the extract in the extraction metadata notes that Standard Life charge statement is ambiguous (0.52% vs 0.18%), affecting the reliability of the cost comparison that justifies switching that plan. Finally, the report's treatment of alternatives is selective: it lists four main options and rejects three with stated reasons, but for Standard Life and Scottish Widows it provides shorter narratives. Scottish Widows is to be encashed (small pots), not consolidated, yet this is not framed as an alternative rejected but as a subsidiary action. The analysis does not explore whether retaining the largest three schemes (Zurich £103,439, Aviva £3,744, Standard Life £3,002 = £110,185 total) and encashing only the small pots (People's £6,601, Scottish Widows £211 = £6,812) would meet the client's objectives at lower cost and risk. In sum: alternatives were considered, rationales were given, but the switch to a consolidated single Aviva solution produces a projected 9.4% maturity value decrease versus the best alternative (retain Zurich, switch others), requires a critical yield of 4.8% p.a. to break even, and the report's claimed cost saving (0.24%) applies only before adviser fees are removed—a scenario not reflected in the stated maturity values. The weakness is not absence of comparison but the mismatch between claimed benefit (cost saving, consolidation) and evidenced outcome (maturity decrease, high critical yield).

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report claims three key benefits justify the switches: (1) Lower Costs – the receiving scheme has lower overall cost structure, with the Aviva plan stated as 0.24% cheaper on a like-for-like basis (page 11). (2) Greater Investment Choice – access to wider range of investment options tailored to risk profile. (3) Multi-asset fund selection for Zurich switch – the report states Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, so a blend of risk level 7 and level 2 funds was used, and that multi-asset funds available through Aviva have performed better. (4) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility – more flexible retirement options and tax-efficient withdrawal strategies. (5) Adviser Charging Facilitation – ability to deduct adviser charges directly. (6) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments – active oversight of investments. The report also states on page 11: 'Including Adviser Fee: our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans.' This contradicts the lower-cost claim when adviser fees are included.
    File:     The pension switch report [P1] shows on page 8 that Zurich-only comparison projects £102,000 existing vs £100,000 new (low growth), £110,185.60 existing vs £106,880.60 new at point of switch, resulting in £3,305 shortfall in death benefit immediately after switch [P2]. The Standard Life comparison [P4] shows critical yield of 4.8% required to match existing plan at mid-growth (2.95%), with effect of switching being £12,000 or 9.4% decrease in projected value. The comparison summary [P8] ranks outcomes by maturity value, showing Zurich existing pension ranks 1st at £128,000, Existing Solution 2nd at £127,000, and New Solution 5th at £125,000 at mid-growth. No evidence document supports that Greater Investment Choice is a new benefit (Zurich Policy Info [P9] confirms Zurich offers wide range of funds). The switch report does not evidence performance comparison between Aviva funds and Zurich Managed AP fund to support the claim that Aviva blended funds 'have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally.' The like-for-like charge difference stated as -0.24% in the report cannot be reconciled with the pension switch report figures showing overall cost comparison is unfavorable when death benefit impact is included.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs – The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure' and states 0.24% cheaper on like-for-like basis, but then states Including Adviser Fee the plan is 4.24% more expensive.
      - Report states Aviva blended funds 'have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva' (page 7) but provides no performance comparison data to evidence this claim.
      - Report claims Greater Investment Choice as a benefit, but Zurich Policy Info [P9] shows Zurich already offers 'a wide range of funds' and wide fund choices on existing plans, so this is not a new benefit.
      - Pension switch report shows Zurich existing pension projects £128,000 (ranked 1st) vs New Solution £125,000 (ranked 5th) at medium growth, contradicting the net benefit claim.
      - Report states Adviser Charging Facilitation and Ongoing Monitoring as benefits, but these are services provided by the adviser, not features of the receiving scheme itself; they do not represent product benefits that justify higher costs.
    The requirement asks whether, where recommended plans are more expensive, the claimed benefits justify the cost increase. The report makes contradictory claims about cost: it asserts lower costs (0.24% cheaper) on a like-for-like basis but then states the plan including adviser fees is 4.24% more expensive. The pension switch report [P1, P4, P8] shows that on a pure projection basis, the recommended solution underperforms: Zurich switch requires 4.8% critical yield, Standard Life shows £12,000 (9.4%) decrease, and the overall ranking puts the New Solution 5th, below the existing pensions. The claimed benefits do not withstand scrutiny: (1) Lower Costs is internally contradicted and not supported by the switch report figures. (2) Greater Investment Choice already exists on the ceding plans [P9], so it is not a new benefit. (3) Multi-asset fund selection is justified by reference to fund performance ('have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva'), but no comparative performance data is provided in the evidence pack to support this assertion. (4) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility and Adviser Charging Facilitation and Ongoing Monitoring are advisory services, not product features that provide investment benefit; they do not justify a cost increase in the pension vehicle itself. The report does not evidence that any genuine product benefit justifies the 4.24% adviser-fee-inclusive cost increase or the negative critical yields shown in the switch report. The assertion that benefits justify costs, particularly for the Zurich and Standard Life switches where projected values decrease materially, lacks evidentiary support.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts four overarching benefits of switching: (1) 'lower costs, greater investment flexibility, and continued professional oversight' (page 7); (2) 'greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, and continued professional oversight' (pages 7–8); (3) 'lower costs, greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, and continued professional oversight' (page 8); and (4) 'This would not detract from your financial retirement plan' (page 6). Specific rationales for each plan are: (People's Pension) retain as workplace scheme with employer contributions; (Scottish Widows) encash under small pot rules for £170 net; (Aviva) lifestyling drift moves portfolio outside agreed risk range; (Zurich) risk level 5 unavailable, existing blend of risk 7 and 2 lacks diversification; (Standard Life) similar statement about lifestyling drift and risk misalignment.
    File:     [P5], [P6], [P20] (Pension Switch Report) provide a technical comparison across three existing arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) but show the new solution ranked 5th of 10 alternatives by maturity value at £125,000 vs. existing solution at £127,000 (a 1.6% decrease). The report shows 'Like for Like' savings of 0.24% in charges but 'Including Adviser Fee' costs increase by 4.24%. [P8] confirms Aviva holds 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation' with lifestyling; [P12] (Zurich illustration) shows retirement date 7 June 2026 (age 70, not 75 as stated in recommendations); [P16]–[P18] (file notes) record alternatives considered: Defaqto rates all People's Pension funds as risk 9; Scottish Widows discounted for 'no servicing and reduced flexibility'; Aviva alternative discounted because only risk 4 mixed-asset fund available does not meet ATR; Zurich internal switch discounted because blend of 7 and 2 'have not performed as well' and client priority is consolidation; Standard Life discounted as funds 'more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva'. [P21] (Suitability Report) shows People's Pension as 'Current workplace scheme' with note to retain. [P1], [P2], [P4] (provider transfer literature) warn of fraud risks and market timing risk during transfer ('out of the market') but contain no analysis of switching benefits specific to this client.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'lower costs' benefit, but Pension Switch Report shows new solution ranked 5th of 10 (£125,000) versus existing solution 2nd (£127,000), a £2,000 or 1.6% decrease in maturity value.
      - Report claims 'lower costs' but page 11 (Pension Switch Charges) shows 'Including Adviser Fee' the plan is '4.24% more expensive' than existing plans; the 0.24% saving disappears with adviser charges.
      - Report asserts Zurich blend of risk 7 and 2 'lacks proper multi-asset diversification,' but file notes (P16) acknowledge these are Zurich's only options and are being used precisely to approximate risk 5; they do not claim the blend is unsuitable for the client.
      - Report states Scottish Widows will be encashed for £170 net (page 6), but fund value is £211.32; no analysis compares growth potential against encashment, and no evidence that encashment aligns with ATR or capacity for loss.
      - Aviva current fund is 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation' (lifestyling, P8), and report recommends switching within Aviva to 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' and 'CT Universal MAP Balanced' (page 12, Suitability Report); but file notes (P16) record 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR.. Discounted switch'—contradicting the recommendation to switch to Aviva Platform.
      - Report rationale claims alternatives were discounted, but People's Pension (workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions) is marked 'Retain' in the recommendation, not formally discounted; file notes show risk 9 rating, not a substantive comparison of its merits.
      - File notes (P16) state Zurich 'Wouldn't recommend investing in singular asset types' (referring to the risk 7 and 2 blend), yet report recommends consolidation on grounds of alignment, with no resolution of this tension.
      - Charge arithmetic discrepancy: page 5 (Suitability Report) states Standard Life charge as '0.52% fund charge,' but page 11 (Pension Switch Charges) uses 0.18% as the 'Current Annual Recurring Charge of Existing Plan' for Standard Life—these cannot both be the full recurring charge, undermining the cost-benefit comparison.
    The report recommends consolidation of multiple pensions into a new Aviva Platform SIPP. The primary stated benefits are lower costs, greater flexibility, and alignment with ATR (risk 5). However, the evidence reveals material contradictions:
    
    1. **Cost claim vs. maturity value outcome**: The report asserts 'lower costs' as a headline benefit. The Pension Switch Report (P20) compares the existing solution (£127,000 projected maturity at retirement) to the new solution (£125,000). This is a 1.6% decrease in projected value. The 'Like for Like' charge comparison shows 0.24% savings, but when adviser fees are included, the plan becomes 4.24% more expensive. The monetary impact stated on page 11 is £268.94 more expensive. This directly contradicts the assertion of 'lower costs' as a benefit. The report does not reconcile this tension or explain why a more expensive solution with lower projected maturity is nonetheless recommended.
    
    2. **Risk alignment for Zurich**: The file notes (P16) acknowledge that Zurich has no risk 5 funds and no multi-asset funds in the risk range, so a blend of risk 7 and 2 was used. The report then states this blend 'lacks proper multi-asset diversification'—yet these are Zurich's only options. The rationale for rejecting an internal Zurich reallocation is vague ('Wouldn't recommend investing in singular asset types'), but the report does not clearly explain why consolidation to Aviva (which also requires a blend, albeit of named multi-asset products) solves this problem better.
    
    3. **Aviva fund mismatch**: The existing Aviva holding (£3,744.36) is in 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation,' a lifestyling fund (P8). The report recommends switching to HSBC Global Strategy Balanced and CT Universal MAP Balanced (both risk 5). However, file notes (P16) record that the 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR.. Discounted switch.' This appears to be a note about why a pure Aviva reallocation was not pursued, but it creates ambiguity: if Aviva's own risk 4 fund does not meet ATR, how do the recommended risk 5 funds (HSBC and CT) meet it, and why is the recommendation to move to Aviva Platform at all? The note suggests an Aviva-only switch was considered and rejected; the recommendation consolidates three plans into Aviva Platform with different funds. The logic is not clearly traced.
    
    4. **Scottish Widows encashment rationale**: The report recommends encashing Scottish Widows (£211.32) for estimated net of £170. No analysis is provided of the alternative—retaining the fund for growth to retirement. The file notes (P17) record it was 'Discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility with withdrawing,' which refers to Money4Life plans not supporting ongoing adviser charging (P10, P14). This is an operational reason (adviser cannot service it), not a client-centric reason. No analysis weighs growth potential against encashment, and no evidence connects the decision to the client's capacity for loss or financial goals.
    
    5. **People's Pension workplace scheme**: The report states 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions' (page 6). File notes show it was discounted because 'Defaqto risk rates all of them as a 9' (P17)—far above the client's agreed ATR of 5. The recommendation is to retain this scheme. However, no formal analysis of alternatives is evident; the file notes suggest Defaqto rankings rather than a substantive review of why a risk 9 scheme is incompatible with the consolidation objective.
    
    6. **Charge schedule inconsistency**: Standard Life's recurring charge is stated as 0.52% on page 5 but used as 0.18% in the Pension Switch Charges table on page 11. This undermines the cost comparison, as the alleged savings or costs depend on the correct recurring charge. The 0.18% figure appears to be a sub-component or platform charge, not the full recurring charge, creating confusion about whether like-for-like comparison has been performed.
    
    7. **Alternatives consideration**: File notes (P16) record that Zurich, Aviva, Standard Life, and Scottish Widows were each assessed, and specific rationales for discounting were noted. However, the file notes show that the decision to consolidate and the specific fund choices (HSBC, CT Universal MAP) were driven by operational factors (adviser servicing, consolidation priority) as much as by investment merit. The Pension Switch Report ranking (P6) shows the existing Zurich solution in 1st place by maturity value (£128,000), the existing solution overall in 2nd (£127,000), and the new solution in 5th (£125,000). The recommendation to move to a lower-ranked outcome is not explained; the report does not address why a lower projected maturity is justified.
    
    The requirement asks whether replacement rationale is "supported by and consistent with evidence." The report's primary assertion of lower costs contradicts the Pension Switch Report's own comparison. The claim that consolidation solves the risk and flexibility issues is undermined by the file notes showing operational constraints (adviser servicing) driving the decision and the specific fund selections not clearly resolving the stated problems (lifestyling drift, lack of diversification, risk misalignment). Evidence of alternatives considered exists (P16), but the rationale for why consolidation to the new solution is superior to, for example, retaining Zurich (which ranks highest by maturity) or allowing the workplace scheme to continue is not articulated. The Pension Switch Report itself appears to show that consolidation results in a lower projected outcome, not a higher one.
